I had just completed my tenure at Oxford, stepping away from the rigors of academic life to assume the stewardship of my ancestral estate. My father had departed this world when I was but a child, and my mother followed shortly thereafter, leaving me to navigate the solitude of adulthood with little more than the weight of lineage upon my shoulders.

My knowledge of my forebears was scant, distilled mostly into a singular trait: a profound devotion to the study of things. This inclination had passed to me, manifesting as a restless fascination with the physical sciences. It was not merely the mechanics of the universe that captivated me, but the eerie resonances between disparate fields—the way physical hypotheses seemed to echo the metaphysical dreams I often indulged in. I possessed a dangerous tendency to elevate mere speculation into dogma, a mental quirk I deemed irrelevant to my current predicament.

The house itself was ancient, a sprawling edifice that housed a library of comparable antiquity. Its origins predated the printing press, evolving over centuries alongside shifting intellectual tides. To inherit such a repository is to inherit a moving panorama of human obsession; one becomes the custodian of a vast, silent conversation spanning generations. The library had grown voraciously, consuming room after room until it dominated the ground floor. Its central chamber was cavernous, walls lined with books reaching toward the ceiling, spilling into adjacent alcoves connected by arches, staircases, and winding passages.

I spent my days in the great room, surrounded by the dust and wisdom of ages. While modern luminaries like Darwin and Maxwell held their place, it was the ancients—Ptolemy, Dante, the Bacons, Boyle—who truly commanded my attention. They represented the fragile bridge between the known and the dark void of ignorance.

On a particularly somber August evening, rain had battered the estate all day. As twilight approached, the clouds parted, allowing the setting sun to flood the room with golden light. Rising to gaze out the window, I watched the fountain column in the lawn glow crimson. Turning back to my seat, my eyes were drawn to a solitary portrait set within a niche among the shelves. It depicted an ancestor, yet it hung apart from the gallery, isolated in its shrine. The sunlight struck the canvas with such intensity that for the first time, the image seemed to pulse in response to my gaze.

In that flash of brilliance, my vision blurred. I felt compelled to look toward the far end of the room. There, against the shadowed wall, a tall figure reached upward, fingers grasping a bookshelf. The moment my eyes adjusted to the dimming light, the figure vanished. I dismissed it as a trick of the retina, a fleeting hallucination born of fatigue.

Yet, the doubt lingered. A moment later, seeking a specific volume, I found a gap in the row where the book should have stood. The memory of the reaching hand returned instantly. I scanned the area, but the space was empty. The next morning, however, the book was there, precisely where I had expected to find it. No one else in the house had reason to seek such a tome.

Three days later, the mystery deepened.

Near the library's perimeter stood a low, narrow door leading to a closet of rarest volumes. The door itself was thick and heavy, adorned with a peculiar prank by a past owner: shallow shelves mounted across it, displaying only the spines of books. Some titles were whimsical inventions; others marked volumes lost to time. To complete the deception, a craftsman had wedged a fragment of a thin manuscript between the top shelf and the doorframe. The binding was limp vellum, the corner torn diagonally to reveal parchment beneath.

One afternoon, while reading, I glanced up and saw the "book" missing. Anger flared within me. I summoned the butler, a man who had served the family since my father was a boy. His face drained of color as he admitted ignorance. His pallor suggested he knew more than he dared say.

Later that day, returning to my reading, I caught a glimpse of a slender, elderly man in a worn, dark coat slipping through the masked door into the closet. I rushed forward, yanking the door open. The closet was empty. Disappointment and unease washed over me, and I sat down, convinced I had succumbed to madness.

But the nervousness could not be suppressed. Minutes later, I checked the door again. The mutilated volume was back in its place, firmly fixed. I pulled at it; it did not budge.

Bewildered, I called the butler again. He confessed that he had hoped the old gentleman would fade into oblivion. He recalled rumors from his early years of service regarding a spectral figure, though the household had long ceased mentioning him. My grandfather, a man of rigid empiricism, had banned all talk of ghosts, dismissing them as the maids' excuses for impropriety. Yet, a footman had once fled the house in terror.

The butler recounted a village legend of a Mr. Raven, the former librarian to Sir Upward—the very man whose portrait hung in the library. According to the tale, Raven had encouraged Sir Upward to read forbidden, evil texts until both vanished. Some believed Raven was the devil; others, like the old woman who told the story, suspected he was a dead man returning to haunt the world he left behind. The description matched the figure I had seen perfectly.

"I hope it was but a friendly call," the butler murmured, his smile trembling.

I assured him I welcomed Mr. Raven's company, provided he remained silent to the staff. When I asked if he had ever seen the book move on its own, he denied it, treating the shelf as an immovable fixture. He pulled at it once more, confirming its solidity.

As the sun dipped lower, casting long shadows across the rows of leather-bound spines, the library felt less like a collection of knowledge and more like a cage waiting to be opened. The silence was no longer peaceful; it was watchful.

Nothing more occurred for several days. It was roughly a week later that the events I must now recount finally unfolded.

For some time, I had been haunted by the thought of the manuscript fragment, repeatedly attempting to find a means to liberate it, yet failing every time. I could not discern what bound it so tightly. However, I had long harbored an intention to conduct a thorough inspection of the books stored in the closet; the peculiar atmosphere within had begun to unsettle me regarding their state. One day, this vague intention crystallized into a firm resolve. As I rose from my chair to begin the work, I caught a glimpse of something shadowy moving from the closet door toward the far end of the room.

It appeared to be a slight, stooping figure clad in a shabby dress-coat that dragged near the heels. As the garment parted slightly with each step, it revealed thin legs encased in black stockings and large feet shod in wide, slipper-like shoes.

Without hesitation, I followed. Whether I was pursuing a shadow or a substance remained uncertain, yet I never doubted the reality of my pursuit. My guide led me out of the library, across the hall, and to the base of the grand staircase. Ascending to the first floor, where the principal rooms lay, I trailed close behind. We passed these chambers and entered a wide corridor leading to a narrower stairway ascending to the second floor. He climbed it, and so did I. Upon reaching the summit, strange as it may seem, I found myself in a domain almost entirely unknown to me. Deprived of siblings to guide me through such explorations during my childhood, and taken away by my guardian before I could learn the house's secrets, I had never returned until about a month prior to reclaim my inheritance.

Through passage after passage, we arrived at the base of a winding wooden stair. Every step groaned under my weight, yet my guide made no sound. Halfway up, I lost sight of him, and by the time I reached the top, the shadowy form had vanished completely. The space was filled with shadows, yet he was not among them.

I stood in the main garret, a cavernous expanse dominated by massive beams and rafters overhead. Great voids surrounded me, punctuated only by distant doors and long vistas whose gloom was pierced by a few cobwebbed windows and small, dusky skylights. I gazed upon the wide, unexplored territory with a strange mixture of awe and delight; it was truly my own.

In the center of this vastness stood an unpainted enclosure of rough planks, its door standing ajar. Suspecting Mr. Raven might be within, I pushed it open and stepped inside.

The small chamber was bathed in light, yet it possessed the quality of light found only in deserted places—a dull, disconsolate luminescence, as if the room regretted its existence and found itself of no use. A few faint sunbeams, cutting tracks through the cloud of dust I had just stirred, fell upon a tall, narrow mirror with a dusty surface. It was old-fashioned, appearing merely as an ordinary glass pane set in an ebony frame. Perched atop the frame stood a black eagle with wings outstretched, clutching a golden chain in its beak, from which dangled a black sphere.

I had been observing the mirror rather than looking into it when suddenly I realized it reflected neither the chamber nor my own image. I have a fleeting impression that the wall itself seemed to melt away, though what followed explains any such confusion: could I have mistaken a mirror for the glass protecting a wondrous painting?

Before me lay a wild, broken landscape of heath and moor. Desolate hills of modest height, yet of a strangely alien appearance, occupied the middle distance, while the peaks of a far-off mountain range stretched along the horizon. Closest to me was a tract of flat, melancholy moorland.

Being short-sighted, I stepped forward to examine the texture of a stone in the immediate foreground. In doing so, I espied a large, ancient raven hopping toward me with solemn gravity. Its plumage was a purplish black, softened here and there with gray, and it seemed intent on finding worms. Unstartled by the presence of a living creature within a picture, I took another step forward to observe it more closely. Then, I stumbled—likely over the frame of the mirror—and found myself nose-to-beak with the bird. I was no longer in a room; I stood in the open air, upon a houseless heath.

I spun about, my gaze sweeping the void behind me. It was a landscape of indistinct grays, a blur where fog merged with field and cloud dissolved into mountainside. The only certainty was the absence of recognition; I saw nothing I knew. Desperate to correct my failing sight with the reliability of touch, I thrust my arms outward, probing the air as I shuffled this way and that. My search yielded nothing but emptiness. Instinctively, I turned to the sole living presence nearby: the raven. It stood a few paces off, regarding me with an expression that balanced deep respect with a sharp, mocking intelligence. Suddenly, the absurdity of seeking counsel from such a creature crashed over me. I turned back, drowning in bewilderment and fear. Had I stumbled into a realm where the laws of matter and mind had ceased to function? Could a man vanish from the realm of order at any moment, becoming prey to chaos? Yet, here I was. I felt the solid ground beneath my boots and heard the rustle of wind through low-lying vegetation.

"How DID I get here?" I shouted, the question seemingly tearing itself from my throat.

"You came through the door," a voice replied. It was odd, rough-hewn, yet clear.

I scanned the area behind me, then circled the space, searching for a human silhouette. Panic seized me—the terror that madness had finally arrived. Must I now distrust my senses and my own consciousness? The very instant the doubt took root, I knew the source of the voice. It was the raven. He stood looking up, his posture one of patient expectation. Though the sun did not shine, the bird cast a distinct shadow, a dark extension that seemed woven into his very feathers.

I implore my reader to assist me in making myself intelligible—if understanding is even possible between us. I exist within a state of affairs, an economy of conditions so alien to the ways of our world that my best attempt at description is merely a sketch of what I mean. I fear I have undertaken an impossibility: to articulate the ineffable. I have already written statements I would gladly erase if I possessed the vocabulary to replace them with truth. Every time I try to pin this reality to words, I risk losing the thing itself, feeling as though I am waking from a dream where the familiar object shifts through a succession of forms until its nature becomes unrecognizable.

I recalled that a bird capable of addressing a man must possess the right to a civil answer; perhaps, as a bird, an even greater claim.

His speech carried a natural croak, lending it a rough edge, yet the tone was not disagreeable. His words offered little enlightenment, but they were not rude.

"I did not come through any door," I countered.

"I saw you come through it!" the raven asserted, his voice positive yet devoid of disrespect. "I saw you with my own ancient eyes!"

"I never saw any door!" I insisted.

"Of course not!" he returned. "All the doors you have seen—and you haven't seen many—were doors *in*. Here, you encountered a door *out*! The strange thing for you will be this: the more doors you exit, the farther you get *in*!"

"Oblige me by telling me where I am."

"That is impossible. You know nothing of 'whereness.' The only way to discover your location is to begin to make yourself at home."

"How am I to begin that where everything is so strange?"

"By doing something."

"What?"

"Anything. And the sooner you begin, the better! For until you are at home, you will find it just as difficult to leave as it is to enter."

"I have, unfortunately, found it too easy to enter. Once out, I shall not try again!"

"You have stumbled in, and may, possibly, stumble out again. Whether your entry was unfortunate remains to be seen."

"Do you never go out, sir?"

"When I please, I do, but not often, nor for long. Your world is such a half-baked place; it is so childish and self-satisfied. In fact, it is not sufficiently developed for an old raven at your service!"

"Am I wrong, then, in presuming that a man is superior to a bird?"

"That is as it may be. We do not waste our intellects on generalizations; we take man or bird as we find him. Now, I think it is my turn to ask you a question!"

"You have the best of rights," I replied, "in the fact that you CAN do so!"

"Well answered!" he rejoined. "Tell me, then, who you are—if you happen to know."

"How should I help knowing? I am myself, and I must know!"

"If you know you are yourself, you know that you are not somebody else. But do you know that you are yourself? Are you sure you are not your own father—or, excuse me, your own fool? Who are you, pray?"

Instantly, I realized I could offer him no notion of my identity. Indeed, who was I? To say "I am who" would be no answer. I understood then that I did not know myself, did not know what I was, and lacked any grounds to determine that I was one entity and not another. As for the name I bore in my own world, I had forgotten it, and I did not care to recall it, for it meant nothing here, and its origin was of no consequence. I had almost forgotten that in my world, everyone possessed a name! So I held my peace, which proved to be wisdom. What could I say to a creature such as this raven, who saw through accident straight into essence?

"Look at me," he said, "and tell me who I am."

As he spoke, he turned his back. Instantly, I knew him. He was no longer a raven, but a man of middle height, stooped and very thin, wearing a long black tail-coat. He turned again, and I saw him as a raven.

"I have seen you before, sir," I said, feeling foolish rather than surprised.

"How can you say so from seeing me behind?" he rejoined. "Did you ever see yourself behind? You have never seen yourself at all! Tell me now, then, who I am."

"I humbly beg your pardon," I answered. "I believe you were once the librarian of our house, but more *who* I do not know."

"Why do you beg my pardon?"

"Because I took you for a raven," I said, seeing him before me as plainly a raven as a bird or man could look.

"You did me no wrong," he returned. "Calling me a raven, or thinking me one, you allowed me existence, which is the sum of what one can demand of his fellow-beings. Therefore, in return, I will give you a lesson: No one can say he is himself until first he knows that he *IS*, and then what *HIMSELF* is. In fact, nobody is himself, and himself is nobody. There is more in it than you can see now, but not more than you need to see. You have, I fear, gotten into this region too soon, but none the less you must get to be at home in it. For home, as you may or may not know, is the only place where you can go out and in. There are places you can go into, and places you can go out of; but the one place, if you do but find it, where you may go out and in both, is home."

He turned to walk away, and again I saw the librarian. He did not appear to have changed, only to have taken up his shadow. I know this seems nonsense, but I cannot help it.

I gazed after him until he vanished. Whether distance hid him or he disappeared among the heather, I could not tell.

Could it be that I was dead and did not know it? Was I in what we used to call the world beyond the grave? Must I wander seeking my place? How was I to find myself at home? The raven said I must do something: what could I do here? And would that make me somebody? For now, alas, I was nobody!

I took the path Mr. Raven had gone and followed him slowly. Soon, I saw a wood of tall, slender pine trees and turned toward it. The scent of the forest met me, and I hastened to bury myself within its gloom.

Plunged into the twilight shadows, I spied something with a shine standing between two tree stems. It had no color, yet it resembled the translucent trembling of hot air rising from sun-baked ground on a radiant summer noon, vibrant like the struck chords of a musical instrument. What it was grew no plainer as I approached. When I came close, I ceased to see it clearly; only the forms and colors of the trees beyond seemed strangely uncertain. I attempted to pass between the stems but received a slight shock, stumbled, and fell. When I rose, I found myself before the wooden wall of the garret chamber. I turned, and there was the mirror, upon whose top the black eagle seemed but a moment ago to have perched.

Terror seized me, and I fled. Outside the chamber, the wide garret spaces possessed an uncanny look. They seemed to have long waited for something; it had come, and now they waited again! A shudder racked me on the winding stair: the house had grown strange to me! Something was about to leap upon me from behind! I darted down the spiral, struck the wall, fell, rose, and ran. On the next floor, I lost my way, traversing several passages a second time before finding the head of the stair. At the top of the great staircase, I regained my composure somewhat and sat recovering my breath in the library.

Nothing should ever again make me ascend that last terrible stair! The garret at the top pervaded the whole house! It sat upon it, threatening to crush me out of existence! It was the brooding brain of the building, full of mysterious dwellers, one or other of whom might appear in the library where I sat at any moment! I was nowhere safe! I would let the dreadful place, in which an aerial portal stood ever open to creatures whose life was other than human! I would purchase a crag in Switzerland and build a wooden nest of one story with never a garret above it, guarded by some grand old peak that would send down nothing worse than a few tons of overwhelming rock!

I knew all the time that my thinking was foolish, and I was even aware of an undertone of contemptuous humor in it; but suddenly it was checked, and I seemed again to hear the croak of the raven.

"If I know nothing of my own garret," I thought, "what is there to secure me against my own brain? Can I tell what it is even now generating? What thought it may present me the next moment, the next month, or a year away? What is at the heart of my brain? What is behind my THINK? Am I there at all? Who, what am I?"

I could no more answer the question now than when the raven put it to me in—"Where in? Where at?" I said, giving myself up as knowing anything of myself or the universe.

I started to my feet, hurried across the room to the masked door, where the mutilated volume, sticking out from the flat of soulless, bodiless, non-existent books, appeared to beckon me. I went down on my knees and opened it as far as its position would permit, but saw nothing. Rising again, I lit a taper and peered as into a pair of reluctant jaws. I perceived that the manuscript was verse. Further discovery was impossible. Beginnings of lines were visible on the left-hand page, and ends of lines on the other; but I could not, of course, reach the beginning and end of a single line, and was unable, in what I could read, to guess at the sense. The mere words, however, woke in me feelings which to describe was, from their strangeness, impossible. Some dreams, some poems, some musical phrases, some pictures, wake feelings such as one never had before, new in color and form—spiritual sensations, as it were, hitherto unproved. Here, some of the phrases, some of the senseless half-lines, some even of the individual words affected me in similar fashion—as with the aroma of an idea, rousing in me a great longing to know what the poem or poems might, even yet in their mutilation, hold or suggest.

I copied out a few of the larger shreds attainable and tried hard to complete some of the lines, but without the least success. The only thing I gained in the effort was so much weariness that, when I went to bed, I fell asleep at once and slept soundly.

In the morning, all that horror of the empty garret spaces had left me.

The sun blazed with an intensity that felt less like warmth and more like exposure, though I suspected the day would not hold its promise. I glanced into the milky sapphire ring upon my finger, checking the star embedded within for clarity. It appeared even more nebulous than I had anticipated, a blurred point of light in a shifting sea. Rising from the breakfast table, I moved to the window, drawn back to the stone. Outside, the lawn was slick with the heavy rain of the night; a thrush struggled futilely to break the shell of a snail, its movements frantic against the damp earth.

As I twisted the ring, seeking the star's response to the solar glare, a keen black eye fixed itself upon me from the milky blue depths of the gem. The sight jolted me so violently that I dropped the ring. When I bent to retrieve it, the eye was gone. In that exact instant, the sun vanished, swallowed by a dark vapor that spread rapidly until the entire sky was choked with cloud. The air grew stifling, heavy with humidity, before a sudden gust tore through the room. A flash of lightning illuminated the gloom, followed by a single, sharp crack of thunder, and then the rain descended in torrents.

I had thrown open the window, standing there watching the precipitous deluge, when I saw a raven approaching across the grass. He moved with a solemn, deliberate gait, utterly indifferent to the falling water. Recognizing him, I felt a surge of relief that I remained on the ground floor, yet simultaneously a gnawing conviction that disaster loomed if I were not vigilant.

He drew nearer, executed a profound bow, and with a sudden, winged leap landed on the window sill. He stepped over the ledge, dropped into the room, and walked straight to the door. Assuming he intended for the library, I prepared to follow, vowing not to take a single step after him if he ascended the stairs. Instead, he turned neither toward the library nor the staircase, but toward a small door opening onto a grass patch nestled between two sections of the rambling old house. I hurried to open it for him. He stepped out onto the creeper-covered porch and stood gazing at the rain, which fell like a massive, thin cataract. I lingered in the doorway behind him. A second flash of lightning was followed by a prolonged roll of distant thunder. He turned his head over his shoulder to look at me, as if to ask, "Do you hear that?" before swiveling back to contemplate the storm with apparent approval. His posture and mannerisms were so undeniably human that I remarked almost involuntarily, "Fine weather for the worms, Mr. Raven!"

"Yes," he croaked in the familiar, gravelly voice I knew well, "the ground will be lovely for them to emerge and retreat! It must be a magnificent time on the steppes of Uranus!" he added, glancing upward. "I believe it is raining there too; it has been so all week!"

"Why would that make it a grand time?" I asked.

"Because the creatures there are all burrowers," he replied, "like the field-mice and moles here. They will thrive for ages to come."

"How do you know that, if I may be so bold?" I countered.

"As anyone would who has been there to witness it," he answered. "It is a spectacular sight, until you acclimate, when the earth heaves and a beast emerges. You might mistake it for a hairy elephant or a deinotherium, but none of the animals are what we have known here. I was nearly frightened the first time I saw the dry-bog-serpent wallow out—such a head, such a mane! And SUCH eyes! But the shower is nearly over. It will cease directly after the next thunderclap. Here it comes!"

A flash accompanied his words, followed by the thunder half a minute later. Then, the rain stopped abruptly.

"Now we must depart!" the raven said, stepping to the front of the porch.

"Depart where?" I asked.

"To where we must go," he answered. "Did you truly believe you had arrived home? I warned you that entry and exit are not pleasures to be taken at will until you are truly at home!"

"I do not wish to go," I said.

"That makes no difference—at least, not much," he replied. "This is the path!"

"I am perfectly content where I am."

"You think so, but you do not. Come along."

He hopped from the porch onto the grass and turned, waiting.

"I will not leave the house today," I declared with stubbornness.

"You will enter the garden!" he rejoined.

"I yield for the moment," I replied, stepping from the porch.

The sun pierced the clouds, and raindrops flashed and sparkled on the grass. The raven walked ahead.

"You will wet your feet!" I cried.

"And mire my beak," he answered, immediately plunging it deep into the sod and drawing out a great, wriggling red worm. He threw back his head and tossed it into the air. It spread great wings, gorgeous in red and black, and soared aloft.

"Tut! Tut!" I exclaimed. "You are mistaken, Mr. Raven: worms are not the larvae of butterflies!"

"Never mind," he croaked. "It will suffice for once! I am not a reader at present, but sexton at—a certain graveyard, cemetery, more properly—in—no matter where!"

"I see! You cannot keep your spade still: and when you have nothing to bury, you must dig something up! Only you should mind what it is before you make it fly! No creature should be allowed to forget what and where it came from!"

"Why?" said the raven.

"Because it will grow proud and cease to recognize its superiors."

No man realizes he is making an idiot of himself until it is too late.

"Where DO the worms come from?" the raven asked, suddenly curious.

"From the earth, as you have just seen!" I answered.

"Yes, last!" he replied. "But they could not have come from it first—for that would never return to it!" he added, looking up.

I looked up as well, seeing nothing but a small dark cloud whose edges were tinged red, as if with the light of a setting sun.

"The sun is not setting!" I exclaimed, struck with amazement.

"Oh, no!" returned the raven. "That red belongs to the worm."

"You see what happens when creatures forget their origins!" I cried with some heat.

"It is well, surely, if it allows them to rise higher and grow larger!" he returned. "But indeed, I only teach them to find it!"

"Would you fill the air with worms?"

"That is the business of a sexton. If only the rest of the clergy understood it as well!"

His beak plunged into the soft turf again, and out came the wriggling worm. He tossed it into the air, and away it flew.

I looked behind me and let out a cry of dismay: I had only moments ago declared I would not leave the house, and now I was a stranger in a strange land!

"What right do you have to treat me thus, Mr. Raven?" I said with deep offense. "Am I, or am I not, a free agent?"

"A man is as free as he chooses to make himself, never an atom freer," the raven answered.

"You have no right to make me do things against my will!"

"When you possess a will, you will find that no one can."

"You wrong me in the very essence of my individuality!" I persisted.

"If you were an individual, I could not; therefore, since you are not, I do not. You are merely beginning to become an individual."

Surrounding me was a pine forest, into which my eyes searched desperately for an unaccountable glimmer that might guide me home. Alas, how could I any longer call that house HOME, where every door and every window opened outward, and even the garden I could not keep inside?

I suppose I looked discomfited.

"Perhaps it may comfort you," said the raven, "to be told that you have not yet left your house, nor has your house left you. At the same time, it cannot contain you, nor do you inhabit it!"

"I do not understand you," I replied. "Where am I?"

"In the region of the seven dimensions," he answered, with a curious noise in his throat and a flutter of his tail. "You had better follow me carefully for a moment, lest you harm someone!"

"There is no one to harm but yourself, Mr. Raven! I confess I would rather like to hurt you!"

"That you see no one is where the danger lies. But you see that large tree to your left, about thirty yards away?"

"Of course I do: why should I not?" I answered testily.

"Ten minutes ago you did not see it, and now you do not know where it stands!"

"I do."

"Where do you think it stands?"

"Why THERE, where you know it is!"

"Where is THERE?"

"You bore me with your silly questions!" I cried. "I am growing tired of you!"

"That tree stands on the hearth of your kitchen and grows nearly straight up its chimney," he said.

"Now I KNOW you are mocking me!" I answered with a scornful laugh.

"Was I mocking you when you discovered me looking out of your star-sapphire yesterday?"

"That was this morning—not an hour ago!"

"I have been widening your horizon longer than that, Mr. Vane; but never mind!"

"You mean you have been making a fool of me!" I said, turning from him.

"Excuse me: no one can do that but yourself!"

"And I decline to do it."

"You mistake."

"How?"

"In declining to acknowledge yourself as one already. You make yourself such by refusing what is true, and for that you will sorely punish yourself."

"How, again?"

"By believing what is not true."

"Then, if I walk to the other side of that tree, I shall walk through the kitchen fire?"

"Certainly. You would first, however, walk through the lady at the piano in the breakfast-room. That rosebush is close by her. You would give her a terrible start!"

"There is no lady in the house!"

"Indeed! Is not your housekeeper a lady? She is counted such in a certain country where all are servants, and the liveries one and multitudinous!"

"She cannot use the piano, anyhow!"

"Her niece can: she is there—a well-educated girl and a capital musician."

"Excuse me; I cannot help it: you seem to me to be talking sheer nonsense!"

"If you could but hear the music! Those great long heads of wild hyacinth are inside the piano, among the strings, and give that peculiar sweetness to her playing!—Pardon me: I forgot your deafness!"

"Two objects," I said, "cannot exist in the same place at the same time!"

"Can they not? I did not know!—I remember now they do teach that with you. It is a great mistake—one of the greatest ever made by a wiseacre! No man of the universe, only a man of the world could have said so!"

"You, a librarian, and talk such rubbish!" I cried. "Plainly, you did not read many of the books in your charge!"

"Oh, yes! I went through all in your library—at the time—and came out the other side not much the wiser. I was a bookworm then, but when I came to know it, I woke among the butterflies. To be sure, I have given up reading for many years—ever since I was made sexton.—There! I smell Grieg's Wedding March in the quiver of those rose-petals!"

I went to the rose-bush and listened hard, hearing not the thinnest ghost of a sound, but smelling something I had never before detected in any rose. It was still the scent of roses, but altered, caused, I supposed, by the Wedding March.

When I looked up, the bird stood by my side.

"Mr. Raven," I said, "forgive me for being so rude: I was irritated. Will you kindly show me my way home? I must go, for I have an appointment with my bailiff. One must not break faith with his servants!"

"You cannot break what was broken days ago!" he answered.

"Do show me the way," I pleaded.

"I cannot," he returned. "To go back, you must go through yourself, and that way no man can show another."

Entreaty was vain. I must accept my fate! But how was life to be lived in a world of which I had all the laws to learn? There would, however, be adventure! that held consolation; and whether I found my way home or not, I should at least have the rare advantage of knowing two worlds!

I had never yet done anything to justify my existence; my former world was no better for my sojourn in it. Here, however, I must earn, or in some way find, my bread! But I reasoned that, as I was not to blame for being here, I might expect to be cared for here as well as there! I had had nothing to do with entering the world I had just left, and in it I had found myself heir to a large property! If that world, as I now saw, had a claim upon me because I had eaten and could eat again, upon this world I had a claim because I must eat—when it would in return have a claim on me!

"There is no hurry," said the raven, who stood regarding me; "we do not go much by the clock here. Still, the sooner one begins to do what has to be done, the better! I will take you to my wife."

"Thank you. Let us go!" I answered, and immediately he led the way.

The path wound deeper into the pine-forest, a corridor of shadows where the air grew thick with a sacred, suffocating gloom. We walked in silence, the only sounds the crunch of our boots and the rustle of needles overhead. The trees before us swelled in size, ancient sentinels twisted by centuries into grotesque, gnarled forms that seemed to lean inward, guarding secrets too old for mortal tongues. As we pressed on, the canopy thinned, allowing slivers of pale light to pierce the darkness until we emerged onto an open heath.

"You see that hawthorn?" my guide asked, his voice rasping like dry leaves. He extended a long, black beak, pointing toward the edge of the clearing.

I squinted against the glare. "I see a gnarled old man, with a great white head," I replied, my eyes tracing the twisted silhouette.

"Look again," he corrected gently. "It is a hawthorn."

"It certainly looks ancient," I conceded, "but this is not the season for blossoms."

"The season for the hawthorn to blossom," the raven intoned, "is simply when the hawthorn blossoms. That specific tree stands in the ruins of the church on your home-farm. Recall the morning of the thunder; were you not intending to give the bailiff directions regarding its churchyard?"

"I was planning to tell him I wished to transform it into a wilderness of rose-trees, forbidding the plough from coming within three yards of the site."

"Listen!" the raven commanded, holding his breath as if straining to catch a whisper from the void.

I strained my ears. Was it the sighing of a distant wind, or the ghost of music that had once known joy? The sound was faint, almost imperceptible, yet it vibrated through the marrow of my bones.

"They go there still," the raven murmured.

"Who goes there? And where do they go?" I asked, turning to face him.

"Some of the people who once prayed there," he replied, "still make the journey to the ruins. But I fear they will not go much longer."

"What compels them now?"

"They require assistance from one another to complete their thinking and hatch their feelings," the raven explained. "They speak and sing together, and in doing so, the great thought floats out of their hearts like a massive ship leaving a river at high tide."

"Do they pray as well as sing?"

"No," the raven shook his head. "They have discovered that each prays best in their own silent heart. Some are always at prayer. Look! There goes one!"

He pointed upward. A snow-white pigeon mounted the sky, its wings beating with frantic, accelerating speed, spiraling up an invisible staircase of light. Sunlight flashed and quivered off its feathers.

"I see a pigeon!" I exclaimed.

"Of course you see a pigeon," the raven agreed, "for there is the pigeon! But I see a prayer ascending. I wonder now what heart serves as that dove's mother. Perhaps someone has awakened in my cemetery."

"How can a pigeon be a prayer?" I argued, confused. "I understand how it could be a symbol, a likeness, but for a living bird to emerge directly from a heart?"

"It *must* puzzle you! It cannot fail to do so!"

"A prayer is a thought, a purely spiritual entity!" I insisted.

"True enough! But if you understood any world besides your own, you would understand your own much better. When a heart is truly alive, it is capable of thinking living things. There exists one heart whose thoughts are strong, happy creatures, and whose dreams are lives themselves. Some lift heavy thoughts from the ground only to drop them back down; others send their prayers up in living shapes, the nearest likeness they can forge. All living things were thoughts at the beginning, and thus are fit to be used by those who think. When one speaks to the Great Thinker—'Here is one of thy thoughts: I am thinking it now!'—that is a prayer. A word from one of its own little hearts to the big heart. Look, there is another!"

This time, the raven pointed his beak downward, toward the base of a granite block. I looked and saw a small flower. It was unlike anything I had ever witnessed, evoking a feeling of grace and trust, a scent that smelled of a new world that was simultaneously the old. It resembled an anemone, pale rose in hue, with a golden center.

"That is a prayer-flower," the raven stated.

"I have never seen such a flower before!" I rejoined.

"There is no other such. Not a single prayer-flower is ever quite like another," he returned.

"How do you know it is a prayer-flower?" I asked.

"By the expression upon it," he answered. "Beyond that, I cannot tell you. If you know it, you know it; if you do not, you do not."

"Could you not teach me to recognize a prayer-flower when I see it?"

"I could not. But if I could, what greater purpose would serve you? You would not know it of YOURSELF and ITSELF! Why learn the name of a thing when you do not know the thing itself? Whose work is it but your own to open your eyes? Indeed, the business of the universe is to make such a fool of you that you will finally know yourself for one, and so begin to be wise!"

Yet, I saw that the flower was undeniably different from any bloom I had known. Therefore, I knew I was witnessing a shadow of the prayer contained within it. A profound awe washed over me, not just at the sight, but at the thought of a heart listening to the flower.

The landscape shifted beneath our feet, transforming from the jagged, dry expanse of the moor into a realm where the air itself felt ancient and heavy. In the distance, shrouded by a pall of gray clouds that seemed to swallow the setting sun, stood a solitary cottage. The wind here carried a chill that suggested not merely cold weather, but the perpetual night of a forgotten season.

"Here we are at last!" the raven called out, his voice echoing with a weariness that belied his flight. "What a journey! In half the time, I could have flown to Paradise to visit my cousin—the one who never returned to Noah. Oh, dear! It feels almost like winter."

"Winter?" I protested, startled by the sudden shift in tone. "It seems to me only half a day has passed since we left home."

"That is because you are bound by the gravity of your world," the raven replied, his form shifting slightly as if adjusting to a different physics. "You cannot simply pull up the plumb-line of gravity and let the world spin beneath you. Here, however, is my wife's house. She is kind enough to let me reside here, calling it the sexton's cottage."

"But where is the churchyard?" I asked, scanning the flat, desolate heath. "Where are the graves you tend?"

The raven stretched his neck, holding his beak horizontally. He slowly rotated it, pointing first north, then south, east, and west, offering no verbal answer. I followed the direction of his beak with my eyes, and the revelation struck me with the force of a physical blow: there was no church, no stone markers, no distinct boundary. The entire heath was the cemetery. Wherever the dreary wind swept, it marked a burial ground. The raven was the sexton of everything he surveyed, the lord of all that had been laid aside. I stood within the burial ground of the universe itself, bounded only by the low, starless horizon. I had left behind the cycle of spring, summer, and autumn, arriving instead at a winter that slept eternally in a winding sheet of ice. Yet, as the day unfolded in this strange region, I realized the seasons were not linear here. Winter slumbered through the night; Spring awoke with a childlike smile at dawn; Summer blazed in midday glory; and Autumn crept in with the fading afternoon, dying only at the first breath of the ghostly night.

As we approached the cottage, the clouded sun plummeted down the western slope, sinking just as we reached the threshold. An instant later, a coldness seized me, tangible and suffocating, like the grasp of an icy death. I stumbled across the door, fighting to shut it against the gale that swelled on the moor. Inside, silence fell instantly.

A single candle flickered on a rough wooden table. My first sight was what I assumed to be a coffin lid propped against the wall, but it swung open—it was a door—and a woman stepped through. She was clad entirely in white, as pure as fresh snow, yet her face, though pale, radiated a warmth that snow could never possess. Her features were flawless, but it was her eyes that arrested my breath. All the vitality of her being was concentrated there, glowing with an inner light. It was as if approaching death illuminated her countenance, yet those eyes held the fierce, dark life of a nation. Within each pupil, a whole night-sky was condensed; stars flashed in the blackness, framed by an iris of eternal twilight. What such eyes truly *were*, only God could know; they seemed to pour directly from His own essence. Her face might have been a primeval perfection, but those living eyes were a continuous creation.

"Here is Mr. Vane, wife!" the raven announced.

"He is welcome," she answered. Her voice was low, rich, and gentle, resonant with treasures of immortal sound.

I stared, unable to speak.

"I knew you would be glad to see him," the raven added.

The woman remained by the door, refusing to advance further.

"Will he sleep?" she asked softly.

"I fear not," the raven replied. "He is neither weary nor burdened."

"Why, then, have you brought him?"

"I fear it may prove precipitate," he admitted.

"I do not quite understand," I said, a vague foreboding tightening my chest, though a desperate hope for escape lingered. "Surely a man must perform a day's work first!"

I looked into the woman's white face, my heart fluttering wildly. She met my gaze in silence.

"Let me return home first," I insisted, "and come back after I have found, made, invented, or discovered something!"

"He has not yet learned that the day begins with sleep!" the woman said, turning to her husband. "Tell him he must rest before he can accomplish anything."

"Men," the raven countered, "think so highly of having done things that they fall asleep upon them. They cannot even empty an egg without turning into the shell and lying down!"

His words pulled my gaze away from the woman. Suddenly, the raven vanished, replaced by the Librarian—the same slender, elderly man in a rusty black coat, broad-bodied and long-tailed. I had only seen his back before; now, for the first time, I saw his face. It was gaunt, revealing the sharp shape of bones beneath the skin, a testament to the skulls his former profession had made familiar. Yet, in truth, I had never seen a face so vivid, a look so keen and friendly. His pale blue eyes held a haze, as if they had wept much, yet they burned with intelligence.

"You knew I was not a raven!" he said, smiling.

"I knew you were Mr. Raven," I replied, "but somehow, I thought you a bird as well."

"What made you think me a bird?"

"You looked like one, and I saw you digging worms from the earth with your beak."

"And then?"

"Tossing them in the air."

"And then?"

"They grew into butterflies and flew away."

"Did you ever see a raven do that? I told you I was a sexton!"

"Does a sexton toss worms into the air and turn them into butterflies?"

"Yes."

"I never saw one do that!"

"You saw me do it!—But I remain the Librarian in your house, for I was never dismissed, nor did I ever relinquish the office. Now, I am the Librarian here as well."

"But you just told me you were the sexton here!"

"So I am. It is much the same profession. Except you are a true sexton, books are merely dead bodies to you, and a library is nothing but a catacomb!"

"You bewilder me!"

"That is perfectly fine!"

For a moment, he stood silent. The woman, motionless as a statue, stood guard by the coffin-door.

"Upon occasion," the sexton said finally, "it is more convenient to present one's bird-self to the front. Everyone, as you should know, possesses a beast-self, a bird-self, a stupid fish-self, yes, and even a creeping serpent-self—all of which require significant effort to crush. In truth, one also has a tree-self and a crystal-self, and countless others, all needing to find harmony. You can discern the nature of a man by observing which creature most frequently emerges to the front."

He turned to his wife, and I studied him more closely. He stood taller than usual, more erect. Like his wife, his face was very pale, his nose elegantly framing a beak that had retreated within it. His lips were thin and colorless, yet their curves were beautiful, quivering with a shadowy smile that blended humor, love, and pity.

"We are in need of food and drink, wife," he said. "We have traveled a long way."

"You know, husband," she answered, "we can give only to those who ask."

She turned her unchanging, radiant face toward me.

"Please give me something to eat, Mrs. Raven," I said, my voice trembling slightly. "And something—whatever you will—to quench my thirst."

"Your thirst must be greater before you can receive what will quench it," she replied. "But whatever I can give, I will gladly offer."

She moved to a cupboard in the wall, retrieved bread and wine, and placed them on the table.

We sat down to a perfect meal. As I ate, the bread and wine seemed to penetrate deeper than mere hunger and thirst could explain. Anxiety and discomfort dissolved, replaced by a profound sense of expectation.

I grew drowsy, and for the first time, I felt truly weary.

"I have earned neither food nor sleep, Mrs. Raven," I said. "Yet you have given me the former freely, and now I hope you will grant me the latter, for I sorely need it."

"Sleep is too precious a thing to be earned," the sexton interjected. "It must be given and accepted, for it is a necessity. However, it would be perilous to use this house merely as a halfway hostelry—for a night's rest only."

A wild-looking black cat leaped onto his knee. He patted it gently, as one soothes a child to sleep, yet the gesture seemed to me like patting down the sod upon a grave, lovingly, with an inward lullaby.

"Here is one of Mara's kittens!" he told his wife. "Will you give it a bit and send it out? She may need it!"

The woman took the kitten gently, offered it a scrap of bread, and exited with it, closing the door softly behind her.

"How then am I to make use of your hospitality?" I asked.

"By accepting it to the full," he answered.

"I do not understand."

"In this house, no one wakes of their own accord."

"Why?"

"Because no one anywhere ever wakes of their own accord. You cannot wake yourself any more than you can create yourself."

"Then perhaps you or Mrs. Raven would kindly call me!" I pleaded, the vague foreboding returning with renewed intensity.

"We cannot."

"How dare I then go to sleep?" I cried.

"If you wish to possess the rest of this house, you must not trouble yourself with waking. You must go to sleep heartily, altogether, and outright." My soul sank within me.

The sexton sat, gazing steadily into my face. His eyes seemed to whisper, *Will you not trust me?* I met his gaze and answered,

"I will."

"Then come," he said. "I will show you your couch."

As we rose, the woman entered. She took up the candle, turned toward the inner door, and led the way. I followed close behind her, and the sexton trailed in her wake.

The air hit me like stepping into a deep freeze, a sudden, biting chill that stole the warmth from my bones. Behind me, the heavy door slammed shut, sealing us in. The sexton muttered something to his wife, and she turned. The transformation was instantaneous and terrifying. Where there had been ordinary humanity, there was now a brilliance that seemed to burn too brightly for mortal eyes to contain. Her face flashed with a radiance reminiscent of Beatrice, glowing with a purity like a white rose in the light of redemption. It was as if eternal life itself poured from her, a continuous, electric stream. Even her hands seemed to emit a white luminescence, every pearl-shell helmet gleaming like a captured moonstone. Her beauty was so overwhelming, so alien, that I was relieved when she finally turned her gaze away.

Yet, the candlelight our guide held barely pierced the gloom. At first, I saw nothing but darkness. Then, a faint shimmer caught my eye, hovering just above the floor. Was it a bed? Could anything live, let alone sleep, in such a profound cold? And if it couldn't wake of its own accord, was it not a miracle it remained still? Beyond that, other faint glimmers danced in the periphery, uncertain and wavering.

A few steps brought us to the first form: a human shape beneath a sheet, rigid and motionless. I could not tell if it was male or female; the light seemed to recoil from the face as we passed. Soon, I realized we were walking down an aisle lined with these couches. Upon almost every one, facing the passage, lay a figure—sleeping or dead—covered in a sheet as white as fresh snow. My soul fell silent, crushed by a dread that had no name. We moved through aisle after aisle, surrounded by countless couches. I could only glimpse a few at a time, yet they seemed to stretch away in every direction, vanishing into an infinite void. Was this my chosen bed? Was I condemned to sleep among the unwaking, with no one to rouse me? Was this the sexton's library, and were these his books? Truly, this was no halfway house; it was a chamber of the dead.

"One of the cellars I am placed to watch!" the sexton whispered, his voice low, as if fearing to disturb his silent guests. "Much wine is set here to ripen! But it is dark for a stranger!" he added.

"The moon is rising; she will soon be here," his wife replied. Her clear, sweet voice sounded like ancient sorrow that had long been bid farewell.

As she spoke, the moon peered through an opening in the wall, and a thousand white gleams responded to her touch. Yet, I could not yet discern a beginning or an end to the rows of couches. They stretched away endlessly, as if inviting the scattered world to sleep upon them. Along the far, receding narrow ways, each couch stood solitary, occupied by a lone sleeper. At first, I thought their sleep was death, but I quickly realized it was something deeper, something I could not comprehend.

The moon climbed higher, shining through other apertures, but the sheer scale of the place defied my understanding. Sometimes it resembled a long cathedral nave; at others, a vast barn converted into a dwelling of tombs. She looked colder than any moon seen in the frostiest night of Earth. Where her beams struck directly, they cast a bluish, icy sheen on the white sheets and the pale faces below—or perhaps it was the faces themselves that made the moon appear so cold.

Among those I could see, all shared a brotherhood of death, yet none were alike in the histories etched upon their features. Here lay a man who had died in the prime of his strength; though this was not death, I had no other name for it. His dark beard flowed like a liberated stream from the glacier of his frozen countenance. His forehead was smooth as polished marble, and a shadow of pain lingered about his lips, but only a shadow. Beside him lay the form of a girl, passing lovely to behold. The sadness of parting had not yet been absorbed into perfect peace, but absolute submission held her placid features. There was no sign of wasting disease, no trace of killing care or grief of heart; if pain had existed, it was long charmed into sleep, never to wake. Many beautiful ones lay very still—some mere children—but I saw not a single infant.

The most beautiful of all was a lady whose white hair alone suggested her age when she first fell asleep. Upon her stately countenance rested not submission, but a noble acquiescence, an assurance firm as the foundations of the universe that all was as it should be. On some faces lingered the almost obliterated scars of strife, the marring of hopeless loss, the fading shadows of sorrows that had seemed inconsolable. The aurora of the great morning had not yet quite melted them away, but those faces were few. Every one bearing such a brand of pain seemed to plead, "Pardon me: I died only yesterday!" or "Pardon me: I died but a century ago!" That some had been dead for ages, I knew not merely by their unutterable repose, but by something for which I had neither word nor symbol.

We came at last to three empty couches. Immediately beyond them lay the form of a beautiful woman, a little past the prime of life. One of her arms lay outside the sheet, her hand open with the palm upward, centered on a dark spot. Next to her stood the stalwart figure of a middle-aged man. His arm too was exposed, the strong hand almost closed, as if clenched around the grip of a sword. I thought he must be a king who had died fighting for the truth.

"Will you hold the candle nearer, wife?" the sexton whispered, bending down to examine the woman's hand.

"It heals well," he murmured to himself. "The nail found in her nothing to hurt!"

Finally, I ventured to speak.

"Are they not dead?" I asked softly.

"I cannot answer you," he replied in a subdued tone. "I almost forget what they mean by DEAD in the old world. If I said a person was dead, my wife would understand one thing, and you would imagine another. This is but one of my treasure vaults," he continued, "and all my guests are not laid in vaults. Out there on the moor, they lie thick as the leaves of a forest after the first blast of your winter—thick, let me say rather, as if the great white rose of heaven had shed its petals over it. All night the moon reads their faces, and smiles."

"But why leave them in the corrupting moonlight?" I asked.

"Our moon," he answered, "is not like yours—the old cinder of a burnt-out world. Her beams embalm the dead, not corrupt them. You observe that here the sexton lays his dead on the earth; he buries very few under it! In your world, he lays huge stones on them, as if to keep them down. I watch for the hour to ring the resurrection-bell and wake those that are still asleep. Your sexton looks at the clock to know when to ring the dead-alive to church; I hearken for the cock on the spire to crow: 'AWAKE, THOU THAT SLEEPEST, AND ARISE FROM THE DEAD!'"

I began to conclude that the self-styled sexton was in truth an insane parson; the whole thing was too mad! But how was I to escape? I was helpless. In this world of the dead, the raven and his wife were the only living beings I had yet seen. Whither should I turn for help? I was lost in a space larger than imagination; for if here two things, or any parts of them, could occupy the same space, why not twenty or ten thousand? But I dared not think further in that direction.

"You seem in your dead to see differences beyond my perception!" I ventured to remark.

"None of those you see," he answered, "are in truth quite dead yet, and some have but just begun to come alive and die. Others had begun to die, that is, to come alive, long before they came to us; and when such are indeed dead, that instant they will wake and leave us. Almost every night some rise and go. But I will not say more, for I find my words only mislead you. This is the couch that has been waiting for you," he ended, pointing to one of the three.

"Why just this?" I said, beginning to tremble, anxious to delay by parley.

"For reasons which one day you will be glad to know," he answered.

"Why not know them now?"

"That also you will know when you wake."

"But these are all dead, and I am alive!" I objected, shuddering.

"Not much," rejoined the sexton with a smile. "Not nearly enough! Blessed be the true life that the pauses between its throbs are not death!"

"The place is too cold to let one sleep!" I said.

"Do these find it so?" he returned. "They sleep well—or will soon. Of cold they feel not a breath: it heals their wounds. Do not be a coward, Mr. Vane. Turn your back on fear, and your face to whatever may come. Give yourself up to the night, and you will rest indeed. Harm will not come to you, but a good you cannot foreknow."

The sexton and I stood by the side of the couch, his wife, holding the candle, at its foot. Her eyes were full of light, but her face had returned to a still whiteness; it was no longer radiant.

"Would they have me make of a charnel-house my bed-chamber?" I cried aloud. "I will not. I will lie abroad on the heath; it cannot be colder there!"

"I have just told you that the dead are there also," said the librarian.

"I will NOT," I cried again. And in the encompassing dark, the two figures gleamed out like specters waiting on the dead; neither answered me. Each stood still and sad, looking at the other.

"Be of good comfort; we watch the flock of the great shepherd," the sexton said to his wife.

Then he turned to me.

"Didst thou not find the air of the place pure and sweet when thou enteredst it?" he asked.

"Yes; but oh, so cold!" I answered.

"Then know," he returned, and his voice was stern, "that thou who callest thyself alive hast brought into this chamber the odours of death, and its air will not be wholesome for the sleepers until thou art gone from it!"

They walked farther into the great chamber, leaving me alone in the moonlight with the dead.

I turned to escape.

What a long way I found it back through the dead! At first, I was too angry to be afraid, but as I grew calm, the still shapes became terrible. Finally, with loud offense to the gracious silence, I ran, I fled wildly, and, bursting out, flung the door shut behind me. It closed with an awful silence.

I stood in pitch darkness. Feeling about me, I found a door, opened it, and was aware of the dim light of a lamp. I stood in my library, with the handle of the masked door in my hand.

Had I come to myself out of a vision? Or lost myself by going back to one? Which was the real—what I now saw, or what I had just ceased to see? Could both be real, interpenetrating yet unmingling?

I threw myself on a couch and fell asleep.

In the library was one small window to the east, through which, at this time of the year, the first rays of the sun shone upon a mirror whence they were reflected on the masked door. When I woke, there they shone, and thither they drew my eyes. With the feeling that behind it must lie the boundless chamber I had left by that door, I sprang to my feet and opened it. The light, like an eager hound, shot before me into the closet and pounced upon the gilded edges of a large book.

"What idiot," I cried, "has put that book in the shelf the wrong way?"

But the gilded edges, reflecting the light a second time, flung it onto a nest of drawers in a dark corner, and I saw that one of them was half open.

"More meddling!" I cried, and went to close the drawer.

It contained old papers and seemed more than full, for it would not close. Taking the topmost one out, I perceived that it was in my father's writing and of some length. The words on which first my eyes fell instantly made me eager to learn what it contained. I carried it to the library, sat down in one of the western windows, and read what follows.

The world outside was a spectacle of golden sun and azure sea, a canvas of life where green shoots reached upward and birds soared into the air I had breathed since infancy. Yet, I viewed this splendor with a detached awe, knowing it was merely a passing stage act, ready to be lifted at any moment to reveal the machinery of something far more profound.

It was shortly after my father's death that I found myself in the library, a thin, listless figure staring at a portrait hanging among the books. It was a distant ancestor, a face I knew only by reputation, and I wished desperately to learn its origin. My attention drifted to a volume on the shelf, but soon I glanced up to find a visitor approaching—not from the door, but seemingly emerging from the space between me and the portrait.

He was a thin, pale man dressed in rusty black, his features sharp and eager, dominated by a notable nose that reminded me of a specific jug my sisters once called "Mr. Crow."

"Finding myself in your vicinity, Mr. Vane, I have given myself the pleasure of calling," he said in a peculiar, yet not unpleasant, voice. "Your honored grandfather treated me—as I may say without presumption—as a friend, having known me from childhood as his father's librarian."

At the time, I failed to register just how ancient the man truly was.

"May I ask where you live now, Mr. Crow?" I asked.

He offered an amused smile. "You nearly hit my name, which shows the family insight. You have seen me before, but only once, and could not then have heard it!"

"Where was that?"

"In this very room. You were quite a child, however!"

I searched my memory, uncertain if I recognized him, though for a fleeting moment the image clicked. I begged him to clarify his identity.

"There is such a thing as remembering without recognizing the memory within it," he remarked. "As for my name—which you have guessed near enough—it used to be Raven."

The name struck a chord; marvelous tales had brought it to me before.

"It is very kind of you to come and see me," I said, gesturing to a chair. "Will you not sit down?"

He seated himself immediately. "You knew my father, then, I presume?"

"I knew him," he answered with a curious smile, "but he did not care about my acquaintance, and we never met. That gentleman, however," he added, pointing to the portrait, "old Sir Up'ard, his people called him, was in his day a friend of mine yet more intimate than ever your grandfather became."

The interview began to feel strangely surreal. It was hardly stranger that my visitor remembered Sir Up'ard than that he should have been my great-grandfather's librarian!

"I owe him much," he continued. "Although I had read many more books than he, through the special direction of his studies, he was able to inform me of a certain relation of modes which I should never have discovered of myself, and could hardly have learned from anyone else."

"Would you mind telling me all about that?" I asked.

"By no means—as much at least as I am able: there are not such things as wilful secrets," he replied, launching into his tale.

"That closet held his library—a hundred manuscripts or so, for printing was not then invented. One morning I sat there, working at a catalogue of them, when he looked in at the door and said, 'Come.' I laid down my pen and followed him—across the great hall, down a steep rough descent, and along an underground passage to a tower he had lately built, consisting of a stair and a room at the top of it. The door of this room had a tremendous lock, which he undid with the smallest key I ever saw. I had scarcely crossed the threshold after him, when, to my eyes, he began to dwindle, and grew less and less. All at once my vision seemed to come right, and I saw that he was moving swiftly away from me. In a minute more he was the merest speck in the distance, with the tops of blue mountains beyond him, clear against a sky of paler blue. I recognized the country, for I had gone there and come again many a time, although I had never known this way to it."

"Many years after, when the tower had long disappeared, I taught one of his descendants what Sir Upward had taught me; and now and then to this day I use your house when I want to go the nearest way home. I must indeed—without your leave, for which I ask your pardon—have by this time well established a right of way through it—not from front to back, but from bottom to top!"

"You would have me then understand, Mr. Raven," I said, "that you go through my house into another world, heedless of disparting space?"

"That I go through it is an incontrovertible acknowledgement of space," returned the old librarian.

"Please do not quibble, Mr. Raven," I rejoined. "Please to take my question as you know I mean it."

"There is in your house a door, one step through which carries me into a world very much another than this."

"A better?"

"Not throughout; but so much another that most of its physical, and many of its mental laws are different from those of this world. As for moral laws, they must everywhere be fundamentally the same."

"You try my power of belief!" I said.

"You take me for a madman, probably?"

"You do not look like one."

"A liar then?"

"You give me no ground to think you such."

"Only you do not believe me?"

"I will go out of that door with you if you like: I believe in you enough to risk the attempt."

"The blunder all my children make!" he murmured. "The only door out is the door in!"

I began to think he must be crazy. He sat silent for a moment, his head resting on his hand, his elbow on the table, and his eyes on the books before him.

"A book," he said louder, "is a door in, and therefore a door out. I see old Sir Up'ard," he went on, closing his eyes, "and my heart swells with love to him: what world is he in?"

"The world of your heart!" I replied; "that is, the idea of him is there."

"There is one world then at least on which your hall-door does not open?"

"I grant you so much; but the things in that world are not things to have and to hold."

"Think a little farther," he rejoined. "Did anything ever become yours, except by getting into that world? The thought is beyond you, however, at present! I tell you there are more worlds, and more doors to them, than you will think of in many years!"

He rose, left the library, crossed the hall, and went straight up to the garret, familiar evidently with every turn. I followed, studying his back. His hair hung down long and dark, straight and glossy. His coat was wide and reached to his heels. His shoes seemed too large for him.

In the garret, light came through at the edges of the great roofing slabs, showing us parts where there was no flooring, forcing us to step from joist to joist. In the middle of one of these spaces rose a partition with a door. Through it I followed Mr. Raven into a small, obscure chamber whose top contracted as it rose, going slanting through the roof.

"That is the door I spoke of," he said, pointing to an oblong mirror that stood on the floor and leaned against the wall. I stepped in front of it and saw our figures dimly reflected in its dusty face. There was something about it that made me uneasy. It looked old-fashioned and neglected, but notwithstanding its ordinary seeming, the eagle perched with outstretched wings on the top appeared threatening.

"As a mirror," said the librarian, "it has grown dingy with age; but that is no matter: its clearness depends on the light."

"Light!" I rejoined. "There is no light here!"

He did not answer me, but began to pull at a little chain on the opposite wall. I heard a creaking; the top of the chamber began to turn slowly. He ceased pulling, looked at his watch, and began to pull again.

"We arrive almost to the moment!" he said. "It is on the very stroke of noon!"

The top went creaking and revolving for a minute or so. Then he pulled two other chains, now this, now that, and returned to the first. A moment more and the chamber grew much clearer: a patch of sunlight had fallen upon a mirror on the wall opposite that against which the other leaned, and on the dust I saw the path of the reflected rays to the mirror on the ground. But from the latter none were returned; they seemed to go clean through; there was nowhere in the chamber a second patch of light!

"Where are the sunrays gone?" I cried.

"That I cannot tell," returned Mr. Raven. "Back, perhaps, to where they came from first. They now belong, I fancy, to a sense not yet developed in us."

He then talked of the relations of mind to matter, and of senses to qualities, in a way I could only partially understand, before moving on to yet stranger things which I could not at all comprehend. He spoke much about dimensions, telling me that there were many more than three, some of them concerned with powers which were indeed in us, but of which as yet we knew absolutely nothing. His words, however, I confess, took little more hold of me than the light did of the mirror, for I thought he hardly knew what he was saying.

Suddenly, I was aware that our forms had vanished from the mirror, which seemed full of a white mist. As I gazed, I saw, growing gradually visible beyond the mist, the tops of a range of mountains, which became clearer and clearer. Soon the mist vanished entirely, uncovering the face of a wide heath, on which, at some distance, was the figure of a man moving swiftly away. I turned to address my companion; he was no longer by my side. I looked again at the form in the mirror and recognized the wide coat flying, the black hair lifting in a wind that did not touch me. With a surge of terror, I rushed from the place.

I placed the manuscript down, a heavy comfort settling in my chest to know my father had glimpsed that mysterious realm and recognized Mr. Raven. Yet, the silence of his passing echoed louder than any revelation. I had never learned the cause of his death, and a terrible suspicion took root: he must have followed Mr. Raven, crossing over without return. Shame, hot and sudden, burned through my regret for fleeing. How vast the truths I had missed! What wonders lay beyond the veil of ordinary sight! Surely the Ravens were benevolent souls; a night in their sanctuary would have been harmless, perhaps even holy. They were strange, yes, but their peculiarity was faculty, whereas mine was merely beauty. I had rejected them, treating them as threats rather than guides. The more I weighed my own cowardice, the more I loathed myself. Why fear the dead? To share their sacred rest was an honor I had spurned. What harm could a sleeping king or a wounded lady inflict? I longed for the stillness of their faces, and tears streamed down my cheeks. Collapsing onto a couch, I surrendered to sleep.

I awoke abruptly, as if summoned by a voice. The house stood silent, hollow as an empty church, save for a blackbird singing on the lawn. "I must go," I told myself, "and ask forgiveness. I will do whatever they require." Rising, I climbed the stairs to the garret.

The wooden chamber awaited, unchanged since my first arrival, the mirror dimly reflecting the room. Noon approached; the sun would be higher. I needed to raise the hood and adjust the mirrors. If only I had seen Mr. Raven perform this ritual!

I pulled the chains, letting light spill onto the first mirror. Turning to the second, I saw the shapes of my previous vision—distinct yet trembling like a landscape disturbed by a gentle breeze. I touched the glass; it remained impermeable. Suspecting polarization, I shifted the mirrors, altering their angles until, by chance, the alignment clicked. Suddenly, the mountains appeared blue, steady, and clear. Stepping forward, my feet found the heather.

I knew only that the path to the cottage led through a pine forest. I waded through thickets and small fir-woods, convinced I recognized the terrain, yet no forest appeared. As the sun dipped toward the horizon and the air grew chill with the coming winter, a small black figure emerged toward me: the raven.

I hastened to meet him. "Sir, I beg your pardon for my rudeness last night," I said. "Will you take me with you now? I confess I do not deserve it."

"Ah!" he replied, looking up. After a brief pause, "My wife does not expect you tonight. She regrets that we encouraged your stay last week."

"Take me to her," I pleaded humbly, "that I may tell her my sorrow."

"It is of no use," he answered. "Your night had not come then, or you would not have left. It is not come now, and I cannot show you the way. The dead rejoiced beneath their daisies, lying among the roots of heaven's flowers, thinking of your delight when winter passes. But ere you left them, they shivered in their beds. When the spring of the universe arrives—though not for ages, and I do not care to count them—they will wake."

"Tell me one thing, Mr. Raven: is my father with you? Have you seen him since he left the world?"

"Yes; he is with us, fast asleep. That was the figure you saw with his arm on the coverlet, his hand half-closed."

"Why did you not tell me? I should have been so near him!"

"And turn your back on him!" the raven corrected.

"I would have lain down at once had I known!"

"I doubt it. Had you been ready to lie down, you would have known him. Old Sir Up'ard and your twice great-grandfather are gone long ago. Your great-grandfather has been with us for years; he will soon begin to stir. You saw him last night, though you did not know him."

"Because he is so much nearer waking than you. No one who refuses to sleep can ever wake."

"I do not understand you!"

"You turned away, and chose not to understand!" I fell silent, fearing he would depart.

"And my grandfather?" I asked.

"No; he remains in the Evil Wood, fighting the dead."

"Where is the Evil Wood? I must find him."

"You will not find it, but you will hardly miss it. It is the place where those who refuse to sleep wake at night to kill their dead and bury them."

"I cannot understand you!"

"Naturally not. Neither do I understand you; I cannot read your heart or your face. When my wife and I fail to understand our children, it is because there is not enough of them to be understood. Only God understands foolishness."

Feeling naked and worthless, I said, "Will you show me the nearest way home? I know there are many paths, for I have taken two already."

"There are indeed many ways."

"Please, tell me how to recognize the nearest."

"I cannot," the raven answered. "You and I use the same words with different meanings. We often cannot tell people what they NEED to know because they WANT to know something else, leading to misunderstanding. Home is always within the palm of your hand; telling you how to reach it is useless. But you will get there; you must get there; you have to get there. Everyone who is not at home must go home. You thought you were at home where I found you: if that had been your home, you could not have left. Nobody can leave home. And nobody ever is or will be at home without having gone there."

"Enigma treading on enigma!" I cried. "I did not come here to be asked riddles."

"No; but you came, and found the riddles waiting! Indeed, you are the only riddle. What you call riddles are truths, seeming so only because you are not true."

"Worse and worse!" I shouted.

"And you MUST answer the riddles!" he continued. "They will ask themselves until you understand yourself. The universe is a riddle trying to escape, and you are holding your door hard against it."

"Will you not, in pity, tell me what I must do?"

"How should I tell YOUR to-do, or the way to it?"

"If I am not to go home, at least direct me to my kind."

"I know of none. The beings most like you lie in that direction." He pointed his beak. I saw only the setting sun, blinding and bright.

"Well," I said bitterly, "I feel hardly treated—taken from my home, abandoned in a strange world, and denied instruction on where to go or what to do!"

"You forget," the raven said, "that when I brought you and you declined my hospitality, you reached what you call home safely. Now you have come of your own accord. Good night."

He turned and walked slowly away, his beak lowered. I stood dazed. True, I had come of my own volition, but had I not intended atonement? My heart ached, my mind void of quest or purpose, hope or desire. I gazed after him, wanting to follow, yet feeling it futile.

Suddenly, he pounced on a spot, driving his entire body weight into his bill, digging vigorously for moments. Then, with a flutter of wings, he threw back his head, and something shot from his bill, soaring high into the air. Just then, the sun set, and the air plunged into dusk. Yet, the object opened into a soft radiance, pulsing toward me like a firefly, but larger and yellower. It flew over my head. I turned and followed it.

Here I must interrupt my narrative to remark on the constant struggle to articulate the unsayable. The events recorded are, by their nature and the nature of the creatures involved, so inexpressibly alien to the economy of this world that I can present them only by translating their effects upon me into the forms and language of earthly life—not the things themselves, but the feelings they awaken. Even this translation is plagued by a continuous sense of failure. I can capture but one phase of a multitudinously complex significance, or one concentric sphere of a graduated embodiment. A single thing often seems to be and mean many things, with an uncertain identity at its heart that constantly alters its appearance. I am often driven to record what I know is but a clumsy, doubtful representation of the mere feeling aimed at, for no medium in this world is fit to convey its peculiar strangeness with even an approach to clarity. Even to one who knew the region better than myself, I offer no assurance of transmitting the reality of my experience. While without a doubt I was actually regarding a scene of activity, I might simultaneously be aware, in my consciousness, that I was perusing a metaphysical argument.
