The air turned a suffocating black as winter pressed its icy fingers shut around me, yet the fluttering fire burned brighter, more arresting, halting its flight to hover in patient wait. As soon as I stepped beneath its radiance, it drifted forward slowly, pausing occasionally above the jagged rocks. Every time I lifted my gaze, it seemed to swell, until finally, it cast a companion shadow beside me. It was plainly a bird-butterfly, gliding with a swallow-like double beat, its vast, nearly square wings flashing through the entire spectrum of the rainbow. Captivated by their splendor, I became so transfixed by their beauty that I tripped over a low stone and lay stunned. When consciousness returned, the creature hovered directly above my head, radiating a chord of light filled with multitudinous gradations and hues I had never witnessed before. I rose to continue, but unable to tear my eyes from the shining entity to watch my footing, I struck my foot against another stone. Fearful of another fall, I sat down to watch the little glory, and a desperate longing surged within me to possess it. To my unspeakable delight, it began to descend toward me. First slowly, then swiftly, it sank, growing larger as it approached. I felt as though the treasure of the universe was surrendering itself to me—I reached out and grasped it. But the instant my fingers closed around it, its light vanished; all was darkness as pitch. A dead book with boards outspread lay cold and heavy in my hand. I tossed it into the air, hearing it crash among the heather. Burying my face in my hands, I sat in motionless misery.

Yet the cold grew so biting that, fearing to freeze, I forced myself up. The moment I stood, a faint sense of light awakened within me. "Is it coming to life?" I cried, a great pang of hope shooting through me. Alas, no! It was merely the edge of a moon peering up keen and sharp over the level horizon. She brought me light, but no guidance! She would not hover over me, would not wait for my faltering steps! She could offer me nothing but an ignorant choice.

With a full face she rose, and I began to discern my surroundings. Westward of her, and not far away, a range of low hills broke the horizon line; I set out for them.

What a night I endured before reaching them! The moon seemed to know something, for she stared at me oddly. Her gaze was indeed icy-cold, yet full of interest, or at least curiosity. She was not the same moon I had known on Earth; her face was strange to me, and her light even stranger. Perhaps it emanated from an unknown sun! Every time I looked up, I found her staring at me with all her might. At first, I was annoyed, as by the rudeness of a fellow creature; but soon I perceived—or fancied I did—a certain wondering pity in her gaze: *Why was I out in her night?* Then, for the first time, I understood the awful nature of being awake in the universe: I WAS, and I could not help it.

As I walked, my feet left the heather and trod upon bare, spongy soil, something akin to dry, powdery peat. To my dismay, it gave a momentary heave under me; then, presently, I saw what appeared to be the ripple of an earthquake running ahead of me, shadowy in the low moonlight. It passed into the distance, but while I stared after it, a single wave rose and crept slowly toward me. A yard or two away, it burst, and from it, with a scramble and a bound, emerged an animal like a tiger. Clots of mold hung about his mouth and ears, and his eyes winked and flamed as he rushed at me, baring white teeth in a soundless snarl. I stood fascinated, unconscious of either courage or fear. He turned his head to the ground and plunged into it.

"That moon is affecting my brain," I muttered as I resumed my journey. "What life can exist here but the phantasmic—the stuff of which dreams are made? I am indeed walking in a vain show!"

Thus I strove to keep my heart above the waters of fear, unaware that the very entity I distrusted was my defense against the realities I mistook for phantoms: her light controlled the monsters; without it, I would have scarce taken a second step on the hideous ground. "I will not be appalled by that which only seems!" I told myself, yet felt it a terrible thing to walk on a sea where such fishes disported themselves below. With that, a step or two from me, the head of a worm began to emerge slowly from the earth, as big as a polar bear's and much resembling it, with a white mane on its red neck. The wriggling draws with which its huge length extricated itself were horrible, yet I dared not turn my eyes away. The moment its tail was free, it lay as if exhausted, wallowing in feeble effort to burrow again.

"Does it live on the dead," I wondered, "and is it unable to hurt the living? If they scent their prey and come out, why do they leave me unharmed?"

I now know it was the moon that paralyzed them.

All night long as I walked, hideous creatures, no two alike, threatened me. In some, beauty of color enhanced the loathsomeness of their shape: one large serpent was covered from head to distant tail with feathers of glorious hues.

I became so accustomed to their hurtless menaces that I fell to beguiling the way with inventions of monstrosities, never suspecting that I owed each moment of my life to the staring moon. Though hers was no primal radiance, it so hampered the evil things that I walked in safety. For light is still light, even if but the last of a countless series of reflections! How swiftly would not my feet have carried me over the restless soil had I known that, if still within their range when her lamp ceased to shine on the cursed spot, I should that moment be at the mercy of such as had no mercy—the center of a writhing heap of hideousness, every individual of it as terrible as before it had merely seemed! Fool of ignorance, I watched the descent of the weary, solemn, anxious moon down the widening vault above me, with no worse uneasiness than the dread of losing my way—where, as yet, I had indeed no way to lose.

I was drawing near the hills I had made my goal, and she was now not far from their skyline, when the soundless wallowing ceased, and the burrow lay motionless and bare. Then I saw, slowly walking over the light soil, the form of a woman. A white mist floated about her, now assuming, now losing, then reassuming the shape of a garment as it gathered to her or was blown from her by a wind that dogged her steps.

She was beautiful, yet with such a pride mixed with misery on her countenance that I could hardly believe what I saw. Up and down she walked, vainly endeavoring to lay hold of the mist and wrap it around her. The eyes in the beautiful face were dead, and on her left side was a dark spot against which she would now and then press her hand, as if to stifle pain or sickness. Her hair hung nearly to her feet, and sometimes the wind would so mix it with the mist that I could not distinguish one from the other; but when it fell gathering together again, it shone a pale gold in the moonlight.

Suddenly pressing both hands to her heart, she fell to the ground, and the mist rose from her and melted into the air. I ran to her. But she began to writhe in such torture that I stood aghast. A moment more, and her legs, hurrying from her body, sped away as serpents. From her shoulders fled her arms, also as serpents in terror. Then something flew up from her like a bat, and when I looked again, she was gone. The ground rose like the sea in a storm; terror seized me; I turned to the hills and ran.

I was already on the slope of their base when the moon sank behind one of their summits, leaving me in its shadow. Behind me rose a waste and sickening cry, as of frustrated desire—the only sound I had heard since the fall of the dead butterfly; it made my heart shake like a flag in the wind. I turned, saw many dark objects bounding after me, and made for the crest of a ridge where the moon still shone. She seemed to linger there that I might see to defend myself. Soon I came in sight of her and climbed the faster.

Crossing the shadow of a rock, I heard the creatures panting at my heels. But just as the foremost threw himself upon me with a snarl of greedy hate, we rushed into the moon together. She flashed out an angry light, and he fell from me a bodiless blotch. Strength came to me, and I turned on the rest. But one by one, as they darted into the light, they dropped with a howl; and I saw—or fancied I saw—a strange smile on the round face above me.

I climbed to the top of the ridge: far away shone the moon, sinking to a low horizon. The air was pure and strong. I descended a little way, found it warmer, and sat down to wait for the dawn.

The moon went below, and the world again was dark.

The sun crested the horizon, painting the sky in hues of bruised purple and burning gold. I climbed to the ridge once more, peering back into the hollow I had traversed under the silver moon. It was empty now, a void scrubbed clean of all life. A cold dread settled in my stomach: did the vast, shimmering plain ahead teem with ravenous things, invisible to the naked eye?

I turned my gaze forward, scanning the landscape that lay between me and the distant, darker patch I had identified as a forest. It stretched out like a desolate desert, barren and vast. There was no smoke, no dust clouds, no sign of cultivation—only the endless, clear blue of a cloudless sky. The air was so perfectly still it felt heavy, pressing against my ears.

I descended from the ridge and pushed onward toward that imagined green sanctuary. If there was any sign of life, it had to be within those trees; this arid wasteland could not possibly harbor anything.

By the time I reached the edge of the plain, my vision confirmed what I had feared: a wide, ancient riverbed, now a graveyard of stone. The ground was a chaotic mosaic of flat, channeled rock and jagged, pinnacled spires, carved by the relentless flow of waters long gone. Innumerable dry runs scored the earth, devoid of moisture. Here and there, a patch of dry moss clung to a channel, or a lichen, hard as iron, stained a grey rock. The silence was absolute, a suffocating quiet that felt less like peace and more like the breath held before a scream.

It took the entire day to reach the forest, yet not a single trickle of water had I crossed. Even as the sun hammered down, baking the stones to white heat, I was haunted by an auditory hallucination. I could hear it so clearly—the roar of a thousand waters, the rush of a mighty river—that I struggled to reconcile the sound with the parched reality before my eyes.

As the sun began its descent, I left the silent riverbed and stepped into the woods. The canopy blocked the direct rays, casting the forest floor in a tapestry of blessed, dappled shadows. I had expected a familiar pine grove, but this was a chaotic garden of strange flora. Some trees bore a haunting resemblance to Earth's giants, while others defied description entirely. I collapsed beneath the sprawling boughs of a tree that looked like a blooming eucalyptus, though its flowers were grotesque. Each bloom possessed a hard, skull-like calyx, the top lifting like a lid to reveal a frothing, brain-like interior. From the shelter of its falchion-shaped leaves, I gazed deeper into the gloom.

Soon, the forest began to close in. The aisles narrowed, the glades vanished, and the night descended with terrifying speed. The cold was instant and biting. How do I convey the wild, spectral chill of this place to you?

The great tree I leaned against rose high before branching, yet its limbs drooped low, threatening to cage me in as I rested against its smooth trunk. My eyes, drifting in a listless stare, watched the foliage begin to shift. Under the fading twilight, the clumps of leaves started to suggest shapes other than their own. A light wind stirred, rocking a neighboring tree. Its branches swayed, every twig and leaf syncing in a rhythmic dance. Among the shifting greens, I saw a pack of wolves, straining desperately against an invisible wizard's leash. They were not merely mimicking movement; they were fighting with a savage intensity that greyhounds could not match.

My interest sharpened as the wind grew stronger. Another mass of foliage, denser and more compact, resolved into a group of horses' heads and forequarters, caparisoned in their stalls. Their necks bobbed up and down with impatience, a rhythm broken by the wilder, side-to-side sway of the wind. What strange, gaunt heads they were! Several were bare skulls; one had skin pulled tight over its bones, another had lost its lower jaw, hanging low in unutterable weariness, yet occasionally jerking upward as if trying to bite the air. Above them, floating on the very tip of a branch, stood the form of a woman, waving her arms in an imperious, commanding gesture.

The clarity of these visions first startled me, then unsettled me. What if my mind could not withstand the illusion of reality? But the twilight dissolved into darkness, the wind died away, and the shapes were swallowed by the night. I fell asleep.

I awoke to a far-off, confused rushing, mingled with faint, human cries. The sound grew, swelling into a tumult of gathering multitudes that seemed to fill the entire wood. I froze, not daring to move a muscle lest I betray my presence to hostile entities.

Slowly, the moon crested the tree line and spilled her pale light into the forest. With her first gleam, the noises erupted into a deafening uproar, and dim shapes materialized around me. As the moon climbed higher, the chaos sharpened into a furious battle raging inches from my hiding spot. Wild cries, roars of rage, the shock of collision, and prolonged struggles surged in my ears, interwoven with articulate words. Curses, creeds, snarls, sneers, laughter, mockery, sacred names, and howls of hate collided in a chaotic maelstrom.

Skeletons and phantoms fought in a mad confusion. Swords swept through the ghosts, causing them only to shiver; maces crashed upon the skeletons, shattering them hideously, yet not one ceased to fight as long as a single joint held two bones together. Bones of men and horses lay scattered and heaped, ground underfoot by the combatants. Everywhere, the bone-gaunt white steeds charged; everywhere, on foot or riding misty battle-horses, the indestructible spectres raged and ravened. Weapons and hoofs clashed and crushed, while skeleton jaws and phantom throats swelled the deafening tumult with the war-cry of every opinion, good or bad, that had ever bred strife, injustice, or cruelty. The holiest words accompanied the most hateful blows. Distorted truths flew hurtling through the wind of javelins and bones.

Moments passed where a warrior would turn against his comrades, fighting more wildly than before, shouting, "THE TRUTH! THE TRUTH!" One figure I noted wheeled in a perpetual circle, striking on all sides. Occasionally, a weary pair would sit side by side for a minute before rising to renew the fierce combat. No one stooped to comfort the fallen; no one stepped wide to spare him.

The moon shone until the sun rose, and all night long I watched a woman move at her will above the strife-tormented multitude. She appeared on this front, then that, one arm outstretched urging the fight, the other pressed against her side. "Ye are men: slay one another!" she shouted. I saw her dead, glassy eyes and the dark spot upon her brow, and my memory returned to the vision of the previous night.

This was the battle of the dead, witnessed as I lay hidden beneath the tree.

Just before sunrise, a breeze swept through the forest, carrying a voice that cried out, "Let the dead bury their dead!" At the word, the contending thousands dropped noiselessly. When the sun finally looked in, it saw no bone, but only here and there a withered branch.

I rose and resumed my journey through a wood as quiet as any that grows from the quiet earth. The morning wind had ceased, and the trees were silent. No bird sang, no squirrel, mouse, or weasel showed itself, and not a single moth flew athwart my path. Yet, I kept a vigilant watch over myself, refusing to let my eyes rest on any forest shape. All the time, faint sounds of mattocks and spades and hurling bones echoed in my mind. Any moment, my eyes might open to things I would not see. Prudence whispered that perhaps ten thousand phantoms waited only for my consenting fancy to manifest them.

In the middle of the afternoon, I emerged from the wood to find myself before a second net of dry water-courses. I initially thought I had wandered from my intended line and reversed direction, but soon realized I had not. I concluded I had simply reached another branch of the same ancient riverbed. I began to cross it immediately, finding myself in the bottom of a wide channel just as the sun set.

I sat down to await the moon, and as sleepiness overtook me, I stretched out on the moss. The moment my head touched the ground, I heard the sounds of rushing streams—all sorts of sweet, watery noises. The veiled melody of molten music sang me into a dreamless sleep. When I awoke, the sun was already up, illuminating the wrinkled country in a wide, stark visibility. Covered in shadows, the land lay striped and mottled like the skin of a wild beast. As the sun rose higher, the shadows diminished, as if the rocks were re-absorbing the darkness that had oozed from them during the night.

Until now, I had loved my Arab mare and my books more than any living man or woman. Now, at last, my soul was athirst for human presence. I longed even for those inhabitants of this alien world whom the raven had so vaguely described as nearest to my own kind. With a heavy yet hopeful heart, and my mind haunted by a doubt as to whether I was traveling in any direction at all, I kept wearily moving "north-west and by south."

I knelt beside a channel, my eyes fixed on a diminutive shrub that marked the outlying picket of an unseen army. It bore a small, unfamiliar fruit; instinct warned me against gathering it, yet I felt the weight of hundreds of eyes watching from behind the rocks, hungry for the answer to whether I would yield. Moving onward, I encountered plants of increasing stature until I realized they were not shrubs but dwarf-trees. By the time I reached the second branch of the river-bed, the channels were choked with them, forcing me to leap across gaps where others might have waded. In one particularly dense thicket, I heard a rush like a flock of birds taking flight from an ivied wall, though no wings stirred the air.

Further along, large fruit-bearing trees stood sentinel on the edge of a hollow, the dried basin of an ancient lake. While the forest above teemed with diverse species, the hollow was dominated almost exclusively by these fruit-laden giants. Exhausted, I descended a slope of grass and moss to lie down. Just beyond, a tiny tree offered rosiest apples no larger than cherries, within easy reach. I plucked one and ate it. The flavor was exquisite. As I reached for a second, a sudden eruption of clear, sweet laughter, like the music of a brook, startled me with delight.

"He likes our apples! He likes our apples! He's a good giant! He's a good giant!" cried a chorus of small voices.

"He's a giant!" one objected.

"He IS rather big," another agreed, "but littleness isn't everything! It won't keep you from growing big and stupid unless you take care!"

I rose on my elbow to stare. Surrounding me was a multitude of children of all ages, from toddlers to teenagers, with a few elders standing apart in a quieter knot. They chattered in groups, debating and contradicting with the fervor of adults, yet with greater merriment and better manners. I understood nearly everything they said, a fact that did not surprise me; to understand is not more wonderful than to love. Their logic was sound: having seen my hand reach for a second apple after eating the first, they deduced my approval.

A movement broke the chatter. A sweet-faced boy, looking innocently roguish, stepped forward holding a huge green apple. Silence fell over the throng.

"Eat, good giant," he commanded softly.

I sat up, accepted the offering with a smile, and prepared to bite. But the moment my teeth touched the skin, I flung the apple far away.

Delight erupted anew. The children swarmed me, nearly smothering me in their embrace, kissing my face and hands, clinging to my legs and shoulders. Overwhelmed by the affection of these lovely little goblins, I collapsed to the ground.

"Good, good giant!" they cried. "We knew you would come! Oh, you dear, good, strong giant!"

Their babble resumed, rising in jubilant shouts from hundreds of clear throats. Then, abruptly, silence returned. Those nearby drew back; those atop me dismounted, their faces shifting from joy to concern.

"Get up, good giant!" a little girl urged. "Make haste! Much haste! He saw you throw his apple away!"

Before she finished, I was on my feet. She pointed up the slope. There, standing on the brow, was a clownish, ill-featured figure a few inches taller than myself. He looked hostile, yet I felt no immediate fear; he held no weapon, and my little friends had vanished into the crowd.

He began to descend. Hoping to gain better footing, I moved upward. He growled like a beast as he turned toward me. At a level spot, I halted and waited. As he approached, he extended a hand. I reached for it in friendship, but he snatched it back, threatened a blow, and offered it again. I understood his demand: he wanted the apple I had discarded. I made a grimace of distaste and gestured rejection.

He responded with a howl of rage that seemed to ask, "Do you dare tell me my apple was not fit to eat?"

"One bad apple may grow on the best tree," I replied.

Whether he grasped my meaning, I could not tell. He stepped closer, and I braced myself. His attack was delayed until a second giant, similar in appearance, emerged from behind me. As the first lunged, I struck him across the face, but the second caught me on the back of the head. Together, they overpowered me.

They dragged me into the woods above the valley, where their tribe dwelled in wretched huts constructed of fallen branches and scattered stones. They shoved me into one, throwing me onto the floor and kicking me. A woman watched with indifference. I noted that during my captivity, I struggled to distinguish the sexes; they differed so little in appearance and manner. I often wondered if I had stumbled upon a fungoid people, possessing just enough cognition to express anger and greed. Their diet of tubers, bulbs, and fruits was inexpressibly repulsive to me, yet nothing offended them more than seeing my dislike. I was cuffed by the women and kicked by the men whenever I refused to swallow their food.

That night, lying on the floor barely able to move, I slept deeply and woke somewhat refreshed. In the morning, they dragged me to the valley, tied my feet with a long rope to a tree, and placed a flat stone with a saw-like edge in my left hand. When I shifted it to my right, they kicked me and forced it back into my left. They made it clear: I was to scrape the bark off every branch that lacked fruit, or suffer further punishment. They left me there.

I set about the dreary labor, hoping that by satisfying their demands, I might be granted solitude to observe and plan my escape. Fortunately, a dwarf-tree grew close by. Every few minutes, I plucked and ate a small fruit, which wonderfully refreshed and strengthened me.

I had scarcely begun my labor when the air grew thick with the sound of tiny voices. From the dense undergrowth of miniature trees, which carpeted the forest floor like living moss, emerged the Little Ones. Within moments, a swarm of them surrounded me. I signaled that the giants had only just departed and remained nearby, but they laughed—a sound like a chorus of silver bells—and declared the wind pure and clean.

"They are too blind to see us," one chirped.

"Do you like the rope binding your ankles?" another asked, her voice bright with curiosity.

"I wear it to convince them I cannot remove it," I lied, hoping to maintain the illusion of captivity.

"They can barely see their own toes!" she giggled. "Just take short steps, and they will believe the rope is secure."

As she spoke, she danced with boundless joy. A larger girl knelt to inspect the clumsy knot at my feet. I smiled, expecting her delicate fingers to fail, but in an instant, the bond was loose. They seated me upon the earth and fed me with fruits of exquisite sweetness. Then, the smallest among them began to play with me in a frenzy of chaotic delight, rendering my work impossible. As one grew weary, another took her place, and this cycle continued until the sun dipped below the horizon, casting long shadows over the clearing. Heavy footsteps approached, signaling the giants' return. The children scattered instantly. I quickly re-tied the rope around my ankles.

"We must be careful," the girl who had freed me whispered urgently. "A single trampling of one of their clumsy feet could crush a tiny life!"

"Can they not see you at all?" I asked.

"They might spot movement," she replied, her tone grim. "And if a pile of them landed on you, it would be terrible; they hate every living thing except themselves. Not that they are very alive either!"

She whistled a sharp, bird-like note. Instantly, the forest fell silent; not a child was seen or heard, and she vanished into the foliage.

My master arrived to reclaim me. He untied my ankles, dragged me to the threshold of his hut, threw me onto the dirt, re-bound my feet, delivered a kick, and departed.

At that moment, escape was within my grasp. Yet, having found friends, I hesitated. They were so enchanting, so full of whimsical grace, that I felt compelled to learn more of them. "Tomorrow," I promised myself with a surge of hope, "I shall see them again!" But from the silence of the huts until sleep claimed me, I heard their whispers drifting through the night, knowing I was watched with affectionate intensity. After that, they rarely allowed me solitude.

I never truly understood the giants. They possessed no malice, only a profound stupidity. Often, I avoided their blows by seizing their feet and toppling them, a trick that usually ended their aggression for the day.

In contrast, the Little Ones were a constant source of wonder. Their actions delighted and surprised me daily. My loathing for my labor faded as they brought amusement and solace to my monotonous existence. Soon, my love for them became overwhelming. Though I knew little of the world, they seemed wise beyond their size, capable of learning anything. I had no bed but the bare ground, yet I frequently awoke nestled in a cradle of children—some in my arms, others curled tightly around me, the smallest drawn nearest to my warmth. I rarely suffered the chill of the night. Before dawn and after dusk, they always brought me sustenance.

One morning, I awoke to find myself alone. Moments later, subdued sounds approached. Lona, the tallest and most solemn of the group, regarded by all as their mother, emerged from the woods. She was followed by the multitude, moving in joyful silence to avoid waking the giant at my door. In her arms, she carried a boy-baby. Previously, the youngest had been a girl, roughly a year old. Three older girls served as her nurses, though they shared the infant's care with the entire community. Among the Little Ones, dolls were unknown; the older tended the younger, who in turn cared for the tiniest.

Lona approached and placed the infant in my arms. The baby opened his eyes, gazed at me, closed them, and drifted back to sleep.

"He loves you already," Lona said softly.

"Where did you find him?" I asked.

"In the wood, of course," she answered, her eyes shining. "That is where we always find them. Isn't he beautiful? We searched all night. Sometimes it is difficult!"

"How do you know when one is there?"

"I cannot explain," she replied. "Everyone rushes to tell the others, yet we never discover who spoke first. Sometimes I think someone whispered it in sleep, and another heard it half-awake. When a baby appears in the wood, no one stops to ask questions. By the time we find it, it is too late."

"Do more boys or girls arrive?"

I noticed that asking the same precise question twice caused them to furrow their brows.

"I do not know," she admitted.

"You can count them, surely!"

"We never do that. We would not like to be counted."

"Why?"

"It would not feel smooth. We prefer not to know."

"Where do the babies originate?"

"From the wood—always. There is no other place."

She knew their origin perfectly and considered it the only truth required.

"How often do you find one?"

"Such a happy event consumes all our joy, and we forget the previous one. You too are glad to have him, are you not, good giant?"

"Yes, indeed!" I replied. "But how do you feed him?"

"I will show you," she said, departing briefly to return with two or three ripe plums. She offered one to the baby's lips.

"He would open his mouth if he were awake," she noted, lifting him. She squeezed a drop of juice onto the fruit's surface and held it to his lips. Without stirring, the infant began to suckle. She continued slowly until only skin and stone remained.

"There!" she cried, her voice triumphant. "A world of big apples with nothing for the babies! We would not stay in it—would we, darling? We would leave it to the bad giants!"

"But what if you let the stone fall into his mouth?" I asked.

"No mother would do that," she replied firmly. "I would not be fit to hold a baby!"

I marveled at the woman she would become. But what happened when they grew up? Where did they go? This led me back to the mystery of their arrival.

"Will you tell me where you lived before?" I asked.

"Here," she answered.

"Have you NEVER lived elsewhere?" I ventured cautiously.

"Never. We all came from the wood. Some believe we dropped from the trees."

"How is it there are so many of you, so small?"

"I do not understand. Some are smaller, some larger. I am very big."

"The baby will grow bigger, won't he?"

"Of course!"

"And will you grow bigger?"

"I do not think so. I hope not. I am the biggest. It frightens me sometimes."

"Why should it frighten you?"

She offered no answer.

"How old are you?" I persisted.

"I do not know what you mean. We are all just that."

"How big will the baby grow?"

"I cannot tell," she said, her voice trembling slightly. "Some," she added, "begin to grow after we think they have stopped. That is a frightful thing. We do not speak of it!"

"What makes it frightful?"

She paused, then answered, "We fear they may be beginning to grow giants."

"Why should you fear that?"

"Because it is so terrible. I do not want to talk about it!"

She pressed the baby to her chest with such anxiety that I dared not press further.

Soon, I noticed traces of greed and selfishness in a few of the smaller children. The older girls cast worried glances at them frequently. None of them assisted with my work; they would do nothing for the giants! Yet, they never ceased their tender care for me. They sang to me for hours, climbed trees to pop fruit into my mouth, and maintained a constant vigil against the giants' approach.

Occasionally, they gathered to tell stories—mostly childish and often seemingly meaningless. On one occasion, a moody little fellow sang a strange, crooning song with a refrain so poignant that, though unintelligible to me, it brought tears to my eyes. This reaction puzzled the onlookers. Then, I realized I had never seen water fall, lie, or run in that world. There had been plenty in some ancient age, but the Little Ones had never witnessed it before seeing my tears. Nevertheless, they seemed to possess a dim, instinctive understanding of their origins. A very small child approached the singer, shook his clenched fist in his face, and muttered something like, "Squeeze the juice out of the good giant's seeberries! Bad giant!"

One day, as Lona sat with the baby at the foot of my tree, I asked, "Why do I never see children among the giants?"

She stared, searching for meaning in my words, then replied, "They are giants; there are no little ones among them."

"Have they never any children?" I asked.

"No; there are never any in the wood for them. They do not love them. If they saw ours, they would stamp them."

"Is the number of giants always the same? I thought, before I knew better, that they were your fathers and mothers."

She burst into the merriest laughter, then said, "No, good giant; WE are THEIR firsters."

As the words left her lips, the joy vanished from her face, replaced by fear.

I stopped working and gazed at her, bewildered.

"How CAN that be?" I exclaimed.

"I do not say; I do not understand," she answered. "But we were here and they were not. They go from us. I am sorry, but we cannot help it. THEY could have helped it."

"How long have you been here?" I asked, increasingly confused, hoping for a clue.

"Always, I think," she replied. "I think somebody made us always."

I returned to my scraping.

She saw my confusion.

"The giants were not made always," she resumed. "If a Little One does not care, he becomes greedy, then lazy, then big, then stupid, then bad. The dull creatures do not know they come from us. Very few believe we exist anywhere. They say NONSENSE! Look at little Blunty: he is eating one of their apples! He will be the next! Oh, oh! He will soon be big and bad and ugly, and not even know it!"

The child stood apart, eating an apple nearly as large as his head. I had often thought he looked less healthy than the others; now, he looked repulsive.

"I will take the horrid thing from him!" I cried.

"It is no use," she answered sadly. "We have done all we can, and it is too late! We feared he was growing because he would not believe us. But when he refused to share his berries and claimed he had gathered them for himself, we knew! He is a glutton, and there is no hope. It makes me sick to see him eat!"

"Could not some of the boys watch him and prevent him from touching those poisonous things?"

"He may have them if he wants: it is all the same—to eat the apples, or to be a boy who eats them if he can. No, he must go to the giants! He belongs to them. You can see how much bigger he is than when you first arrived! He is bigger since yesterday."

"He looks as much like that hideous green lump in his hand as a boy could!"

"It suits what he is becoming."

"His head and it might swap places!"

"Perhaps they do!"

"Does he want to be a giant?"

"He hates the giants, but he is making himself one anyway; he likes their apples! Oh baby, baby, he was just such a darling as you when we found him!"

"He will be very miserable when he realizes he is a giant!"

"Oh, no; he will like it well enough! That is the worst part."

"Will he hate the Little Ones?"

"He will be like the rest; he will not remember us—most likely, he will not believe we exist. He will not care; he will eat his apples."

"Tell me how it happens. I understand your world so little! I come from a world where everything is different."

"I do not know about WORLD. What is it? What more but a word in your beautiful big mouth? That makes it something!"

"Never mind the word; tell me what happens next to Blunty."

"He will wake one morning and find himself a giant—not like you, good giant, but like any other bad giant. You will hardly recognize him, but I will tell you which. He will think he has always been a giant and will not know you, or any of us. The giants have lost themselves, Peony says, and that is why they never smile. I wonder if they are glad because they are bad, or bad because they are not glad. But they cannot be glad when they have no babies! I wonder what BAD means, good giant!"

"I wish I knew no more about it than you!" I returned. "But I try to be good, and intend to keep trying."

"So do I—and that is how I know you are good."

A long silence followed.

"Then you do not know where the babies come from into the wood?" I asked, making one final attempt.

"There is nothing to know there," she answered. "They are in the wood; they grow there."

"Then how is it you never find one before it is fully grown?" I asked.

She furrowed her brow and fell silent for a moment.

"They're not there till they're finished," she said.

"It is a pity the little sillies cannot speak until they have forgotten everything they needed to tell

For a fleeting moment, my only desire was to remain in the company of the Little Ones, to let the days dissolve in their quiet presence. But soon, a restless current stirred within me. A vague, insistent thought took root: *I ought to be doing something.* I was not meant merely to be the fattener of boors, a passive observer in a world that demanded discovery. This was a marvellous realm, and it was my duty to uncover its laws. If I wished to repay the children's boundless goodness, I had to learn more of them than their simple words could convey—and to do that, I needed freedom. Surely, no suppression of growth could be essential to their loveliness, their truth, or their purity! In no conceivable world could such a discord exist between a constitution and its natural outcome; life and law could not be so at variance that perfection required thwarting development. Yet, the growth of the Little Ones *was* arrested. Something interfered. What was it?

Lona appeared to be the eldest, though she seemed no older than fifteen, yet she had long overseen a multitude who, in semblance and behavior, were mere children regarding her as their mother. Were they growing at all? I doubted it. They possessed scarcely an idea of time, and knew nothing of their own age. Lona herself believed she had lived forever. Full of wisdom yet empty of knowledge, she was simultaneously their Love and their Law. But what I perceived as her ignorance might simply be my own lack of insight. Her singular anxiety was clear: she feared her Little Ones would grow and change into "bad giants." Their "good giant" was bound to do his best for them, yet without deeper knowledge of their nature or history, he could do nothing and thus felt compelled to leave. They remained as they were; they had not become dependent on me. They were still my protectors, not mine. My presence, I realized with a sinking heart, only brought them closer to danger from their idiotic neighbors. I longed to teach them mathematics, to help them write down the dainty melodies they murmured and forgot. But knowledge, I mused, could make bad people worse; it must make good people better. I was convinced they would learn math, but might they not also be taught to record their songs?

The conclusion was inevitable: I must rise and continue my travels, hoping to find an elucidation of the fortunes and destiny of these bewitching creatures.

Yet, my design did not pass swiftly into action until what now occurred.

Preparing them for my temporary absence, I spoke to them one day while working, confessing that I would long ago have fled the bad giants if not for my love of the Little Ones. As if by one accord, they rushed forward, crowding upon me. They scrambled over one another, climbed the tree, and dropped onto my head until I was nearly smothered. With three tiny ones in my arms, one clinging to each shoulder, another standing straight up on my head, and four or five holding me fast by the legs, while a multitude climbed and descended upon this chaotic mass, I was helpless, overwhelmed like a boulder by lava. Absorbed in the merry struggle, none of them saw my tyrant approaching until he was almost upon me. With a single cry of "Take care, good giant!" they scattered like mice, dropped like hedgehogs, and fled up the tree like squirrels. The very same moment, the bad giant struck, his club landing with a sharp blow around the stem, knocking me to the ground. The children later told me they had sent him "such a many bumps of big apples and stones" that he was frightened and ran blundering home.

When I regained consciousness, it was night. Above me hung a few pale stars waiting for the moon. I thought I was alone. My head throbbed, and a terrible thirst gripped me. Turning wearily to my side, the moment my ear touched the earth, I heard the gushing and gurgling of water. The soft noises made me groan with longing, and instantly I was surrounded by a multitude of silent children. Delicious little fruits visited my lips, arriving again and again until my thirst was quenched.

Then, I became aware of sounds I had never heard there before: the air was filled with little sobs.

I tried to sit up, and a pile of small bodies instantly heaped itself against my back. Struggling to my feet amidst much pushing and pulling, I found the Little Ones were wonderfully strong for their size.

"You must go away, good giant," they said. "When the bad giants see you hurt, they will all trample on you."

"I think I must," I answered.

"Go and grow strong, and come again," they urged.

"I will," I replied, and sat down.

"Indeed you must go at once!" whispered Lona, who had been supporting me and now knelt beside me.

"I listened at his door," said one of the bigger boys. "I heard the bad giant say to his wife that he had found you idle, talking to a lot of moles and squirrels, and when he beat you, they tried to kill him. He said you were a wizard, and they must knock you, or they would have no peace."

"I will go at once," I said, "and return as soon as I have found out what is needed to make you bigger and stronger."

"We don't want to be bigger," they answered, their faces grave. "We WON'T grow bad giants! We are strong now; you don't know how much strong!"

It was useless to hold out a prospect that held no attraction for them. I said nothing more, rose, and moved slowly up the slope of the valley. Instantly, they formed a long procession; some led the way, some walked beside me helping me, and the rest followed. They kept feeding me as we went.

"You are broken," they said, "and much red juice has run out of you: put some in."

When we reached the edge of the valley, the moon was just lifting her forehead over the rim of the horizon.

"She has come to take care of you, and show you the way," said Lona.

I questioned those around me as we walked and learned of a great place with a giant-girl for queen. When I asked if it was a city, they said they did not know. Neither could they tell how far off it was, in what direction, or the giant-girl's name. All they knew was that she hated the Little Ones and would like to kill them, though she could not find them. I asked how they knew this; Lona answered that she had always known it. If the giant-girl came to look for them, they must hide hard. When I told them I would go to ask her why she hated them, they cried out, "No, no! she will kill you, good giant; she will kill you! She is an awful bad-giant witch!"

I asked them where I was to go then. They told me that beyond the baby-forest, away where the moon came from, lay a smooth green country, pleasant to the feet, without rocks or trees. But when I asked how I was to set out for it, they replied, "The moon will tell you, we think."

They were taking me up the second branch of the river bed; when they saw the moon reach her height, they stopped to return.

"We have never gone so far from our trees before," they said. "Now mind you watch how you go, that you may see inside your eyes how to come back to us."

"And beware of the giant-woman that lives in the desert," said one of the bigger girls as they turned, "I suppose you have heard of her!"

"No," I answered.

"Then take care not to go near her. She is called the Cat-woman. She is awfully ugly—and SCRATCHES."

As soon as the bigger ones stopped, the smaller had begun to run back. The others now looked at me gravely for a moment, then walked slowly away. Last to leave me, Lona held up the baby to be kissed, gazed into my eyes, whispered, "The Cat-woman will not hurt YOU," and departed without another word. I stood a while, gazing after them through the moonlight, then turned and, with a heavy heart, began my solitary journey. Soon the laughter of the Little Ones overtook me, like innumerable sheep-bells rippling the air and echoing in the rocks. I turned again, gazing after them: they went gamboling along with never a care in their sweet souls. But Lona walked apart with her baby.

Pondering as I went, I recalled many traits of my little friends. Once, when I suggested they leave their country to go with me to find another, they answered, "But that would be to NOT ourselves!" So strong was their love of place that their country seemed essential to their very being. Without ambition or fear, discomfort or greed, they had no motive to desire change; they knew of nothing amiss. Except for their babies, they had never had a chance to help anyone but myself. How were they to grow? But again, why should they grow? In seeking to improve their conditions, might I not do them harm? To enlarge their minds after the notions of my world—might it not be to distort and weaken them? Their fear of growth as a possible start for gianthood might be instinctive!

The part of the philanthropist is indeed a dangerous one; and the man who would do his neighbor good must first study how not to do him evil, and must begin by pulling the beam out of his own eye.

The moon hung full and bright, a familiar silver coin against the velvet dark, though tonight it wore an expression I had never seen before—a faint, enigmatic smile. As she dipped below the horizon, her lower rim seemed to dissolve into the earth, revealing not the cratered surface I knew, but a perfect, glowing cottage painted directly onto her disc. Through its open door and window, a warm light spilled forth, carrying with it a sudden, undeniable conviction: I was expected there.

In an instant, the moon vanished, swallowed by the encroaching night, and the cottage dissolved into the darkness. The landscape around me was a jagged succession of small ravines, impossible to navigate in the gloom. I resolved to wait for dawn, stretching out in a shallow sandy hollow. I made a supper of the fruits the children had given me at parting and drifted quickly into sleep.

I woke abruptly, staring up at constellations alien to my home world. After a moment of gazing, I sensed a presence seated on the ground a short distance above me. Startled, I realized I was no longer alone. The figure sat between me and the sky, its silhouette stark against the stars. From my low position in the hollow, it loomed larger than any human.

It turned its head, and only then did I see that its back was facing me.

"Will you not come with me?" a voice asked. It was sweet and mellow, unmistakably female.

"I thank you," I replied, "but I am not uncomfortable here. Where would you have me go? I like sleeping in the open air."

"There is no harm in the air," she returned, "but the creatures that roam these wastes at night are not ones a man would wish to have near while he sleeps."

"I have not been disturbed," I said.

"No," she admitted. "I have been sitting by you since you lay down."

"That is very kind of you! How did you know I was here? Why show me such favor?"

"I saw you," she answered, still keeping her back to me, "in the moonlight just as she set. I see poorly in the day, but perfectly at night. The shadow of my house would have hidden you, but both doors were open. I was out on the waste and saw you enter this hollow. You were asleep before I could reach you, and I was unwilling to disturb you. People are frightened if I approach suddenly. They call me the Cat-woman. It is not my name."

I recalled the children's warnings—that she was ugly and scratched. Yet her voice was gentle, tinged with apology. She could not be a malevolent giantess.

"You shall not hear it from me," I answered. "Please tell me what I *may* call you!"

"When you know me, call me by the name that seems to fit," she replied. "That will tell me what sort you are. People rarely give me the right one. It is well when they do."

"I suppose, madam, you live in the cottage I saw in the heart of the moon?"

"I do. I live there alone, except for visitors. It is a poor place, but I do what I can for my guests, and sometimes their sleep is sweet to them."

Her voice settled over me, inducing a strange stillness.

"I will go with you, madam," I said, rising.

She rose instantly and, without glancing back, led the way. I could just barely distinguish her form to follow. She was taller than I, yet not as towering as I had imagined. The fact that she never turned her face toward me piqued my curiosity, though it did not instill fear; her voice rang with such truth. Yet, how could I name someone I could not see? I tried to walk alongside her, but failed. When I quickened my pace, she matched it effortlessly, maintaining a lead. Soon, a flicker of unease took root. Why was she so careful not to be seen? Extraordinary ugliness might account for it—perhaps she feared terrifying me. A horror of inconceivable monstrosity began to assail me. Was I following an unheard-of hideousness through the dark? Almost, I regretted accepting her hospitality.

Neither of us spoke, and the silence grew oppressive. I *had* to break it.

"I want to find my way," I said, "to a place I have heard of, but whose name I have not yet learned. Perhaps you can tell it to me!"

"Describe it, and I will direct you. The stupid Bags know nothing, and the careless little Lovers forget almost everything."

"Where do those live?"

"You have just come from them!"

"I never heard those names before!"

"You would not have heard them. No one knows their own name!"

"Strange!"

"Perhaps so! But hardly anyone anywhere knows their own name! It would make many a fine gentleman stare to hear himself addressed by what is truly his name!"

I fell silent, wondering what my name might be.

"What do you fancy yours is now?" she continued, as if sensing my thought. "But, pardon me, it is a matter of no consequence."

I opened my mouth to answer, only to discover that my name had vanished from my mind entirely. I could not even recall the first letter! This was the second time I had been asked my name and failed to provide it.

"Never mind," she said. "It is not needed. Your real name is written on your forehead, but at present it whirls about so irregularly that no one can read it. I will do my part to steady it. Soon it will slow, and I hope, settle."

This startled me, and I fell quiet.

We left the channels and walked for a long time, yet the cottage remained unseen.

"The Little Ones told me," I said finally, "of a smooth green country, pleasant to the feet!"

"Yes?" she responded.

"They also told me of a girl-giantess who is a queen somewhere. Is that her country?"

"There is a city in that grassy land," she replied, "where a woman is princess. The city is called Bulika. But certainly the princess is not a girl! She is older than this world and came to it from yours—with a terrible history that is not yet over. She is an evil person and prevails greatly with the Prince of the Power of the Air. The people of Bulika were once simple folk, tilling the ground and pasturing sheep. She came among them, and they received her hospitably. She taught them to dig for diamonds and opals and sell them to strangers, making them abandon tillage and pasturage to build a city. One day they found a huge snake and killed it; so enraged was she that she declared herself their princess and became terrible to them. The name of the country at that time was THE LAND OF WATERS, for the dry channels you have crossed were then overflowing with live torrents, and the valley where the Bags and Lovers now grow fruit-trees was a lake receiving much of the flow. But the wicked princess gathered up what she could of the water over the whole country, closed it in an egg, and carried it away. Her lap, however, would not hold more than half; and the instant she departed, the remainder fled underground, leaving the country as dry and dusty as her own heart. Were it not for the waters beneath, every living thing would have perished long ago. For where there is no water, no rain falls; and where no rain falls, no springs rise. Ever since, the princess has lived in Bulika, holding the inhabitants in constant terror and doing her best to prevent their multiplication. Yet they boast and believe themselves prosperous, certainly a self-satisfied people—good at bargaining and buying, good at selling and cheating; holding well together for a common interest, and utterly treacherous where interests clash; proud of their princess and her power, and despising everyone they outwit; never doubting themselves the most honorable of all nations, and each man counting himself superior to any other. The depth of their worthlessness and the height of their vainglory can only be understood by those who have been there to see, who have learned to know these miserable, misgoverned, and self-deceived creatures."

"I thank you, madam. And now, if you please, will you tell me something about the Little Ones—the Lovers? I long heartily to serve them. Who and what are they? And how do they come to be there? Those children are the greatest wonder I have found in this world of wonders."

"In Bulika you may perhaps find light on those matters. There is an ancient poem in the library of the palace, I am told, which no one there can read, but in which it is plainly written that after the Lovers have endured great troubles and learned their own name, they will fill the land and make the giants their slaves."

"By that time they will have grown a little, will they not?" I asked.

"Yes, they will have grown; yet I think too they will not have grown. It is possible to grow and not to grow, to grow less and to grow bigger, both at once—yes, even to grow by means of not growing!"

"Your words are strange, madam!" I rejoined. "But I have heard it said that some words, because they mean more, appear to mean less!"

"That is true, and such words *must* be understood. It were well for the princess of Bulika if she heard what the very silence of the land is shouting in her ears all day long! But she is far too clever to understand anything."

"Then I suppose, when the little Lovers are grown, their land will have water again?"

"Not exactly so: when they are thirsty enough, they will have water, and when they have water, they will grow. To grow, they must have water. And, beneath, it flows still."

"I have heard that water twice," I said, "once when I lay down to wait for the moon—and when I woke the sun was shining! And once when I fell, nearly killed by the bad giant. Both times came the voices of the water, and healed me."

The woman never turned her head, keeping always a little ahead of me, but I heard every word she spoke. Her voice reminded me strangely of the woman in the house of death. Much of what she said I did not understand, and therefore cannot remember. But I forgot that I had ever been afraid of her.

We continued on, crossing a wide tract of sand before reaching the cottage. Its foundation stood in deep sand, but I could see it was built of rock. In character, the cottage resembled the sexton's, though with thicker walls. The door, heavy and strong, opened immediately into a large, bare room with two small windows opposite each other, lacking glass. My hostess walked in through the open door where the moon had shone, went straight to the farthest corner, took a long white cloth from the floor, and wound it about her head and face. Then she closed the other door, trimmed a small horn lantern standing on the hearth, and turned to receive me.

"You are very welcome, Mr. Vane!" she said, using the name I had forgotten. "Your entertainment will be scanty, but as the night is not far spent and the day not yet here, it is better you should be indoors. Here you will be safe, and a little lack is not a great misery."

"I thank you heartily, madam," I replied. "But, seeing you know the name I could not tell you, may I not now know yours?"

"My name is Mara," she answered.

Then I remembered the sexton and the little black cat.

"Some people," she went on, "take me for Lot's wife, lamenting over Sodom; and some think I am Rachel, weeping for her children; but I am neither of those."

"I thank you again, Mara," I said. "May I lie here on your floor till the morning?"

"At the top of that stair," she answered, "you will find a bed—on which some have slept better than they expected, and some have waked all night and slept all the next day. It is not a very soft one, but it is better than the sand—and there are no hyenas sniffing about it!"

The stair, narrow and steep, led straight up from the room to an unceiled, unpartitioned garret with one wide, low dormer window. Close under the sloping roof stood a narrow bed; the sight of it, with its white coverlet, made me shiver, so vividly it recalled the couches in the chamber of death. On the table lay a dry loaf, and beside it a cup of cold water. To me, who had tasted nothing but fruit for months, they were a feast.

"I must leave you in the dark," my hostess called from the bottom of the stair. "This lantern is all the light I have, and there are things to do tonight."

"It is of no consequence, thank you, madam," I returned. "To eat and drink, to lie down and sleep, are things that can be done in the dark."

"Rest in peace," she said.

I ate the loaf, drank every drop of water, and lay down. The bed was hard, the covering thin and scanty, and the night cold. I dreamed that I lay in the chamber of death, between the warrior and the lady with the healing wound.

I woke in the middle of the night, thinking I heard low noises of wild animals.

"Creatures of the desert scenting after me, I suppose!" I said to myself. Knowing I was safe, I would have gone back to sleep. But instantly a rough purring rose to a howl under my window, and I sprang from my bed to see what beast had uttered it.

Before the cottage door, bathed in the full radiance of the moon, a tall woman stood clothed in white, her back to me. She was stooping over a large white animal like a panther, patting and stroking it with one hand while with the other she pointed to the moon halfway up the heavens, then drew a perpendicular line to the horizon. Instantly, the creature darted off with amazing swiftness in the direction indicated. For a moment my eyes followed

I rose to continue my journey, walking countless miles across desolate sands. My heart ached for a mountain, a towering rock from whose peak I might survey the bleak plain or the dried riverbeds, searching for a sliver of hope. Yet, what purpose could such foresight serve? The true determinant of fate lies not in what lies beyond our vision, but within the man himself; the operation performed upon him is the event. To foresee is not to understand, for if prophecy were merely latent in humanity, it would surface far more often.

The sun hung halfway to the horizon when a rugged ascent appeared before me. But before I could reach it, the urge to climb evaporated, replaced by a desperate need to lie down. By then, twilight had deepened, and the air grew heavy with approaching night. At my feet lay a carpet of the softest, greenest moss—a couch fit for a king. I threw myself upon it, and weariness began to recede. The moment my head touched the ground, for the third time, I heard below me the sound of many waters. They played broken airs and ethereal harmonies against the stones of their buried channels. It was the loveliest chaos of musical material the aquatic harp could send to my ears! What masterpieces a Handel might have crafted from that ever-recurring gurgle and bell-like drip, blending them into mutually destructive yet mesmerizing melodies!

As I lay listening, my gaze wandered up and down the rocky slope rising abruptly above me. I read upon its face the record of ages past, when a cataract rushed down here, filling the channels that had led me to its foot. My heart swelled at the memory of that splendid tumult, where waves danced in helpless fall, massing their music into one great organ-roar below. Soon, the hidden brooks lulled me to sleep, their lullabies weaving themselves into my dreams.

I woke before the sun, eager to climb and see what lay beyond. Alas, there was nothing but a desert of finest sand! Not a trace remained of the river that had plunged down the rocks. The powdery drift had filled its course to the level of the dreary expanse. Looking back, I saw the river had divided into two branches as it fell: the one whose bank I had now followed to the foot of the rocky scarp, and the one I had first crossed to enter the Evil Wood. I could see the wood between the two on the far horizon. Before me and to the left, the desert stretched beyond vision, but far to the right, a lift in the skyline offered hope of the forest my hostess had directed me toward.

I sat down and reached into my pocket for the half-loaf I had brought—only then understanding what my hostess had meant. Verily, the bread was not for the morrow; it had shrunk and hardened into stone. I threw it away and set out again.

About noon, I encountered a few tamarisk and juniper trees, then some stunted firs. As I proceeded, thicker thickets and larger firs met me, until finally I stood in a forest of pines and other trees just like that where the Little Ones found their babies. I believed I had returned to a farther portion of the same place. But what mattered *where* when *everywhere* was the same as *nowhere*? I had not yet, by doing something within it, made *anywhere* into a place. I was not yet alive; I was only dreaming that I lived. I was but a consciousness with an outlook! Truly, I had been nothing else in the world I had left, but now I knew the fact! I told myself that if in this forest I caught the faint gleam of the mirror, I would turn far aside lest it should entrap me unawares and give me back to my old existence. Here, I might learn to be something by doing something! I could not endure the thought of returning with so many beginnings and no end achieved. The Little Ones would meet their appointed fate; the awful witch I would never meet; the dead would ripen and arise without me. I would only wake to know that I had dreamed, and that all my going was nowhither. I would rather go on and on than come to such a close!

I went deeper into the wood; I was weary and intended to rest within it.

The trees were now large, standing in a regular, almost geometric fashion with roomy spaces between. There was little undergrowth, and I could see a long way in every direction. The forest was like a great church—solemn, silent, and empty, for I met nothing on two feet or four that day. Now and then, a swift thing or a slow thing would cross the space where my eye happened to settle, but always at a distance, enhancing only the sense of wideness and vacancy. I heard a few birds and saw plenty of butterflies, some of marvellously gorgeous coloring and combinations, others of a pure, dazzling whiteness.

Coming to a spot where the pines stood farther apart, allowing flowering shrubs to thrive, and hoping it signaled a nearby dwelling, I took the direction where yet more and more roses grew. I was hungry for the voice and face of my kind—for any living soul, human or otherwise, which I might in some measure understand. What a hell of horror, I thought, to wander alone, a bare existence never going out of itself, never widening its life in another life, but bound by the cords of its poor peculiarities, lying an eternal prisoner in the dungeon of its own being! I began to learn that it was impossible to live for oneself, save in the presence of others—and then, alas, fearfully possible! Evil was only through good; selfishness but a parasite on the tree of life! In my own world, I had the habit of solitary song; here, not a crooning murmur ever parted my lips! There, I sang without thinking; here, I thought without singing! There, I had never had a bosom-friend; here, the affection of an idiot would be divinely welcome. "If only I had a dog to love!" I sighed, regarding with wonder my past self, which preferred the company of book or pen to that of man or woman; which, if the author of a tale I was enjoying appeared, would wish him away so I might return to his story. I had chosen the dead rather than the living, the thing thought rather than the thing thinking. "Any man," I said now, "is more than the greatest of books!" I had not cared for my live brothers and sisters, and now I was left without even the dead to comfort me!

The wood thinned yet more, and the pines grew larger, sending up huge stems like columns eager to support the heavens. More trees of other kinds appeared; the forest was growing richer! The roses were now trees, their flowers of astonishing splendour.

Suddenly, I spied what seemed a great house or castle. But its forms were so strangely indistinct that I could not be certain it was more than a chance combination of tree-shapes. As I drew nearer, its lines held together, yet neither they nor the body of it grew more definite. When at length I stood in front of it, I remained as doubtful of its nature as before. Habitable house or castle, it certainly was not; it might be a ruin overgrown with ivy and roses! Yet of building hidden in the foliage, not the poorest wall-remnant could I discern. Again and again, I seemed to descry what must be building, but it always vanished before closer inspection. Could it be, I pondered, that the ivy had embraced a huge edifice and consumed it, its interlaced branches retaining the shapes of the walls it had assimilated? I could be sure of nothing concerning its appearance.

Before me was a rectangular vacancy—the ghost of a doorway without a door. I stepped through it and found myself in an open space like a great hall, its floor covered with grass and flowers, its walls and roof of ivy and vine, mingled with roses.

There could be no better place to pass the night! I gathered a quantity of withered leaves, laid them in a corner, and threw myself upon them. A red sunset filled the hall; the night was warm, and my couch restful. I lay gazing up at the living ceiling, with its tracery of branches and twigs, its clouds of foliage, and peeping patches of loftier roof. My eyes waded about as if tangled in it until the sun was down and the sky began to grow dark. Then the red roses turned black, and soon only the yellow and white were visible. When they vanished, the stars came instead, hanging in the leaves like live topazes, throbbing and sparkling and flashing many colors. I was canopied by a tree from Aladdin's cave!

Then I discovered that it was full of nests, from which tiny heads, nearly indistinguishable, kept popping out with a chirp or two and disappearing again. For a while there were rustlings and stirrings and little prayers; but as the darkness grew, the small heads became still. At last, every feathered mother had her brood quiet under her wings. The talk in the little beds was over, and God's bird-nursery was at rest beneath the waves of sleep. Once more, a few flutterings made me look up: an owl went sailing across. I had only a glimpse of him, but several times felt the cool wafture of his silent wings. The mother birds did not move again; they saw that he was looking for mice, not children.

About midnight, I came wide awake, roused by a revelry whose noises were yet not loud. Neither were they distant; they were close to me, but attenuated. My eyes were so dazzled, however, that for a while I could see nothing; at last, they came to themselves.

I was lying on my withered leaves in the corner of a splendid hall. Before me was a crowd of gorgeously dressed men and gracefully robed women, none of whom seemed to see me. In dance after dance, they vaguely embodied the story of life, its meetings, its passions, its partings. A student of Shakespeare, I had learned something of every dance alluded to in his plays, and hence partially understood several of those I now saw—the minuet, the pavane, the hey, the coranto, the lavolta. The dancers were attired in fashion as ancient as their dances.

A moon had risen while I slept and was shining through the countless-windowed roof; but her light was crossed by so many shadows that at first I could distinguish almost nothing of the faces of the multitude. I could not fail, however, to perceive that there was something odd about them. I sat up to see them better. Heavens! Could I call them faces? They were skull fronts—hard, gleaming bone, bare jaws, truncated noses, lipless teeth which could no more take part in any smile! Of these, some flashed set and white and murderous; others were clouded with decay, broken and gapped, colored of the earth in which they seemed so long to have lain. Fearful yet, the eye-sockets were not empty. In each was a lidless living eye! In those wrecks of faces, eyes of every color, shape, and expression glowed or flashed or sparkled. The beautiful, proud eye, dark and lustrous, condescending to whatever it rested upon, was the more terrible; the lovely, languishing eye, the more repulsive; while the dim, sad eyes, less at variance with their setting, were sad exceedingly, and drew the heart in spite of the horror out of which they gazed.

I rose and went among the apparitions, eager to understand something of their being and belongings. Were they souls, or were they and their rhythmic motions but phantasms of what had been? By look nor by gesture, not by the slightest break in the measure, did they show themselves aware of me. I was not present to them: how much were they in relation to each other? Surely they saw their companions as I saw them! Or was each only dreaming itself and the rest? Did they know each other as they appeared to the others—a death with living eyes? Had they used their faces, not for communication, not to utter thought and feeling, not to share existence with their neighbors, but to appear what they wished to appear and conceal what they were? And, having made their faces masks, were they therefore deprived of those masks, condemned to go without faces until they repented?

"How long must they flaunt their facelessness in faceless eyes?" I wondered. "How long will the frightful punishment endure? Have they at length begun to love and be wise? Have they yet yielded to the shame that has found them?"

I heard not a word, saw not a movement of one naked mouth. Were they because of lying bereft of speech? With their eyes, they spoke as if longing to be understood: was it truth or falsehood that spoke in their eyes? They seemed to know one another: did they see one skull beautiful, and another plain? Difference must be there, and they had had long study of skulls!

My body was to theirs no obstacle: was I a body, and were they but forms? Or was I but a form, and were they bodies? The moment one of the dancers came close against me, that moment he or she was on the other side of me, and I could tell, without seeing, which, whether man or woman, had passed through my house.

On many of the skulls, the hair held its place, and however dressed, or in itself however beautiful, to my eyes it looked frightful on the bones of the forehead and temples. In such cases, the outer ear often remained also, and at its tip, the jewel of the ear as Sidney calls it, would hang, glimmering, gleaming, or sparkling—pearl or opal or diamond—under the night of brown or of raven locks, the sunrise of golden ripples, or the moonshine of pale, interclouded, fluffy cirrus clouds—all lichenous on the ivory-white or damp-yellow naked bone. I looked down and saw the daintily domed instep; I looked up and saw the plump shoulders basing the spring of the round, full neck—which withered at half-height to the fluted shaft of a gibbous cranium.

The music became wilder

I had not taken ten steps when a grotesque shape halted my progress. Drawing closer, I discerned a carriage of archaic design, its frame rotting yet stubbornly upright upon massive wheels. Flanking the central pole lay the bleached skeletons of two horses, their grim white skulls pointing upward as shriveled reins connected to the tattered hammer-cloth where a skeleton coachman sat. Both doors hung open, revealing two skeletal figures slumped in their respective corners.

As I watched, they stirred. With a rattling crash of bone against bone, they leaped from the doors. One collapsed immediately; the other stood for a fleeting moment, its structure trembling violently, before creeping to the opposite side of the carriage. Its leg-bones, thin and brittle, seemed insufficient to bear its weight, yet it managed to kneel beside its fallen comrade, attempting to lift it, nearly toppling in the effort.

The prostrate figure rose with a sudden surge of effort, settling into a sitting posture. It turned its yellowed cranium this way and that, then, ignoring its neighbor, scrambled to its feet by gripping the spokes of the rear wheel. Standing half-erect, back to its companion, it held one knee-joint with both hands. With similar difficulty and several contortions, the kneeling figure followed suit and addressed its partner.

"Have you injured yourself, my lord?" the voice came, distant and ill-formed, as if blown away by a spectral gale.

"Yes, I have," the other replied in a rougher tone. "You offered no aid, and this cursed knee is dislocated!"

"I did my utmost, my lord."

"No doubt, my lady, for it was dire! I feared I'd never find my footing again!—But, bless my soul, madam! Are you also missing your bones?"

She glanced down at herself.

"I have nothing else to be missing," she retorted. "And YOU, at least, cannot complain! But what on earth is this? Am I dreaming?"

"You may be dreaming, madam—I cannot say; but this knee of mine forbids the grateful illusion.—Ha! I too perceive I have nothing but bones to walk in! Not unbecoming for a man, perhaps! I trust, by goodness, they are not MY bones! Every ache is worse than another, and this loose knee the worst. The bed must have been damp—and I too drunk to notice!"

"Probably, my lord of Cokayne!"

"What! What!—You make me think I'm dreaming too, with all these aches! How do YOU know the title my rowdy cronies give me? I don't recall you!—Anyhow, you have no right to take liberties! My name is—Lord—tut, tut! What do you call me when I'm—when you are sober? I can't—at the moment!—Why, WHAT IS my name?—I must have been VERY drunk when I went to bed! I often am!"

"You visit so rarely that I do not know, my lord; but I may take your word for THAT!"

"I hope so!"

"—if for nothing else!" "Hoity-toity! I never told you a lie in my life!"

"You never told me anything but lies."

"Upon my honour!—Why, I never saw the woman before!"

"You knew me well enough to lie to, my lord!"

"I seem to begin dreaming I've met you before, but, upon my oath, there is nothing to identify you! Without your clothes, who is to tell who you may not be?—One thing I MAY swear—that I never saw you so undressed before!—By heaven, I have no recollection of you!"

"I am glad to hear it: my memories of you are the less distasteful!—Good morning, my lord!"

She turned away, hobbling with a clacking rhythm, pausing a few paces later.

"You are just as heartless as—as any other woman, madam!—Where in this hellish place shall I find my valet?—What was the cursed name I used to call the fool?"

He twisted his bare head on its creaking pivot, still clutching his knee with both hands.

"I will serve as your valet for once, my lord," the lady said, turning back. "—What can I do for you? It is difficult to say!"

"Tie my leg on, of course, you fool! Can't you see it's almost off? Heigho, my dancing days!"

She scanned her surroundings with empty sockets, finding a piece of fibrous grass. She bound the adjoining parts that formed the knee together. Once finished, he took a few carefully tentative steps.

"You used to stamp rather differently, my lord!" she remarked as she rose.

"Eh? What!—Now that I look at you again, it seems I used to hate you!—Eh?"

"Naturally, my lord! You hated many people!—your wife, of course, among them!"

"Ah, I begin, I be-gin---- But—I must have been somewhere for a long time!—I really forget!—There! Your damned, miserable bit of grass is breaking!—We used to get on PRETTY well together—eh?"

"Not that I remember, my lord. The only happy moments I shared with you were scattered across the first week of our marriage."

"Was that the way of it? Ha! ha!—Well, it's over now, thank goodness!"

"I wish I could believe it! Why were we sitting in that carriage together? It wakes apprehension!"

"I think we were divorced, my lady!"

"Hardly enough: we are still together!"

"A sad truth, but capable of remedy: the forest seems extensive!"

"I doubt! I doubt!"

"I am sorry I cannot think of a compliment without lying. To judge by your figure and complexion, you have lived hard since I last saw you! I cannot surely be QUITE so naked as your ladyship!—I beg your pardon, madam! I trust you'll take it I jest in a dream! It matters not; dreaming or waking, all is appearance! You can't be certain of anything, and that's as good as knowing there is nothing! Life may teach any fool that!"

"It has taught me the fool I was to love you!"

"You were not the only fool! Women had a trick of falling in love with me:—I had forgotten you were one of them!" "I did love you, my lord—a little—at one time!"

"Ah, there was your mistake, my lady! You should have loved me much, loved me devotedly, loved me savagely—loved me eternally! Then I should have grown tired of you sooner, and not hated you so much afterward!—But let bygones be bygones!—WHERE are we? Locality is the question! To be or not to be, is NOT the question!"

"We are in the other world, I presume!"

"Granted!—but which or what sort of other world? This can't be hell!"

"It must: there's marriage in it! You and I are damned in each other."

"Then I'm not like Othello, damned in a fair wife!—Oh, I remember my Shakespeare, madam!"

She picked up a broken branch from a bush, steadying herself, and walked away, tossing her small skull.

"Give that stick to me," cried her late husband. "I need it more than you."

She gave no answer.

"You mean to make me beg for it?"

"Not at all, my lord. I mean to keep it," she replied, continuing her slow departure.

"Give it me at once; I mean to have it! I require it."

"Unfortunately, I think I require it myself!" returned the lady, walking faster, her joints cracking and bones clinking sharply.

He started to follow, but nearly fell; his grass binding had burst. With an oath, he stopped, grasping his leg again.

"Come and tie it up properly!" he would have thundered, but only piped and whistled!

She turned to look at him.

"Come and tie it up instantly!" he repeated.

She walked a step or two farther.

"I swear I will not touch you!" he cried.

"Swear on, my lord! There is no one here to believe you. But, pray, do not lose your temper, or you will shake yourself to pieces. Where to find string enough to tie up all your crazy joints is more than I can tell."

She returned, kneeling once more at his side—first, however, placing the disputed stick beyond his reach and within hers.

The instant she finished retying the joint, he lunged, seemingly to seize her by the hair, but his hard fingers slipped on the smooth poll.

"Disgusting!" he muttered, grabbing her upper arm-bone instead.

"You will break it!" she said, looking up from her knees.
