"I will, then!" he answered, beginning to strain.

"I shall not tie your leg again the next time it comes loose!" she threatened.

He gave her arm a vicious twist, but luckily her bones were in better condition than his. She stretched her other hand toward the broken branch.

"That's right: reach me the stick!" he grinned.

She swung it around with such force that one of the bones of his sounder leg snapped. He fell, choking with curses. The lady laughed.

"Now you will have to wear splints always!" she said. "Such dry bones never mend!"

"You devil!" he cried.

"At your service, my lord! Shall I fetch you a couple of wheel-spokes? Neat—but heavy, I fear!"

He turned his bone-face aside, offering no answer, and lay groaning. I marveled he hadn't shattered completely upon falling. The lady rose and walked away—not ungracefully, I thought.

"What can come of it?" I murmured to myself. "These are too wretched for any world, and this cannot be hell, for the Little Ones are here, and the sleepers too! What can it all mean? Can things ever come right for skeletons?"

"There are words too big for you and me: ALL is one of them, and EVER is another," a voice near me said. It was a voice I knew.

I looked about but saw no speaker.

"You are not in hell," it resumed. "Neither am I. But those skeletons are in hell!"

Before he finished, I caught sight of a raven perched on a beech branch directly overhead. The moment he left it, he alighted on the ground, standing there as the thin old man of the library, with his long nose and long coat.

"The male was never a gentleman," he continued, "and in this bony stage of retrogression, with his skeleton showing through his skin and his character outside his manners, he does not look like one. The female is less vulgar and possesses a little heart. But, with society's restraints removed, you see them now just as they are and always were!"

"Tell me, Mr. Raven, what will become of them," I said.

"We shall see," he replied. "In their day they were the handsomest couple at court; and now, even in their dry bones, they seem to regard their former repute as an inalienable possession. To see their faces, however, may yet do something for them! They felt themselves rich too while they had pockets, but they have already begun to feel rather pinched! My lord used to regard my lady as a worthless encumbrance, for he was tired of her beauty and had spent her money; now he needs her to cobble his joints for him! These changes have roots of hope in them. Besides, they cannot now get far away from each other, and they see none else of their own kind: they must at last grow weary of their mutual repugnance, and begin to love one another! For love, not hate, is deepest in what Love 'loved into being.'"

"I saw many more of their kind an hour ago, in the hall close by!" I said.

"Of their kind, but not of their sort," he answered. "For many years these will see none such as you saw last night. Those are centuries in advance of these. You saw that those could even dress themselves a little! It is true they cannot yet retain their clothes so long as they would—only, at present, for a part of the night; but they are pretty steadily growing more capable, and will by and by develop faces; for every grain of truthfulness adds a fibre to the show of their humanity. Nothing but truth can appear; and whatever is must seem."

"Are they upheld by this hope?" I asked.

"They are upheld by hope, but they do not in the least know their hope; to understand it, is yet immeasurably beyond them," answered Mr. Raven.

His unexpected appearance caused me no astonishment. I was like a child, constantly wondering, and surprised at nothing.

"Did you come to find me, sir?" I asked.

"Not at all," he replied. "I have no anxiety about you. Such as you always come back to us."

"Tell me, please, who am I such as?" I said.

"I cannot make my friend the subject of conversation," he answered, with a smile.

"But when that friend is present!" I urged.

"I decline the more strongly," he rejoined.

"But when that friend asks you!" I persisted.

"Then most positively I refuse," he returned.

"Why?"

"Because he and I would be talking of two persons as if they were one and the same. Your consciousness of yourself and my knowledge of you are far apart!"

The lapels of his coat flew out, and the lappets lifted, and I thought the metamorphosis of HOMO to CORVUS was about to take place before my eyes. But the coat closed again in front of him, and he added, with seeming inconsequence,

"In this world never trust a person who has once deceived you. Above all, never do anything such a one may ask you to do."

"I will try to remember," I answered;

I pressed onward, my gaze fixed on the moon. She hung low, staring blindly into the canopy, a battered disc of tarnished copper, dim and weary. No cloud shielded her; the stars burned too fiercely for her pale countenance. "Will this endure forever?" she seemed to whisper, her light flickering in protest. She marched one way, I another, yet our paths wove together through the woods for miles. We shared little conversation, my eyes glued to the earth, but her desolation was a weight I felt without needing to see it. I was the living shadow; she was the dull, fading shine.

Then, beneath the sprawling boughs of a great tree, something white arrested my attention. Drawing near, the vague shape resolved into a human form. "Another skeleton," I murmured, dropping to my knees. Yet, as my hand closed over it, I realized it was flesh, though barely. It lay on its side, radiating a chill that was not the cold of stone, but the terrifying absence of life. The closer I peered, the more certain I became: she was dead. For a fleeting, bewildered instant, I imagined her a wild dancer, a ghostly Cinderella lost in this strange, outdoor night, perished before she could return home. She was naked, her skin so wasted that every rib stood out like a wire beneath a thin elastic casing. Her teeth, beautiful yet terrible, gleamed ghastly through retracted lips, and her hair cascaded in thick, fine strands, black as the void.

She was the frame of a tall, likely graceful woman. How had she arrived here? Not of her own volition, certainly not in such a state of ruin. Strength must have failed her; she had fallen and starved. But how could she be so emaciated? And why was she stripped bare? Which savages had taken her clothes, or what beasts had devoured them? The fact that her body remained was not so strange.

I rose, trembling with a mix of revulsion and duty. I could not leave her exposed. Natural reverence demanded it; even the garment of a woman commands respect. To leave her uncovered was to invite irreverent eyes and brutal claws. Years might pass before the rains washed her into the soil, but the ground here was hard, locked tight with interlacing roots. I had only my bare hands.

At first glance, death seemed recent—no signs of decay marred her. But what was left of her to decay? Could she still be alive? In this alien world, logic often bent. Even if she were, revival was unlikely, yet I needed certainty before I could bury her.

Recalling a cluster of ripe grapes I had spotted in the forest-hall's doorway, I remembered the juice I had carried. Perhaps it could revive her. It was all I possessed. Her mouth hung slightly open, though her head lay at an awkward angle. Sliding my arm under her shoulder, I discovered the pine needles beneath were warm. She could not have been dead long; she *might* still be breathing, though I felt no heartbeat and saw no rise of her chest. One of her hands was clenched tightly around something small. I forced a grape into her mouth, but there was no swallow.

Desperation drove me to act. I spread a thick bed of pine needles and dry leaves, draped my own garment—still warm from my body—over it, and lifted her onto this makeshift cradle. I covered her completely, trapping whatever faint warmth remained within, hoping the returning sun would amplify it. Another grape yielded no result; her mouth and throat remained still.

"Doubt," I told myself, "is a poor excuse for inaction." Her skin was so tight against her bones that friction was dangerous. I crawled into the leafy heap, embracing her as closely as I dared. I had little heat left, but I would give it all to her. Thus I spent the remaining night, sleepless and yearning for dawn. Her cold seeped into me, but none of my warmth returned.

If I had fled from the beautiful sleepers in their silver couches to lie alone with such a bedfellow, I would have refused a lovely privilege for an awful duty. Beneath the slow-setting moon, I lay with the dead, guarding for the light.

As the darkness receded and the eastern horizon grew dim, I noticed a subtle undulation near the ground—a large snake gliding in a straight line. Soon, a roe doe and her fawn appeared, followed by bear-cubs leading smaller ones. The light intensified until a troop of horses trotted past. Though no larger than Shetland ponies, they moved with the perfect form and gait of full-grown beasts, representing every breed from cart-horse to charger. Dwarf cattle and small elephants followed in their wake.

"Why are the children not here!" I cried out. "The moment I am free of this burden, I must return for them!"

Where were these creatures bound? Was this an exodus or a morning ritual? I waited for the sun. Until he rose, I could not abandon her. Placing my hand on her body, I noted it felt marginally warmer. Had it absorbed some of my lost heat? Or had it generated its own? Hope had not diminished, but it had not grown either.

The day's forehead began to glow, and soon the sun peered up, as if witnessing the stir of this new world for the first time. At his innocent brilliance, I rose, feeling a surge of life against death. Removing the handkerchief I had used to shield her face from the pine needles, I scanned her features anxiously. Had I found a jewel or merely its empty case?

Her face lay motionless, drawn and hollow, every bone sharp beneath the skin. Every tooth cut through her lips. Her garment was worn to its threads, yet the bird of heaven might still nestle within, waiting to sing. The sun beat down on her face. I rearranged the handkerchief, laid a few leaves lightly over her, and finally set out to follow the creatures.

Their trail was well-worn, merging with others from the sides, broadening into a path. As I walked, the trees retreated and the grass thickened. Soon the forest vanished, replaced by a vast expanse of lush green stretching to the horizon. A small river flowed along the forest's edge, and the track led to it. Seeing the water sparked a new, undefined hope. The stream looked deep and full, yet narrow, rising in a bluish mist that vanished as it ascended. On the opposite bank, small animals fed in the plentiful grass. They must sleep in the forest and swim across at dawn. I knelt to drink, but the water was scalding hot and tasted of strange metal.

I leaped up. Here was the warmth I sought—the first necessity of life! I raced back to my helpless charge.

No one who has not faced such solitude can understand the redemption I felt in saving this woman. "Prove what she may," I thought, "I shall at least no longer be alone!" I had found myself such poor company that now I truly understood hope. This blessed water would expel the cold death and drown my desolation.

I carried her to the stream. She was tall yet miraculously light; her bones were so delicate she felt like a sleeping child. I was so hopeful that I feared moving her too roughly. I laid her on the bank and dipped my garment into the hot water, beginning to bathe her pitiful form. So wasted she was that, save for the black abundance of her hair, I could not guess her age. Her eyelids were just barely open, a crack in the clouds of night through which no sun shone, making her look all the more dead.

The longer I bathed her, the less hope I held that she would ever regain strength or lift those lids. Still, I worked continuously, ensuring no part cooled while I attended to another. Gradually, the body warmed. Finally, I ventured to submerge her. I entered the stream, drawing her in while keeping her face above the water, letting the swift current wash over her. I noted that despite the heat, her clenched hand never relaxed.

After ten minutes, I lifted her out, dried her, and covered her with leaves. I ran to the forest for more, then for a third and fourth load.

Now I could leave her to explore for shelter. Running upstream toward rocky hills I had spotted, I found the river bursting forth from a rock at the base of one hill. It seemed to have tumbled down a stair inside, eager to escape, finding its door only at the foot. The opening was too small for the torrent, so I crept through into a small cave. Inside, the water rose quietly from the ground like the base of a column, running along a deep, narrow channel.

I saw my chance. If I could find fallen boughs long enough to span the channel, sturdy enough to bear weight without bending, I could build a couch upon them, cushioned with smaller branches and leaves, kept constantly warm by the stream below. I ran back to check on my charge.

She lay exactly as I had left her. The heat had not revived her, but it had not extinguished hope. I gathered boulders from the channel, arranging them at her feet and flanks. Returning to the wood, I quickly found suitable beech boughs, their dry yellow leaves still clinging. I laid a floor of bridge-bed over the torrent, crossing it with smaller branches, interlacing twigs, and burying everything deep in leaves and moss.

When I had finally constructed a warm, dry, soft couch, I carried her body to it. It was so light I sometimes feared, as I placed her down, that I would find a skeleton. Laying her gently on the pathless bridge, the relief of parting with that fear was greater than the relief of setting her down. I covered her with a thick layer of leaves and tried feeding her again with a grape. To my joy, I could open her mouth slightly wider. The grape lay unheeded, but I hoped the juice might trickle down.

After an hour or two, she was no longer cold. The brook's warmth had penetrated her frame—truly, just a frame—and she was warm to the touch. Not the warmth of life, perhaps, but a warmth that made life possible if she were alive. I had read of one in a trance lying motionless for weeks!

Day after day, night after night, for seven long cycles, I sat or lay, waking and sleeping, but always watching. Every morning I bathed in the hot stream, feeling refreshed as if I had eaten and drunk, giving me the courage to lay her in it daily. Once, a shadow of discoloration on her left side sent a terrible shock through me, but by the next morning it had vanished. I continued the treatment, placing a fresh grape in her mouth each morning after her bath.

I ate the grapes and berries I found in the forest, believing that with my daily baths in that river, I could survive without food entirely.

Every time I slept, I dreamed of a wounded angel, unable to fly, staying with me until she loved me and refused to leave. Every time I woke, I saw instead the white, motionless, wasted face upon the couch. Even Adam, when first seeing Eve asleep, could not have looked more anxiously for her awakening than I did for this woman's. Adam knew nothing of himself, perhaps nothing of his need for another self; I, an alien among my kind, had learned to love what I had lost. If this one shred of womanhood disappeared, I would have nothing but a consuming hunger for life. I forgot even the Little Ones; things were not amiss with them. Here lay someone who might wake, open eyes, and look upon me!

Now I truly knew solitude—the gaze upon one who saw and heard nothing, moved and spoke nothing. I saw that a man alone is merely a being who *can* become a man, a need, a possibility. To be sufficient for himself, a being must be an eternal, self-existent worm! Man is so superbly constituted, so simply complex, rising from lower organisms and spiritual structures, that no atmosphere less divine than that offered by other souls can comfort or nourish his life. Nowhere but in other lives can he breathe. Only by the reflection of other lives can he ripen his specialty, develop the idea of himself, the individuality that distinguishes him. Were all men alike, each would still possess individuality through personal consciousness, but there would be little reason for more than two or three such beings. For the development of the differences that create a large and lofty unity, making millions into a church, endless influence and reaction are indispensable. A man to be perfect—complete, having reached the spiritual condition of persistent and universal growth, inheriting the infinitude of his Father—must have the education of a world of fellow-men. Save for the hope of life's dawn in the form beside me, I would have fled to the beasts that grazed and did not speak. Better to wander with them than to live alone! But with the faintest prospect of a woman as my friend, I, poorest of creatures, was yet a possible man.

I awoke from a deep slumber to the throbbing agony of my right hand. The back of it was swollen, marked by a triangular wound that resembled the bite of a leech. By evening, the swelling had receded, the pain almost vanished, leaving only a faint scar. I scoured the cave, lifting every stone, searching for any creature capable of such an attack, but found nothing.

Days stretched into weeks. The figure in the corner remained motionless, eyes sealed shut. Yet, it was not dead; there was no scent of decay, only the crisp purity of the cave air. More subtly, the sharp angles of the skeleton seemed to soften, the form rounding out, the skin losing its parchment-like tension. If this transformation was real, then life had returned—the tide that had receded toward the infinite was flowing again. I watched with feverish hope, twenty times a day seeking proof of revival, twenty times doubting, sometimes despairing entirely. Yet, whenever I recalled the moment I discovered her, hope surged anew.

Weeks passed. One night, unable to sleep, I rose to breathe the cooler air of the cavern entrance. The moon was full, casting a silver clarity over the shadows. Before leaving, I glanced at my charge. "Eternal bliss!" I whispered, my heart hammering against my ribs. "Do I see her eyes?" They appeared to glow—great orbs, dark as the void between stars, luminous from an excess of darkness, set against the pale whiteness of her face. I crept closer, terrified my heartbeat might startle her. I leaned down. Alas, her lids were closed tight. Hope and imagination had conspired to deceive me. My desire would remain unfulfilled. I turned away, collapsed onto the cave floor, and wept. Then, a thought struck me: her eyes had been slightly open before, a tiny slit through which nothingness had peered. Perhaps she had opened them for a fleeting moment and fallen back asleep; perhaps she was awake, holding them shut. In either case, life, however faint, must have commanded them. Comforted by this logic, I slept.

That night, I was bitten again. I awoke with a burning thirst.

Morning brought another search, equally fruitless. The wound was identical, healing swiftly by evening. I reasoned that a large leech occasionally ventured from the hot stream. "But if its goal is blood," I told myself, "so long as I guard this place, my treasure is safe."

That same morning, after peeling a grape as my ritual, I placed it in her mouth. Her lips twitched, accepting the fruit. I *knew* then that she lived.

Hope ignited within me. She must be able to rise at will. I ventured into the forest, seeking materials. Fibrous skeletons, reminiscent of the prickly pear, suggested themselves immediately. I gathered them, dried them in the sun, separated the reticulated layers, and began weaving two loose garments—one for her waist, one for her shoulders. Using the stiletto-point of an aloe leaf and various filaments, I stitched three thick layers of the tissue together.

For the following week, the only change was that she consumed grapes more readily. Signs grew clearer: she was becoming plumper, her skin fairer. Yet her eyes remained closed. A terrible fear would seize me, that her growth was some grotesque fungal expansion, the grapes insufficient to explain such rapid change.

The bites became regular, occurring every three days. The creature, whatever it was, visited only at night, striking my neck or arm with a single bite while I slept. I lay awake for hours, watching, hearing nothing, seeing nothing. I never felt the sting. Despairing of ever catching it, I ceased my searches. I knew I was losing blood dangerously, yet I cared little. Death was yielding to life before my eyes; a soul was gathering strength to save me from solitude. We would depart together, and I would recover.

The garments were finished. Satisfied with my work, I matted the fibers into sandals.

One night, I woke suddenly, breathless and faint, craving air. I rose to crawl from the cave when a faint rustle in the bedding leaves froze me.

"I caught the vile thing," a weak voice murmured, speaking my mother tongue. "I caught it in the very act!"

She was alive! She spoke! I held my breath, terrified that my transport would terrify her.

"What creature?" I breathed.

"The creature that was biting you," she answered.

"What was it?"

"A great white leech."

"How big?" I forced myself to remain calm.

"Nearly six feet long, I should think."

"You have saved my life, perhaps! But how could you touch such horror! How brave!"

"I did!" was her only reply, and I thought I heard a shudder.

"Where is it? What did you do?"

"I threw it in the river."

"It will return, I fear!"

"I doubt I could have killed it, even if I knew how. I heard you moaning, rose to check, saw the monster at your neck, and pulled it away. I could not hold it, nor throw it far. I only heard it splash into the water!"

"We'll kill it next time!" I declared, then turned pale, sought the open air, and collapsed.

When consciousness returned, the sun was high. The lady stood a distance away, looking, even in the clumsy attire I had crafted, both grand and graceful. I *had* seen those glorious eyes! Through the night they had shone! Dark as primordial darkness, they now outshone the day. She stood erect as a column, regarding me. Her pale cheek showed no emotion, only inquiry. I rose.

"We must go!" I said. "The white leech..."

I stopped. A strange smile flickered across her beautiful face.

"Did you find me here?" she asked, pointing to the cave.

"No; I brought you here," I replied.

"You brought me?"

"Yes."

"From where?"

"From the forest."

"What have you done with my clothes—and my jewels?"

"You had none when I found you."

"Then why did you not leave me?"

"Because I hoped you were not dead."

"Why should you have cared?"

"Because I was very lonely, and wanted you to live."

"You would have kept me enchanted for my beauty!" she said, with proud scorn.

Her words and gaze roused my indignation.

"There was no beauty left in you," I said.

"Then why did you not let me alone?"

"Because you were of my own kind."

"Of *your* kind?" she cried, in a tone of utter contempt.

"I thought so, but find I was mistaken!"

"Doubtless you pitied me!"

"Never had woman more claim on pity, or less on any other feeling!"

With an expression of pain, mortification, and unutterable anger, she turned from me and stood silent. Starless night lay profound in the gulfs of her eyes; hate for the one who restored it had slain their splendor. The light of life had gone from them.

"Had you failed to rouse me, what would you have done?" she asked suddenly, without moving.

"I would have buried it."

"It? What? You would have buried *this*?" she exclaimed, flashing around me in a white fury, arms thrown out, eyes darting forks of cold lightning.

"Nay; that I saw not! These weary weeks of watching and tending have brought you back," I answered—for with such a woman, I must be plain. "Had I seen the smallest sign of decay, I would have buried you instantly."

"Dog of a fool!" she cried. "I was but in a trance—Samoil, what a fate! Go and fetch the she-savage from whom you borrowed this hideous disguise."

"I made it for you. It is hideous, but I did my best."

She drew herself up to her tall height.

"How long have I been insensible?" she demanded. "A woman could not make that dress in a day!"

"Not in twenty days," I rejoined, "hardly in thirty!"

"Ha! How long do you pretend I have lain unconscious? Answer me at once."

"I cannot tell how long you lay when I found you, but there was nothing left of you but skin and bone. That was more than three months ago. Your hair was beautiful; nothing else! I have done for it what I could."

"My poor hair!" she said, bringing a great armful of it around from behind her. "It will be more than a three-months' care to bring *you* to life again! I suppose I must thank you, though I cannot say I am grateful!"

"There is no need, madam: I would have done the same for any woman—yes, or for any man either!"

"How is it my hair is not tangled?" she said, fondling it.

"It always drifted in the current."

"How? What do you mean?"

"I could not have brought you to life but by bathing you in the hot river every morning."

She gave a shudder of disgust, standing for a while with her gaze fixed on the hurrying water. Then she turned to me.

"We must understand each other!" she said. "You have done me the two worst of wrongs—compelled me to live, and put me to shame. Neither can I pardon!"

She raised her left hand and flung it out as if repelling me. Something ice-cold struck me on the forehead. When I came to myself, I was on the ground, wet and shivering.

I rose, my heart a chaotic drum against my ribs, and scanned the darkness. For a heartbeat, she was gone. The solitude crashed back in, heavy and suffocating, like a storm cloud returning after the rain. The woman I had pulled from the jaws of death had vanished, leaving me in a void of absolute desolation. I could not endure this hideous isolation another second. Had I truly committed a crime against her? Was I destined to spend the rest of my existence carrying the weight of a burden I had forced upon her?

I spotted her moving quickly across the grass, heading away from the river. I took a final plunge into the water, a desperate restorative, and launched myself in pursuit. The bite of the white leech and the strike of the woman had drained me, yet my vitality was already surging back. I kept her in my sights with ease.

"Is this the end?" I whispered, a mournful tune playing in my chest. Her eyes, burning with anger and hatred, haunted my vision. I understood her resentment; I had forced life upon her, a cruel gift. But how else could I have hurt her? Why did she loathe me? Could the very concept of modesty rebel against genuine service? How could the proudest soul, who knew every movement I made, harbor even a shred of feeling that I had dishonored her? With what reverence had I touched her! Like a father tending a motherless child, I had borne her and cared for her. Had all my labor, all my desperate hope, been redeemed only by ingratitude? "No," I told myself, forcing conviction into my trembling voice. "Beauty must possess a heart! No matter how deeply buried, it exists! The deeper the tomb, the more powerful and true its awakening will be in its beautiful grave! To rouse that heart is a greater gift than the happiest life; it is to offer her a nobler, higher existence!"

She climbed a gentle slope ahead of me, walking with the straight, steady purpose of one who knows her destination. Suddenly, I realized she was widening the gap between us. I summoned every ounce of strength, and it flooded my veins like a fresh tide. My body felt ethereal, light as wind, and I rapidly closed the distance.

She never glanced back. She moved swiftly, like a Greek goddess fleeing danger, yet without panic. When I was merely three yards away, she turned sharply, her grace unbroken, and halted. She showed no sign of fatigue or heat. Her pallor was not sickness, but a pure, luminous whiteness. Her breathing was slow and deep. Her eyes seemed to fill the heavens, casting light upon the world. It was nearly noon, yet the sensation was akin to a great night where invisible dew makes the stars appear enormous.

"Why do you follow me?" she asked, her voice quiet but stern, as if she had never seen me before.

"I have lived so long," I answered, "on the mere hope of your eyes, that I must want to see them again!"

"You WILL not be spared!" she said coldly. "I command you to stop where you stand."

"Not until I see you in a place of safety will I leave you," I replied.

"Then take the consequences," she said, resuming her swift, gliding walk.

But as she turned, she cast a glance my way, and I stood frozen, as if pierced by a spear. Her scorn had failed; she would kill me with her beauty!

Despair restored my will; the spell shattered. I ran, overtaking her.

"Have pity upon me!" I cried.

She ignored me. I followed her like a child whose mother pretends to abandon him. "I will be your slave!" I shouted, laying my hand on her arm.

She turned as if a serpent had bitten her. I cowered before the blazing intensity of her eyes, unable to look away.

"Pity me," I begged again.

She resumed walking.

The entire day I trailed her. The sun climbed to its zenith, paused, and descended. She never paused, never slowed her pace. I never ceased to follow.

As the sun dipped below the horizon and night crept up, I stayed close. If I lost sight of her for a single moment, it would be forever!

We had walked all day over thick, soft grass. Abruptly, she stopped and threw herself onto it. There was still enough light to see she was utterly exhausted. I stood behind her, gazing down.

Did I love her? I knew she was not good! Did I hate her? I could not leave her! I knelt beside her.

"Go away! Do not dare touch me," she screamed.

Her arms lay on the grass by her sides, paralyzed.

Suddenly, they snapped shut around my neck, rigid as the limbs of a torture-maiden. She pulled my face down to hers, and her lips clung to my cheek. A sharp sting of pain shot through me, pulsing violently. I could not move a hair's breadth. Gradually, the pain faded. A slumberous weariness, a dreamy pleasure, stole over me, and then I knew nothing.

Suddenly, I woke. The moon hung a little way above the horizon, spreading no radiance; it was merely a bright object set in blackness. My cheek stung; I touched it and found a wet spot. My neck ached; there was another wet spot there! I sighed heavily, feeling incredibly tired. I turned my eyes listlessly around—and saw what had happened to the moonlight. It had gathered about the lady! She stood in a shimmering nimbus! I rose and staggered toward her.

"Down!" she cried imperiously, as if addressing a rebellious dog. "Follow me a step if you dare!"

"I will!" I murmured, with an agonized effort.

"Step within the gates of my city, and my people will stone you: they do not love beggars!"

I was deaf to her words. Weak as water, half-awake, I did not know I was moving, but the distance between us shrank. She took one step back, raised her left arm, and with a clenched fist struck me on the forehead. I felt as though hit by an iron hammer and fell.

I sprang to my feet, cold and wet, but clear-headed and strong. Had the blow revived me? It had left neither wound nor pain—but how was I wet? I could not have been lying long, for the moon was no higher!

The lady stood some yards away, her back to me. She was doing something I could not distinguish. Then, by a sudden gleam, I knew she had thrown off her garments and stood white in the dazed moonlight. She stood for a moment—and fell forward.

A streak of white shot away in a swift, drawn line. The same instant, the moon recovered itself, flashing fully, and I saw that the streak was a long-bodied creature rushing in great, low-curved bounds over the grass. Dark spots seemed to run like a stream down its back, as if it had been fleeing along under the edge of a wood, catching the shadows of the leaves.

"God of mercy!" I cried, "is the terrible creature speeding to the night-infolded city?" And I seemed to hear from afar the sudden burst and spread of outcries, as the pale savage bounded from house to house, rending and slaying.

While I gazed after it, fear-stricken, a second large creature, pure white, shot past me from behind like a swift, almost noiseless arrow. Its path was straight for the spot where the lady had fallen, and, as I thought, lay. My tongue stuck to the roof of my mouth. I sprang forward to pursue the beast, but the spot I aimed for was already far behind it.

"It was well," I thought, "that I could not cry out: if she had risen, the monster would have been upon her!"

But when I reached the place, no lady was there; only the garments she had dropped lay dark in the moonlight.

I stood staring after the second beast. It tore over the ground with even greater swiftness than the first—in long, level, skimming leaps, the very embodiment of wasteless speed. It followed the line the other had taken, and I watched it grow smaller and smaller until it disappeared into the uncertain distance.

But where was the lady? Had the first beast surprised her, creeping upon her noiselessly? I had heard no shriek! There had not been time to devour her! Could it have caught her up as it ran and borne her away to its den? So laden, it could not have run so fast! And I should have seen that it carried something!

Horrible doubts began to wake in me. After a thorough but fruitless search, I set out in the track of the two animals.

The moon vanished beneath a heavy, bruised cloud, and from the sudden gray swallowed the world, a white figure erupted—a woman clutching a child to her heaving chest, her body bent in a desperate, driven arc. She moved on a parallel line to my own, a blur of terror and frantic speed, utterly blind to my presence.

"She is hunted!" I whispered to the silence. "A predator of this nightmare night stalks her!"

To pursue her would only ignite fresh panic. Instead, I stepped into her shadow, intending to intercept her pursuer. But before I could turn, a soft, swift rush erupted behind me. Something leaped over my head, striking me with brutal force against the forehead and sending me crashing to the earth. I surged back to my feet instantly, but all I caught was a flash of vanishing white. I chased the beast, blood already trickling down my face, but after only a few paces, a shriek of pure despair tore through the quivering night. I ran harder, though dread coiled in my gut that it was already too late.

Moments later, a low, white shape emerged from the vapor-dusted moonlight. At first, I assumed it was another hunter, but it approached with agonizing slowness, making strange, floundering leaps that spoke of immense pain. I drew aside, waiting. As it neared, I saw it hobbled on three legs, dragging its left fore-paw high above the ground. Its skin was shining white, marred by dark, oval spots, accompanied by a low, rushing sound like water cascading over grass. As it passed, I saw something streaming from the lifted limb.

"It is blood," I realized. "Someone braver than I has wounded this beast!" Yet, a sudden, crushing pity seized me at the sight of the suffering creature. Even if an axe had been in my hand, I could not have brought it down. In a broken succession of hobbling leaps, it vanished into the darkness, its blood seeming to flow back softly through the grass beside me. "If it bleeds like this," I thought, "it will soon be harmless."

I pressed on, hoping to find the woman and perhaps her deliverer.

A short distance away, I found her seated on the grass, the child resting in her lap.

"Can I help you?" I asked.

At my voice, she jumped violently, rising to flee. I threw myself to the ground.

"Do not be afraid," I said quickly. "I was tracking the beast when you found a closer protector! It just passed me, its foot bleeding so profusely that it must be dying!"

"There is little hope of that!" she replied, trembling. "Do you not know whose beast this is?"

Suspicious thoughts stirred within me, but I feigned ignorance. "What has become of her champion?"

"What champion?" she answered, her voice tight. "I saw no one."

"Then how did the monster suffer?"

"I smashed her foot with a stone—hard as I could strike. Did you not hear her cry?"

"You are a brave woman!" I said. "I thought that cry belonged to you!"

"It was the leopardess."

"I have never heard such a sound from an animal's throat! It was the scream of a woman in torture!"

"My voice was gone; I could not have shrieked to save my baby! When I saw that horrid mouth at my darling's little white neck, I grabbed a stone and crushed her lame foot."

"Tell me of this creature," I urged. "I am a stranger here."

"You will learn soon enough if you intend to go to Bulika!" she said. "But I must never return there!"

"Yes, I am going to Bulika—to see the princess."

"Be careful; you had best not go!—But perhaps you are...! The princess is a very good, kind woman!"

I heard a slight rustle. Thick clouds had gathered over the moon, obscuring my companion; I feared she was preparing to run again.

"You are in no danger from me," I assured her. "What oath would you require?"

"I know by your speech that you are not of Bulika," she replied. "I will trust you—I am not of them, either, for if I were, I could not escape. They never trust anyone. I only wish I could see your face! But I like your voice!—There, my darling is asleep! The foul beast has not harmed her!—Yes, it was my baby she sought!" She continued, caressing the sleeping child. "And then she would have torn her mother apart for carrying her off! Some say the princess keeps two white leopardesses; I know only one—with spots. Everyone knows HER! If the princess hears of a baby, she sends her immediately to suck its blood, and then the child either dies or grows up an idiot. I tried to leave with my baby, but the princess was away, and I thought I might wait until I was stronger. But she must have taken the beast with her, returning home just as I fled. I heard the SNIFF-SNUFF of the leopardess behind me, and I ran—oh, how I ran!—But my darling will not die! There is no mark on her!"

"Where are you taking her?"

"Where no one ever tells!"

"Why is the princess so cruel?"

"There is an old prophecy that a child will be the death of her. That is why she refuses all offers of marriage, they say."

"But what will become of her country if she kills all the babies?"

"She cares not for her country. She sends witches to teach women spells that repel infants and force them to eat horrors. Some say she is allied with the Shadows to end the race entirely. At night, we hear the questing beast and lie awake, shivering. She can sense the house where a baby is coming and lies in wait at the door. There are words of power to drive her away, but they do not always work. And here I sit talking, and the beast may have reached home already, with her mistress sending another after us!"

As she finished, she rose in haste.

"I do not think she will ever return home," I said, rising with her. "Let me carry the baby for you!"

She offered no answer. When I reached for the child, she clutched it tighter.

"I cannot understand," I said, walking beside her, "how the brute could bleed so much!"

"Take my advice and stay far from the palace," she warned. "There are sounds within it at night as if the dead are trying to shriek but cannot open their mouths!"

She bid me an abrupt farewell. Clearly, she desired no further company. I stood still, listening until her footsteps faded completely into the grass.

The disorientation was absolute, a hollow ache in my chest that drove me forward in a blind, helpless sprint. Then, the scent hit me—not the metallic tang of iron, but the coppery richness of fresh life. I stumbled into the path of the leopardess, her paws wading in a torrent of crimson that rushed against my ankles with the force of a mountain stream. My instinct screamed that this was her blood, the life-force of the beast I had just witnessed, but a colder, sharper suspicion seized me. No creature, not even the leviathans of our ancient world, could bleed such a volume unless their entire circulatory system were shattered, draining fields and forests into a single, catastrophic gush.

I dipped a finger into the rushing red river. It was cool, clear, and utterly devoid of the viscosity of blood. It was water, a murmuring, channel-less rivulet flowing over the grass, singing a sweet, deceptive song. Yet, I did not drink. Thirst was a secondary concern to the mystery of its origin. I pressed on, guided by the sound, chasing the faint echo of the hot stream I had known before, hoping the light would return. The wetness of my feet had already stilled the tremors in my legs, and as the darkness began to thin, revealing the pale edge of dawn, I saw them: the wall-towers of a city, looming against the aurora, ancient as time itself.

I looked down to find the brook. It was gone. For some time, I had heard its rhythm fading, but now it was silence. Looking back, the grass lay bent in the direction of the flow, dotted with small, glimmering pools, but toward the city, there was no trace. The fountain had paused, or perhaps vanished entirely.

Beyond the walls lay gardens, teeming with vegetables I could not name, yet utterly barren of water, flowers, or animal life. They pressed right up against the fortifications, separated only by massive heaps of gravel and refuse dumped from the battlements. I approached the nearest gate; it hung half-open, rusted shut yet somehow unbarred, guarded by no sentinel. Beyond lay a long, ancient street, silent and empty. Was this a dead city? Doubt gnawed at me, so I turned and fled back out, toiling over dust-heaps and crossing several roads, refusing to re-enter until I knew the inhabitants were awake.

*What was I here for?* The question echoed in the quiet air. *What did I hope to find?*

I needed to see the woman I had brought to life. I did not crave her company; she had awakened frightful suspicions within me, making friendship, let alone love, a wild impossibility. Yet, her presence had altered me, forcing me to analyze a strange, magnetic influence she exerted. I wanted to penetrate the inscrutable nature of her being, to understand the marvels she represented. But I was too daring; a man should not seek knowledge by willingly courting temptation. Still, I had reinstated an evil force on the brink of extinction. I was accountable for whatever mischief might follow. I knew she was the enemy of children; the Little Ones were in danger. I had left them seeking light, and I had found a sliver of it. I needed more. I needed to know how to protect them.

At last, a faint stir broke the stillness. I moved through the next gate and down a narrow street of towering houses to a small square. I sat on the base of a pillar, where a hideous, bat-like creature perched atop the stone. Soon, citizens began to saunter past. I spoke to one, but he offered only a rude stare and a harsh word before moving on.

I rose and wandered through narrower streets, which gradually filled with idlers. I was not surprised to see no children. Near a gate, a group of young men cornered me. They reminded me of the bad giants I had faced before. They stared, pushed, hustled, and threw objects at me. I endured it, unwilling to provoke enmity where I sought refuge. I tried appealing to those who looked benevolent, but no one stopped to listen. I looked poor, and in Bulika, poverty was an offense punishable by scorn. Deformity and sickness were taxed, and the Princess's laws seemed designed to make poverty serve wealth.

Desperate, I took to my heels. No one pursued me beyond the gate, save for a lumbering fellow sitting by the entrance, eating a hunch of bread. In his stupid eagerness to throw a stone after me, he flung the bread instead. I snatched it; he dared not follow to reclaim it, for beyond the walls, everyone was a coward. I retreated a few hundred yards, collapsed in the grass, and ate. The bread fueled me, and the hot sunlight renewed my strength until sleep claimed me.

Night fell, and I woke to the moon shining down with a friendly, familiar brightness—the same moon that had watched me through the terrors of my first night in this strange world. A cold wind blew from the gate, carrying an evil odor, yet it did not chill me; the sun's warmth remained stored in my bones. I crept back into the city, joining the few souls crouched in corners to escape the shivering blast.

Walking slowly down a long, narrow street, a huge white shape bounded across my path, flashing in the moonlight before vanishing. Eager to catch a glimpse again, I turned down the next opening, which led into a wider thoroughfare. Immediately, I saw it on the opposite side, lurking in the shadow: the creature I had followed, trailing what I assumed was a man.

He glanced at the animal behind him every few moments but never spoke to it, never tried to drive it away. As he crossed a patch of moonlight, I saw the impossible truth: he cast no shadow. He was not a man at all, but a flat, superficial shadow of two dimensions. Yet, he was an opaque shadow, blocking light and rendering anything behind him invisible. In the shade, he was blacker than darkness; in the moonlight, he appeared as if he had drawn his shadow up around him, leaving no trace of depth beside or beneath him.

Following close at his heels was the leopardess, the white shadow of his blackness. She glided silently, dragging her own shadow—a stark black line—across the ground. When they passed from shadow into moonlight, the Shadow deepened into absolute void, while the animal flashed into blinding radiance. I walked abreast of them on the opposite side, my bare feet sounding on the flat stones. The leopardess never turned her head or twitched an ear. The Shadow seemed to look at me; for a split second, I lost his profile, seeing only a sharp, upright line.

That instant, the wind found me, blowing through me with icy force. I shuddered from head to toe, and my heart hammered against my ribs like a pebble rattling in a child's toy.

I veered sharply into a shadowed alley, seeking refuge beneath a crumbling stone arch. The moonlight spilled through the gap, illuminating the dust motes dancing in the air. Before I could settle, a figure glided from the darkness behind me—a woman, trembling violently, her eyes wide with terror as she peered out into the light. Moments later, a massive leopard, its white fur marbled with dark, jagged spots, surged across the archway like a living shadow. The woman pressed herself against my back, her breath hitching in a ragged gasp. My heart swelled with a sudden, fierce pity. I wrapped my arm around her shivering form.

"If that beast comes closer," I promised, my voice steady despite the chill, "I will hold it off. You must run."

"Thank you!" she whispered, her voice barely audible over the wind.

"Have you seen this creature before?" I asked gently.

"Many times," she replied, her teeth chattering. "She is the Princess's pet. If you lived here, you would know her name!"

"I am a stranger," I admitted. "But is she truly allowed to roam free?"

"She is caged, her jaws bound, her paws encased in crocodile leather gloves. Yet the chains are not enough. She escapes often, drinking the blood of any child foolish enough to cross her path. Fortunately, mothers are a rare commodity in Bulika."

Her composure shattered then, and she burst into sobs. "I wish I were home! The Princess returned only last night, and the leopardess is already out! How am I to enter my own house? She is hunting me, I know! She will be waiting at my door, watching... But I am a fool to speak to a stranger!"

"All strangers are not evil," I said firmly. "The beast cannot touch you until I have dealt with her, and by then, you will be inside. At least you have a roof over your head! This wind is terrible."

"Take me home safely, and I will offer you shelter from this storm," she pleaded. "But we must wait a moment."

As we waited, I questioned her about her people. She told me they spent their days digging for precious stones in their cellars, yet possessed everything else made for them in distant lands.

"Why?" I asked.

"Because labor is a disgrace," she answered, her eyes filling with shame. "Everyone in Bulika knows that!"

"How can they be rich if no one earns a coin?"

"Their ancestors saved for them," she explained. "They never spend. When funds are needed, they sell a few gems."

"But surely there must be the poor among you?"

"I suppose there must be," she conceded, though with a shrug of indifference. "But we never think of them. When someone falls into poverty, we simply forget them. That is how we maintain our wealth. We intend to remain rich forever."

"And when the mines are empty and the gems sold?" I pressed. "Then what?"

"We have so many, and so many more buried deep, that such a day will never come," she insisted.

"What if a foreign horde descended upon you, seizing all you possess?"

"No foreigner dares approach," she declared proudly. "They are terrified of our Princess. She is the shield that keeps us safe, free, and wealthy!"

Throughout her speech, she would pause, glancing nervously over her shoulder. When I asked why her people harbored such a visceral hatred of outsiders, she answered that the mere presence of a stranger defiled the city.

"How so?" I queried.

"Because we are more ancient and noble than any other nation," she said, her voice rising with conviction. "Therefore, we always expel strangers before nightfall."

"So how can you bring me into your home?" I asked, confused.

"I will make an exception for you," she replied quickly.

"Is there no sanctuary in the city for those without a home?"

"Such a place would be torn down, and its owner burned," she said coldly. "How can purity be preserved unless we keep the lowly at a proper distance? Dignity is a fragile thing!"

She spoke of a Princess who had reigned for millennia, wielding dominion over air, water, earth, and perhaps even fire, answerable to no one. Finally, willing to risk the journey, we navigated the winding, narrow passages of the city. We reached her door without encountering a single living soul. It stood in a broader street, flanked by towering houses, at the top of a steep, narrow staircase. She climbed slowly, and I followed.

Just as we neared the summit, she seized my arm, her fear suddenly manifest. She darted up the remaining steps with frantic speed. I arrived just in time to see the door slam shut in my face, leaving me standing confounded on the landing. There was merely enough space between the opposing doors of the two houses for a man to lie down. Weary and unwilling to let my presence further defile the sacred city of Bulika, I accepted the meager shelter, resting my head against the cold stone.

The moonlight spilled across the cobblestones at the foot of the stairs, illuminating a street choked by an unwholesome, inhospitable wind. Yet, within my retreat, the air remained still. As I composed myself for rest, my eyes snapped open. There, rising above the highest step, was the head of the shining creature I had witnessed trailing the Shadow. The instant our gazes met, she halted, then began to retreat, tail first. I lunged forward, but trapped against the wall, she executed a frantic backward somersault—head over tail—and scrambled down the stairs, vanishing into the gloom. I chased her to the street's edge, scanning the darkness, but she was gone. I returned to my hard couch, my mind racing.

Two evils prowled Bulika now: one spotted, one unspotted. Though I held little value for men or women in this city, the life of a child outweighed my own precarious existence. I resolved to guard the door until dawn.

Soon, the latch dragged slowly. I rose as the door creaked ajar. Standing there was not the woman I had aided, but the muffled figure from the desert. She said nothing, guiding me into an empty, stone-paved chamber and indicating a rug on the floor. Wrapped in its coarse weave, I lay down once more. She sealed the inner door, and the sound of the outer door closing echoed like a final verdict. Darkness pressed in, broken only by the pale moonlight filtering through cracks.

Sleep eluded me. A stifled moan drifted through the silence, growing into the cry of a child, which abruptly shattered into a terrible shriek. I sprang up and darted into the passage. From a side door emerged the white leopardess, clutching a newborn infant in her jaws, carrying it as if it were her own cub. I threw myself upon her, forcing her to release the baby. The infant hit the stone slabs with a piteous wail.

The muffled woman appeared instantly. She stepped over our tangled forms in the narrow corridor, scooped up the child, and vanished. Returning, she lifted me from the beast, opened the door, and pushed me gently out. The leopardess followed at my heels.

"She has failed me," I thought, anger flaring. "She has surrendered me to the beast to be settled with at her leisure! But we shall have a tussle for it!"

I ran down the stairs, fearing she would spring onto my back, but she moved quietly behind me. At the bottom, I turned to seize her, but she leaped over my head. When I spun to face her, she was crouching at my feet. I stooped, stroking her sleek white fur; she responded by licking my bare feet with a hard, dry tongue. I patted and fondled her, a well of tenderness swelling in my chest. She might be treacherous, yet if I rejected every display of affection lest it be feigned, how could I ever find the genuine love that must exist somewhere in every world?

She rose, standing beside me.

Suddenly, a bulky object thudded heavily into the middle of the street, a few yards away. I ran to it, discovering a pulpy mass, barely retaining the form of a woman. She must have been hurled from a neighboring window. I scanned the surroundings: the Shadow walked along the opposite side of the way, and the white leopardess trailed at his heel once more.

I followed, urging in my heart that the beast was likely not acting of her own free will. As I closed the distance, she turned and flew at me with a hideous snarl, forcing me to recoil. Instantly, she resumed her place behind the Shadow. I approached again; she attacked, her eyes blazing like live emeralds. A third time I tested her, and she snapped at me like a dog, biting my hand. My heart faltered, and I cried out. The creature paused, looking at me with a gaze that plainly asked, "Why WOULD you make me do this?"

Turning away, angry at my own foolishness, I realized I had wasted precious time since entering this place. It was night, yet I would go straight to the palace. From the square, I had seen it—a high fortress looming above the city's heart, circled by defenses that seemed more military than royal.

I found the fortifications much neglected, partly ruinous. For centuries, clearly, they had been ignored. Great gates stood open before a rocky chasm, with a drawbridge that looked unused, and it was hard to believe water had ever filled the hollow. All was so still that sleep seemed to permeate the structure, making the moonlight appear discordantly awake. I must enter like a thief or shatter a silence that made the mere thought of noise terrifying.

Like an outcast dog, I paced the walls until I reached a small recess with a stone bench. Taking refuge from the wind, I lay down and, despite the chill, fell fast asleep.

I was awakened by something leaping upon me, licking my face with the rough tongue of a feline. "It is the white leopardess!" I thought. "She has come to suck my blood! And why should she not? It would cost me more to defend than to yield!" I lay still, expecting pain. Instead, a pleasant warmth began to diffuse through me. Stretched at my back, she lay as close as possible, her body heat slowly penetrating mine, her breath—stripped of all wildness—swathing my head and face in a genial atmosphere. A full conviction that her intent was good took hold. I turned like a sleepy child, threw my arm over her, and sank into profound unconsciousness.

When I began to stir, I fancied I lay warm and soft in my own bed. "Is it possible I am at home?" I thought. The familiar scents of the garden seemed to crowd in. I rubbed my eyes and looked out: I lay on a bare stone, in the heart of a hateful city!

I sprang from the bench. Had I truly shared a bed with a leopardess, or had it been a dream? She had just left, for the warmth of her body lingered on my skin.

Leaving the recess, I carried a new hope, strong though shapeless. One thing was clear: I must find the princess. Surely I possessed some power over her, if not dominion. Had I not saved her life, and had she not prolonged it at the expense of my vitality? This reflection gave me the courage to face her, whatever she might be.

I circled the fortress until I reached the open gates, crossing the deep, ravine-like moat to stand upon a vast, paved courtyard. Towering trees, resembling poplars, stood at regular intervals, their canopies forming a green ceiling. In the center rose one tree, taller than the rest, its upper branches spreading wide to mimic the silhouette of a palm. Through the massive trunks, I caught glimpses of the palace—a structure alien yet suggestive of ancient India. It was long and low, crowned by lofty corner towers and a colossal central dome that pierced the roofline halfway to the turrets. The main entrance, a low arch resembling a flattened ellipse, stood wide open. Unchallenged, I stepped into a great hall shaped like a long ellipse.

To one side sat a cage housing a massive leopardess. Chained by a steel collar, her muzzle and paws bound, she lay coiled with her head resting on her paws. Her coat was white, marked with dark ovals, and her eyes were wide, featuring canoe-shaped pupils and great green irises. She stared at me, yet not a muscle moved—no blink, no twitch of a whisker, no shift of her tail, which stretched rigid behind her like iron. She appeared suspended between life and death.

Two low passages branched from this vestibule. I chose one, which quickly forked into a labyrinth of narrow, irregular corridors. At a bottleneck barely wide enough for two, a page collided with me. He recoiled in terror, then, upon recognizing me, his fear curdled into impudence.

"To see the princess?" he asked, puffing out his chest.

"A likely thing!" he scoffed. "I haven't seen her Highness this morning!"

I seized him by the back of the neck, shaking him until he gasped. "Take me to her immediately, or I will drag you until I find her. Let her know how her servants receive her guests."

He glanced at me, then began to lead me blindly, pulling me toward a large kitchen. Servants moved there with feebly sluggish energy, hardly awake. I expected them to swarm and eject me, but instead, they froze, their eyes widening not at me, but at something behind me. Their faces grew ghastly as they stared. Turning, I saw the white leopardess regarding them with an intensity that would have shattered stouter hearts.

Soon, one servant recovered his composure. I released the boy.

"Take me to the princess," I commanded.

"She has not yet left her room, your lordship," he replied.

"Let her know I am here, waiting for an audience."

"Will your lordship give me your name?"

"Tell her that one who knows the white leech desires to see her."

"She will kill me if I deliver such a message. I dare not."

"You refuse?"

He cast a wary glance at the beast and fled. The remaining servants continued to stare, paralyzed by fear of the animal. I turned to the graceful creature, who now stood with her muzzle dropped to my heel. She was a warm splendor in the gloom, white as milk. I stooped to pat her; she looked up, and the mere movement of her head scattered the servants in panic. Rising on her hind legs, she placed her paws on my shoulders. I embraced her, but she pricked her ears, broke free, and vanished in an instant.

The page I had sent earlier returned. "Please, come this way, my lord."

My heart hammered, bracing itself for the encounter. I followed him through endless passages until we entered a room so vast and dark that the walls were invisible. A single spot on the floor reflected a faint light, while everything else was absolute black. Looking up, I saw an oval aperture high in the roof, framed by the joints of black marble blocks. The floor was paved with similar slabs, and the elliptical walls absorbed the scant light. The roof formed the long half of an ellipsoid, with the opening positioned over one of the ellipse's foci. For a fleeting moment, I thought I saw reddish lines, but they vanished before I could examine them.

Suddenly, a radiant form emerged from the center of the darkness, flashing with brilliance. She wore a robe of soft white, her hair cascading like a cataract of black against the marble. Her eyes were a luminous void, her arms and feet like warm ivory. She greeted me with the innocent smile of a girl, her figure suggesting she had only just crossed the threshold of womanhood.

*Alas,* I thought, *I miscalculated my danger! Can this be the woman I rescued—the one who struck me, scorned me, and abandoned me?* I stood gazing into the dark; she gazed back, searching.

She disappeared. *She will not acknowledge me!* I thought. But instantly, her eyes flashed from the shadows straight into mine. She had seen me and come to me!

"You have found me at last!" she said, placing her hand on my shoulder. "I knew you would!"

My body quivered with conflicting sensations, a storm of attraction and repulsion I lacked the power to analyze.

"You shiver!" she observed. "This place is cold for you! Come."

Silent, struck dumb by her beauty and sweetness, I allowed her to lead me by the hand to the spot of light. She stood there for an instant, then spoke.

"You have grown brown since last I saw you," she noted.

"This is almost the first roof I have been under since you left me," I replied.

"Whose was the other?" she asked.

"I do not know the woman's name."

"I would gladly learn it! The instinct of hospitality is not strong in my people!" She took my hand again, leading me through the darkness to a curtain of black fabric. Beyond it lay a white staircase, which she ascended to a beautiful chamber.

"How you must miss the hot flowing river!" she said. "But there is a bath in the corner, free of white leeches! At the foot of your couch lies a garment. When you descend, I shall be in the room to your left, at the foot of the stairs."

As she departed, I accused my own presumption. How could I treat this lovely woman as an entity of evil when she behaved like a sister? Where had the marvelous change occurred? She had struck me before; now she embraced me. She had reviled me; now she claimed she knew I would follow! Did she understand my doubts? Could she explain everything? Could I believe her if she did? Regarding her hospitality, I had surely earned it and might accept it until I formed a final judgment.

Could such beauty and such wickedness coexist in one person? If so, how? Unable to answer the first question, I deferred the second.

Clear as crystal, the water in the great white bath sent a sparkling flash from the corner where it sank into the marble floor, inviting my embrace. Save for the hot streams in the cottage of the veiled woman and the pools in the track of the wounded leopardess, I had not seen water since leaving home. It looked celestial. I plunged in.

Immediately, my brain filled with a strange, delicate odor that I did not entirely like. Doubt returned: had she medicated it? Enchanted it? Was she working on me unlawfully? How could there be water in the palace while the city remained dry? I remembered the crushed paw of the leopardess and sprang from the bath.

What had I been bathing in? Again I saw the fleeing mother, heard the howl, saw the limping beast. But what mattered the source? Was not the water sweet? Was it not the very water the pitcher-plant secreted from its heart for the weary traveler? Water came from heaven; what mattered the well or the spring? Yet, I did not re-enter.

I donned the white wool robe, embroidered on the neck and hem, that lay ready for me, and descended the stairs to the room my hostess indicated. It was round, constructed entirely of alabaster, with no windows. Light permeated everywhere, a soft, pearly shimmer rather than a harsh shine. Vague, shadowy forms drifted over the walls and low dome, like loose rain-clouds over a grey-blue sky.

The princess waited, wearing a robe embroidered with argent rings, discs, rectangles, and lozenges, pressed close together like silver mail. It fell unbroken from her neck, hiding her feet, though its long, open sleeves left her arms bare.

On an ivory table stood cakes and fruit, an ivory jug of milk, a crystal jug of pale rose-colored wine, and a white loaf.

"Here we do not kill to eat," she said. "But I think you will like what I can give you."

I told her I desired nothing better. She seated herself on a couch by the table and gestured for me to join her. She poured a bowl of milk, handed me the loaf, and broke off a piece. Then she filled two silver goblets of grotesquely graceful workmanship from the wine jug.

"You have never drunk wine like this!" she declared.

I drank and marveled; every flower of Hybla and Hymettus seemed to have sent its ghost to swell the soul of that wine.

"And now that you will be able to listen," she continued, "I must do what I can to make myself intelligible. Our natures differ so greatly that this may not be easy. Men and women live but to die; we, those of my kind, live to live on. Old age is a horror to you; to me, it is a dear desire. The older we grow, the nearer we approach perfection. Your perfection is poor, coming soon and lasting briefly; ours is a ceaseless ripening. I am not yet ripe, having lived thousands of your years—how many, I never cared to note. The everlasting cannot be measured."

"Many lovers have sought me," she went on. "I have loved none of them. They sought only to enslave me, as the men of my city seek gems of price. When you found me, I found a man! I put you to the test; you stood it. Your love was genuine—but far from ideal. You loved me truly, but not with true love. Pity is not love. What woman would return love for pity? Such love as yours was then is hateful to me. I knew that if you saw me as I am, you would love me—to have and to hold. I wanted none of that! I desired a love that outlived hopelessness, outmeasured indifference, hate, and scorn. Therefore, I adopted cruelty, despite, and ingratitude. When I left you, I showed myself such that you could no longer follow from pity; I was no longer in need of you. But you must satisfy my desire or set me free—prove yourself priceless or worthless! To satisfy the hunger of my love, you must follow me, seeking nothing—not gratitude, not even pity. Follow and find me, and be content with merest presence, with scantest forbearance. I, not you, have failed; I yield the contest."

She looked at me tenderly, hiding her face in her hands. Yet I caught a flash and a sparkle behind the tenderness and did not believe her. She laid herself out to secure and enslave me; she only fascinated me!

"Beautiful princess," I said, "let me understand how you came to be found in such evil plight."

"There are things I cannot explain," she replied, "until you become capable of understanding them—which can only happen when love grows perfect. Many things are hidden from you that you cannot even wish to know; but any question you can ask, I can answer in some measure."

"I had set out to visit a part of my dominions occupied by a savage dwarf-people, strong and fierce, enemies to law and order, opposed to every kind of progress—an evil race. I went alone, fearing nothing, unaware of the least necessity for precaution. I did not know that upon the hot stream beside which you found me, a certain woman, by no means so powerful as myself, not being immortal, had cast what you call a spell—which is merely the setting in motion of a force as natural as any other, but operating primarily in a region beyond the ken of the mortal who uses it."

"I set out on my journey, reached the stream, bounded across it..." A shadow of embarrassment darkened her cheek. I understood but showed no sign. Checked for a moment, she continued. "...you know what a step it is in parts! But in the very act, an indescribable cold invaded me. I recognized the nature of the assault at once and knew it could affect me only temporarily. By sheer force of will I dragged myself to the wood—and knew nothing more until I saw you asleep and the horrible worm at your neck. I crept out, dragged the monster from you, and laid my lips to the wound. You began to wake; I buried myself among the leaves."

She rose, her eyes flashing as never human eyes had flashed, and threw her arms high over her head.

"What you have made of me is yours!" she cried. "I will repay you as never yet did woman! My power, my beauty, my love are your own: take them."

She dropped to her knees beside me, laying her arms across my knees and looking up into my face. Then, for the first time, I noted a large, clumsy glove on her left hand. In my mind's eye, I saw hair and claws beneath it, knowing it was a hand shut tight—perhaps badly bruised. The other hand was lovely as any could be, and I felt that if I did less than loathe her, I should love her. Refusing to dally with usurping emotions, I turned my eyes aside.

She started to her feet. I sat motionless, looking down.

"To me she may be true!" my vanity whispered. For a moment, I was tempted to love a lie.

An odor, rather than the gentlest of airy pulses, fanned me. I glanced up. She stood erect before me, waving her lovely arms in seemingly mystic fashion.

A frightful roar made my heart rebound against the walls of its cage. The alabaster trembled as if it would shatter into pieces.

I collapsed onto the bed, my mind racing through the tale she had spun. In her haste, she had betrayed herself with a single careless word, unraveling the mystery of my discovery in the forest! The leopardess had leaped away; the princess lay defeated on the riverbank; the flowing water had shattered her spell. Her own confession laid bare the peril facing the Little Ones, and I realized with a jolt that I had just saved the life of their most terrifying foe.

I had barely reached this conclusion when sleep dragged me under. The exquisite wine may not have been entirely harmless.

When I awoke, it was night. A lamp hanging from the ceiling cast a soft, ethereal glow across the chamber. A profound, delicious lethargy wrapped around me. I felt as though I were floating, untethered from land, drifting on the bosom of a twilight sea. Existence itself was pure pleasure. There was no pain. Surely, I was dying.

No pain—ah, what a sudden, sharp sting of mortal agony that was! What a sickening blow! It pierced straight through my heart! Again! That was pure sharpness—and so nauseating! I tried to raise my hand to press against my chest, but something held it down.

The pain was fading, yet my entire body felt paralyzed. Some malevolent force had seized me—something hateful! I wanted to struggle, but my limbs refused to obey. My will screamed in protest, futile and trapped. I surrendered, lying still. Then, I felt a soft hand on my face, guiding my head back into the pillow, and a heavy weight settled across my torso.

My breathing deepened; the weight lifted from my chest. I opened my eyes.

The princess stood above me on the bed, gazing out into the room with the vacant expression of a dreamer. Her great eyes were clear and calm. Her lips bore a look of satisfied passion; she wiped a streak of red from her mouth.

She caught my gaze, leaned down, and struck me across the eyes with the handkerchief in her grasp. It felt like the edge of a blade slicing through my vision, and for a moment or two, I was blind.

I heard a dull, heavy thud, like a large, soft-footed beast landing after a short leap. I opened my eyes and saw the great sweep of a long tail as it vanished through the half-open doorway. I lunged after it.

The creature was gone. I shot down the stairs and into the alabaster hall. The moon hung high, bathing the place in a pale, sun-bleached radiance. The princess was nowhere to be seen. I had to find her; in her presence, I might survive, but outside it, I was helpless! I was a tame animal for her to feed upon, a human spring for a thirst demon! She showed me favor only to use me more easily! My waking eyes did not fear her, but they would close, and she would return! Without seeing her, I felt her presence everywhere, for she could be anywhere—even waiting for me in some hidden cavern of sleep! Only with my eyes fixed on her could I feel safe from her!

Beyond the alabaster hall, it was pitch-black. I had to grope my way forward, hands and feet feeling for purchase. Finally, I brushed aside a curtain and entered a black hall. There, I found a great, silent assembly. How it was visible, I could not say or imagine, for the walls, floor, and ceiling were shrouded in an infinite blackness, darker than the deepest moonless night. Yet my eyes managed to separate, however vaguely, a few individuals from the mass. They interpenetrated and divided, surrounded by the darkness. It seemed my eyes would never fully recover. I pressed my eyeballs and stared again, but the vision remained indistinct. Blackness mingled with form; silence and undefined motion possessed the vast space. It was a dim, confused dance, filled with recurring glimpses of shapes not unknown to me. Now a woman appeared, with glorious eyes staring out of a skull; now an armed figure mounted on a skeleton horse; now one, then another of the hideous burrowing phantasms. I could trace no order or relation in the mingling currents and eddies. If I seemed to catch the rhythm of a dance, it was only to see it break into chaos. With the shifting colors of the seemingly solid shapes, a multitude of shadows mingled, appearing independent of their originals, each moving according to its own free shadow-will. I searched everywhere for the princess, but throughout the wildly changing kaleidoscope, I saw her not, nor found any sign of her presence. Where was she? What might she not be doing? No one paid me the slightest notice as I wandered hither and thither seeking her. Losing hope, I turned away to look elsewhere. Finding the wall, I kept my hand against it, for even then I could not see it clearly, and groped along until I reached a curtained opening into a vestibule.

Dimly lit by moonlight, the leopardess's cage served as the arena for what appeared to be a desperate, silent struggle. Two vastly different forms, human and bestial, with a tangled confusion of mingling bodies and limbs, writhed and wrestled in closest embrace. It had lasted only an instant when I saw the leopardess emerge from the cage, walking quietly toward the open door. As I hurried after her, I glanced back: there was the leopardess in the cage, crouching motionless as when I first saw her.

The moon, halfway up the sky, shone round and clear. The bodiless shadow I had seen the night before walked through the trees toward the gate, and after him went the leopardess, swinging her tail. I followed a little way behind, as silently as they, and neither of them once looked back. Through the open gate, we descended to the city, lying quiet beneath the moonshine. The face of the moon was very still, and its stillness looked like that of expectation.

The Shadow took his way straight to the stair at the top of which I had lain the night before. Without pausing, he ascended, and the leopardess followed. I quickened my pace, but a moment later, I heard a cry of horror. Then came the fall of something soft and heavy between me and the stair, and at my feet lay a body, frightfully blackened and crushed, yet still recognizable as that of the woman who had led me home and shut me out. Standing petrified, I watched as the spotted leopardess bounded down the stairs with a baby clamped in her mouth. I darted forward to seize her before she could turn at the foot, but that very instant, from behind me, the white leopardess, like a great bar of glowing silver, shot through the moonlight and grabbed her by the neck. She dropped the child; I caught it up and stood to watch the battle between them.

What a sight it was! Now one, now the other uppermost, both too intent for any noise beyond a low growl, a whimpered cry, or a snarl of hate—followed by a quicker scrambling of claws as each, worrying and pushing and dragging, struggled for footing on the pavement! The spotted leopardess was larger than the white one, and I was anxious for my friend; but soon I saw that, though neither stronger nor more active, the white leopardess possessed greater endurance. Not once did she lose her hold on the neck of her opponent. From the spotted throat finally issued a howl of agony, changing, by swift-crowded gradations, into the long-drawn crescendo of a woman's uttermost wail. The white one relaxed her jaws; the spotted one drew herself away and rose on her hind legs.

Erect in the moonlight stood the princess, a confused rush of shadows careering over her whiteness—the spots of the leopard crowding, hurrying, fleeing to the refuge of her eyes, where merging they vanished. The last few, outsped and belated, mingled with the cloud of her streamy hair, leaving her radiant as the moon when a legion of little vapors has flown, wind-hunted, off her silvery disc—save that, adown the white column of her throat, a thread of blood still trickled from every wound of her adversary's terrible teeth. She turned away, took a few steps with the gait of a Hecate, fell, covered afresh with her spots, and fled at a long, stretching gallop.

The white leopardess turned also, sprang upon me, pulled my arms asunder, caught the baby as it fell, and flew with it along the street toward the gate.

The spotted leopardess tore up the brow of the hill, a blur of motion against the night, leaving me only a fleeting glimpse of her retreat toward the palace gates. By the time I reached the entrance-hall, the princess was hastily draping a robe around her shoulders, the fabric catching the wind that had already stilled the flow of blood from her wounds, leaving them dark and crusted.

When our eyes met, a flash of irritation crossed her features before she forced a brittle smile. "A small accident," she said, her voice tight. "Hearing the cat-woman was back in the city, I went to drive her away. But she brought one of her horrid creatures. It sprang upon me, claws finding my neck before I could strike!"

She shivered, and despite knowing the truth—that her injuries were real, yet her story a fabrication born of fear—I felt a pang of guilt. I had let her fight alone. My expression must have betrayed my pity.

"Child of folly!" she snapped, though the anger lacked heat. "Do not cry. Wait here; I must enter the black hall briefly. Fetch me something for these scratches."
