I followed her, though a primal instinct warned me to stay back. The moment she crossed the threshold, the air filled with a low, buzzing hum, as if a thousand voices whispered in unison. Light began to shift across the vast assembly, traveling like a ripple from spot to spot. Groups of figures would flare into brilliance—illuminating the skull-headed dancers in the forest-hall where the princess had once watched—and then sink back into shadow, only to reveal the chaos of the Evil Wood where she had urged the fight. Yet, through this kaleidoscope of memory and presence, I saw her now: standing motionless, head bowed, while the light settled finally on the hollow in the heath. There, she walked in vain circles, trying to wrap the elusive vapour around her.

Then, the old librarian stepped forward. He regarded her for a heartbeat, and suddenly, she collapsed. Her limbs fled her body, and her form dissolved into nothingness.

A wild shriek tore through the echoing space. As her eidolon fell, the princess herself, who had stood like a statue before me, crumpled to the ground, lifeless. I turned and fled immediately. Not again would I seek to restore her. Trembling beside the cage, I realized with a jolt of horror that within the black ellipsoid, I had been inside the princess's own mind. I saw the tail of the leopardess quiver once more in the periphery of that fractured vision.

Before I could fully compose myself, her voice cut through the silence beside me. "Come now," she commanded, her tone shifting instantly from despair to command. "I will show you what I need."

She led me into the court. The moon hung near the zenith, its silver radiance outshining the absent gold of the sun. She guided me through the trees to the tallest one, the central sentinel. Its branches drew together at the apex, forming a cluster that resembled a fir-cone from below. Standing beneath it, she gazed upward, speaking to the darkness.

"On the summit of that tree grows a tiny blossom that would heal my scratches instantly," she murmured. "I could be a dove and fetch it, but there is a little snake in the leaves. Its bite would be worse to a dove than a tiger's bite to me. How I hate that cat-woman!"

She turned to me, her face softening into one of her sweetest smiles. "Can you climb?"

The smile vanished as quickly as it came, replaced by a look of profound suffering. I should have left her to her pain, but the way she pressed her hand to her wounded neck struck a chord deep within me.

I studied the tree. From base to branch, the stem was lined with projections, like the remnants of fallen leaves on a palm.

"I can climb that tree," I answered.

"Not with bare feet!" she insisted.

In my haste to pursue the leopardess, I had forgotten my sandals in my room.

"It is no matter," I said. "I have long gone barefoot."

I looked up again, my eyes tracing the rugged bole until they lost themselves in the canopy. The moonlight dappled the trunk like silvery foam, and a faint breeze rustled the tops with a sound like water falling softly into water. I stepped forward to begin my ascent, but she stopped me.

"I cannot let you attempt it with your feet bare!" she argued. "A fall from that height would kill you!"

"So would a bite from the snake!" I retorted, admitting I did not truly believe a snake existed.

"It would not hurt *you*!" she replied sharply. "Wait a moment."

She tore from her garment the two wide borders that met in the front. Kneeling on one knee, she bound my left foot, then my right, securing them with thick, embroidered strips.

"You have left the ends hanging, princess," I noted.

"I have nothing to cut them with," she said, tying the knots tight. "But they are not long enough to get entangled."

I turned to the tree and began to climb.

In Bulika, the cold after sundown was mild compared to other regions, especially near the sexton's cottage. Yet, as I ascended, the chill intensified. A little way up, I felt the cold; higher still, it bit deeper; and among the upper branches, it became absolute. I shivered violently, feeling as though my hands and feet had vanished entirely.

There was hardly any wind, and the branches remained rigid, yet as I neared the summit, a peculiar unsteadiness seized me. Every limb I touched seemed poised to give way. When my head rose above the foliage into the open moonlight, searching for the blossom, I was suddenly drenched from head to foot. The next instant, as if plunged into a violent storm, I was flung about wildly. I felt myself sinking, tossed up and down, rolled over and over, checked and spun again, descending lower and lower. Gasping, gurgling, choking, I crashed onto a solid bottom.

"I told you so!" a voice croaked in my ear.

The dawn's pale light caught the iridescent sheen of the raven's feathers as he perched on the rim of the massive stone basin. I lay submerged in the fountain's water, my father's creation, watching the steel stalk above shoot a hundred-foot torrent into the sky before bursting into foam. My face broke the surface, gasping, as the bird looked down with an expression that felt less like avian curiosity and more like judgment.

"You told me nothing!" I croaked, my voice rough from the cold.

"I instructed you to ignore anyone you distrusted," the raven replied, his tone sharp despite the morning chill. "How could a mortal retain such a directive?"

"The consequence of forgetting is what you must not forget," Mr. Raven said, stepping closer until his shadow fell over the water. He reached down a hand that shimmered with unnatural solidity.

I grasped it, and suddenly the sensation of drowning vanished, replaced by the solid ground of the lawn. I stood dripping, water streaming from my robes.

"Change immediately," he commanded, turning toward the house with a stately stride. "A wetting reveals little of your origin, though here it is merely inconvenient."

As he walked, shifting back into his full form, I laughed, shaking my head. "I have little to change. I discarded my robe to climb the tree."

"It has been ages since I molted," the raven noted dryly.

Inside, the house was silent. I found a dressing gown and descended to the library. The librarian emerged from a closet just as I collapsed onto a couch. Mr. Raven pulled a chair close, sitting without a word for a moment before speaking.

"What does it all mean?" I asked, staring at the ceiling.

"A profound inquiry," he answered. "No one truly knows the nature of things; we only grasp their meaning through application. Whether one learns depends entirely on how they utilize what they possess."

"I have applied nothing yet."

"Not much, but you acknowledge the void. Most spend a lifetime realizing they have learned nothing and achieved less. Yet, you possessed the desire to be useful."

"I wanted to help the children—the Little Ones."

"You started down the wrong path."

"I didn't know the right one."

"That is true, but you are culpable for your ignorance."

"I will accept your explanation once I understand it."

"If you had accepted our invitation, you would have known the path. When a man refuses to act where he stands, he wanders far only to find nothing."

"I have wandered far and found nothing. I left the children to learn how to serve them, only to discover the peril they face."

"When you were with them, you were in the position to aid them. You abandoned your post to search for a job that required you to leave."

"Did you mean I could have helped them by staying?"

"Could you teach them anything by abandoning them?"

"No, but how was I to begin? They were far ahead of me!"

"They were not measuring themselves against you as a ruler. If they possessed your knowledge, or even what you should have known, they would be leagues ahead. But you observed they were not growing—or growing so sluggishly that the concept of growth was foreign to them. They feared growing. Have you ever witnessed a child remain static?"

"But I lacked the power to force their growth!"

"You could have removed the obstacles preventing it."

"What obstacles? I suspected a lack of water."

"Exactly. They have no tears to cry with."

"I would have prevented the need for such tears."

"That is the hallmark of foolish philanthropy. Without the weeping of your world, there would be no cause to save it. You admitted you thought they needed water; why did you not dig wells?"

"It never crossed my mind."

"Not even when the sounds of subterranean waters filled your ears?"

"I believed it once. But I feared the giants for them. That fear drove me to endure so much abuse from those beasts myself."

"In doing so, you nearly conditioned the noble creatures to fear the very 'Bags' who oppressed them. While they fed, comforted, and worshipped you, you allowed yourself to be enslaved by bestial men. You gave the darlings a coward to call their hero. A greater betrayal is hard to imagine. They offered you their hearts; you owe them your soul. You could have turned the giants into servants for the Little Ones."

"I fear your words hold truth, Mr. Raven. But I believed more knowledge might harm them, stripping away their innocence and loveliness."

"They gave you no cause for such fear."

"Is not a little knowledge dangerous?"

"One of your world's greatest lies. Is humanity's greatest wisdom more dangerous than a fragment? The notion that knowledge is inherently grand makes any degree of it seem perilous compared to ignorance. Knowing everything is not greatness."

"At least I served the giants out of love, not cowardice."

"Granted. But you should have served the Little Ones, not the giants. You should have given them water; then they would have quickly shown the giants their true place. Meanwhile, you could have ordered the giants to clear two-thirds of their coarse fruit trees to make room for the delicate ones. You missed your chance with the Lovers, Mr. Vane. You speculated about them rather than helping them."

Silence and shame settled heavy upon me. His words struck true: I had failed the Little Ones, failing them as neighbors and as guardians.

Mr. Raven spoke again, his voice steady despite the weight of his judgment. "You wronged them doubly," he said. "To them, slavery would have been a step forward. A few lessons, administered with a stick from their own trees, would have been a gift beyond measure."

"I did not know they were cowards!" I protested, the denial rising hot in my throat.

"What difference does that make?" he countered, his gaze piercing. "The man who justifies his actions on another's cowardice is, in essence, a coward himself. I fear worse is coming. By now, the Little Ones might have learned to shield themselves from the Princess, or even the Giants—they were always capable of such defense. As it stands, they laughed at them. But now, through your connection with her..."

"I hate her!" I cried out, the emotion tearing from me.

"Did you let her know?"

I fell silent again, the admission hanging between us like a storm cloud.

"Not even to her have you been faithful," Mr. Raven observed softly. "But hush! We are being followed from the fountain. I fear it."

"No living creature did I see," I whispered, scanning the shadows. "Except a disreputable cat that bolted into the shrubbery."

"It was a magnificent Persian," he corrected, his tone sharpening. "Though wet and draggled, making her look worse than disreputable."

"What do you mean?" I gasped, a fresh terror seizing my chest. "There was a beautiful blue Persian about the house, but she fled at the mere sound of water! Could she have been after the goldfish?"

"We shall see," the librarian replied. "I know much of cats, of various breeds. There is something in this room that will unmask her, or I am mistaken."

He rose, moved to the door of the small closet, and retrieved the mutilated volume. Sitting beside me again, he held up the book. It was whole, entire, and sound.

"Where was the other half?" I gasped, my breath catching.

"Sticking through into my library," he answered simply.

I held my peace. To ask further would be to dive into a bottomless sea, and there might be no time left.

"Listen," he commanded. "I intend to read a stanza or two. There is someone present who will find this reading most unpleasant."

He opened the vellum cover, turning a few leaves. The parchment was discolored by age, one leaf bearing a dark stain covering two-thirds of its surface. He turned this page slowly, searching for a specific passage within what appeared to be a continuous poem. About the middle of the book, he began to read.

What follows is not the text itself, but the impression it left upon my mind. The poem seemed written in a language I had never heard, yet I understood it perfectly, unable to write the words or define their meaning save in poor approximation. These fragments are the shapes those words took as they passed through my brain:

"But if I found a man that could believe
In what he saw not, felt not, and yet knew,
From him I should take substance, and receive
Firmness and form relate to touch and view;
Then should I clothe me in the likeness true
Of that idea where his soul did cleave!"

He turned a leaf and read again:

"In me was every woman. I had power
Over the soul of every living man,
Such as no woman ever had in dower—
Could what no woman ever could, or can;
All women, I, the woman, still outran,
Outsoared, outsank, outreigned, in hall or bower.

"For I, though me he neither saw nor heard,
Nor with his hand could touch finger of mine,
Although not once my breath had ever stirred
A hair of him, could trammel brain and spine
With rooted bonds which Death could not untwine—
Or life, though hope were evermore deferred."

He paused, turned another leaf, and began anew:

"For by his side I lay, a bodiless thing;
I breathed not, saw not, felt not, only thought,
And made him love me—with a hungering
After he knew not what—if it was aught
Or but a nameless something that was wrought
By him out of himself; for I did sing

'A song that had no sound into his soul;
I lay a heartless thing against his heart,
Giving him nothing where he gave his whole
Being to clothe me human, every part:
That I at last into his sense might dart,
Thus first into his living mind I stole.

'Ah, who was ever conquering Love but I!
Who else did ever throne in heart of man!
To visible being, with a gladsome cry
Waking, life's tremor through me throbbing ran!'"

A strange, repulsive feline wail echoed from somewhere in the room. I sat up on my elbow, staring into the darkness, seeing nothing.

Mr. Raven turned several leaves and continued:

"Sudden I woke, nor knew the ghastly fear
That held me—not like serpent coiled about,
But like a vapour moist, corrupt, and drear,
Filling heart, soul, and breast and brain throughout;
My being lay motionless in sickening doubt,
Nor dared to ask how came the horror here.

"My past entire I knew, but not my now;
I understood nor what I was, nor where;
I knew what I had been: still on my brow
I felt the touch of what no more was there!
I was a fainting, dead, yet live Despair;
A life that flouted life with mop and mow!

"That I was a queen I knew right well,
And sometimes wore a splendour on my head
Whose flashing even dead darkness could not quell—
The like on neck and arms and girdle-stead;
And men declared a light my closed eyes shed
That killed the diamond in its silver cell."

The ugly cry of feline pain pierced the air once more. I looked again, finding neither shape nor motion. Mr. Raven seemed to listen for a moment, then turned several pages and resumed:

"Hideously wet, my hair of golden hue
Fouled my fair hands: to have it swiftly shorn
I had given my rubies, all for me dug new—
No eyes had seen, and such no waist had worn!
For a draught of water from a drinking horn,
For one blue breath, I had given my sapphires blue!

'Nay, I had given my opals for a smock,
A peasant-maiden's garment, coarse and clean:
My shroud was rotting! Once I heard a cock
Lustily crow upon the hillock green
Over my coffin. Dulled by space between,
Came back an answer like a ghostly mock.'"

Once more, the bestial wail arose.

"I thought some foul thing was in the room!" the librarian exclaimed, casting a glance around. Instantly, however, he turned a leaf or two and read again:

"For I had bathed in milk and honey-dew,
In rain from roses shook, that ne'er touched earth,
And ointed me with nard of amber hue;
Never had spot me spotted from my birth,
Or mole, or scar of hurt, or fret of dearth;
Never one hair superfluous on me grew.

"Fleeing cold whiteness, I would sit alone—
Not in the sun—I feared his bronzing light,
But in his radiance back around me thrown
By fulgent mirrors tempering his might;
Thus bathing in a moon-bath not too bright,
My skin I tinted slow to ivory tone.

"But now, all round was dark, dark all within!
My eyes not even gave out a phantom-flash;
My fingers sank in pulp through pulpy skin;
My body lay death-weltered in a mash
Of slimy horrors—"

With a fearsome yell, a huge white cat rushed from somewhere in the room. Its clammy fur stared in clumps, its tail thick as a cable, its eyes flashing green as chrysoprase, its distended claws entangling itself so that it floundered across the carpet. It made straight for the chimney. Quick as thought, the librarian threw the manuscript between the beast and the hearth.

The cat crouched instantly, its eyes fixed on the book. Yet his voice continued as if he were still reading, his gaze seemingly locked onto the pages rather than the creature:

"Ah, the two worlds! so strangely are they one,
And yet so measurelessly wide apart!
Oh, had I lived the bodiless alone
And from defiling sense held safe my heart,
Then had I scaped the canker and the smart,
Scaped life-in-death, scaped misery's endless moan!"

At these words, a howling, a prolonged yell of agony burst from the cat, forcing us both to cover our ears. When it ceased, Mr. Raven walked to the fireplace, took up the book, and, standing between the creature and the chimney, pointed his finger at her for a moment. She lay perfectly still. He took a half-burnt stick from the hearth, drew a strange sign on the floor, placed the manuscript back in its spot with a look suggesting, "Now we have her, I think!" and, returning to the cat, stood over her in a still, solemn voice:

"Lilith, when you came here on the way to your evil will, you little thought into whose hands you were delivering yourself! Mr. Vane, when God created me—not out of Nothing, as the unwise say, but out of His own endless glory—He brought me an angelic splendour to be my wife; there she lies! For her first thought was POWER; she counted it slavery to be one with me, and bear children for Him who gave her being. One child, indeed, she bore; then, puffed with the fancy that she had created her, would have me fall down and worship her! Finding, however, that I would but love and honour, never obey and worship her, she poured out her blood to escape me, fled to the army of the aliens, and soon had so ensnared the heart of the great Shadow, that he became her slave, wrought her will, and made her queen of Hell. How it is with her now, she best knows, but I know also. The one child of her body she fears and hates, and would kill, asserting a right, which is a lie, over what God sent through her into His new world. Of creating, she knows no more than the crystal that takes its allotted shape, or the worm that makes two worms when it is cloven asunder. Vilest of God's creatures, she lives by the blood and lives and souls of men. She consumes and slays, but is powerless to destroy as to create."

The animal lay motionless, its beryl eyes fixed flaming on the man; his eyes on hers held them fixed so they could not move.

"Then God gave me another wife—not an angel but a woman—who is to this as light is to darkness."

The cat gave a horrible screech and began to grow larger. It went on growing and growing until the spotted leopardess uttered a roar that made the house tremble. I sprang to my feet. I do not think Mr. Raven started even with his eyelids.

"It is but her jealousy that speaks," he said calmly. "Jealousy self-kindled, foiled and fruitless; for here I am, her master now whom she would not have for her husband! While my beautiful Eve yet lives, hoping immortally! Her hated daughter lives also, but beyond her evil ken, one day to be what she counts her destruction—for even Lilith shall be saved by her childbearing. Meanwhile she exults that my human wife plunged herself and me in despair, and has borne me a countless race of miseries; but my Eve repented, and is now beautiful as never was woman or angel, while her groaning, travailing world is the nursery of our Father's children. I too have repented, and am blessed. Thou, Lilith, hast not yet repented; but thou must. Tell me, is the great Shadow beautiful? Knowest thou how long thou wilt thyself remain beautiful? Answer me, if thou knowest."

Then I understood that Mr. Raven was indeed Adam, the old and the new man; and that his wife, ministering in the house of the dead, was Eve, the mother of us all, the lady of the New Jerusalem.

The leopardess reared; the flickering and fleeing of her spots began. The princess at length stood radiant in her perfect shape.

"I AM beautiful—and immortal!" she declared, looking the goddess she would be.

"As a bush that burns, and is consumed," answered he who had been her husband. "What is that under thy right hand?"

Her arm lay across her bosom, her hand pressed to her side.

A swift pang contorted her beautiful face and passed.

"It is but a leopard-spot that lingers! It will quickly follow those I have dismissed," she answered.

"Thou art beautiful because God created thee, but thou art the slave of sin: take thy hand from thy side."

Her hand sank away, and as it dropped, she looked him in the eyes with a quailing fierceness that held no surrender.

He gazed a moment at the spot.

"It is not on the leopard; it is in the woman!" he said. "Nor will it leave thee until it hath eaten to thy heart, and thy beauty hath flowed from thee through the open wound!"

She gave a glance downward and shivered.

"Lilith," said Adam, and his tone had changed to a tender beseeching. "Hear me, and repent, and He who made thee will cleanse thee!"

Her hand returned, quivering, to

"We must be vigilant," he said, his voice tight with a dread that vibrated through the quiet room. "Or she will outwit us once more. She would deceive even the chosen!"

"And how do we keep guard?" I asked, my mind racing to find a logical defense.

"Every conceivable way," he answered, pacing slowly. "She fears, and thus she hates her own creation. She is here, in this house, on a mission to destroy what she made. To her, the birth of children is the death of the parents; every new generation is an enemy to the last. Her daughter appears to her as a gaping chasm through which her immortality—something she believes is inherent to her own soul—is rapidly draining away. To fill that void, she has pursued her offspring with absolute enmity since birth. Yet, the result of her schemes is a colony of children in her claimed region, led by that daughter, who serves as their heart, their head, and their shelter. My Eve longed for the child, wishing to be a mother to her firstborn, but we were unfit to raise her then. She was carried into the wilderness, and for ages, we knew nothing of her fate. But she was divinely fostered, playing with young angels, untouched by care until she found a baby in the woods. Then, the mother within her awoke. Since then, she has found many children, one by one, and that heart is not yet full. Her family is her consuming charge; she has never mothered children more devotedly. Her authority over them is absolute, though unknown to herself, surfacing only in watchful service. She has forgotten the time before them, believing she emerged from the wood as the first of the family."

"You have saved the life of her and their enemy," he continued, his gaze locking onto mine. "Therefore, your life now belongs to them. The princess was on her way to destroy them, but as she crossed that stream, vengeance overtook her. She would have died had you not intervened. You did. Had you not, she would now be raging among the Little Ones, daring to cross the stream again. But there remained a path to the blessed colony through the world of three dimensions. By slaying her former body, she had excluded herself from it; she could not re-enter except through personal contact with someone belonging to that realm. You provided the opportunity. Never in her long years had she had one like it. Her hand, with the lightest touch, rested on one or both of your muffled feet with every step you climbed. In that small chamber, she watches now, waiting for the slightest chance to leave."

"She cannot know anything about the door!" I protested, though my heart trembled beneath my words. "She cannot at least know how to open it!"

"Hush!" the librarian whispered, raising a hand to silence me. "She can hear through anything. You must go at once to my wife's cottage. I will remain to guard her."

"Let me go to the Little Ones!" I cried, desperation rising in my throat.

"Beware, Mr. Vane. Go to my wife, and do as she commands."

His advice offered no comfort. Why hasten to meet measureless delay? If not to protect the children, why go at all? Even now, I believed him only enough to ask questions, not to obey.

"Tell me first, Mr. Raven," I demanded, "why, of all places, you have shut her up there! The night I fled your house, I ran straight into that closet!"

"The closet is no nearer our cottage, nor farther, than any other place."

"But," I countered, struggling against the illogical nature of his words, "how is it that, when you choose, you take from that same door a whole book where I saw and felt only a part? The other part, you just told me, protrudes into your library. When you return it to the shelf, will it not again stick through into that space? Must not the two places, where parts of the same volume exist simultaneously, lie close together? Or can one part of a book be in space, somewhere, while the other is out of space, nowhere?"

"I am sorry I cannot explain it to you," he answered gently. "There is no provision in you for understanding it. Not merely is the phenomenon inexplicable to you, but its very nature is inapprehensible. Indeed, I only partially grasp it myself. Yet, you constantly experience things you do not, and cannot, understand. You think you understand them, but your comprehension is merely habit; you are not surprised because you are used to them. You accept them not because you understand, but because they are there, bound to you by unavoidable relations. The truth is, no man truly understands anything. When he knows he does not understand, that is his first stumbling step—not toward understanding, but toward the capacity to eventually understand. To such mysteries, you are not accustomed, so you do not pretend to understand. Neither I nor any man can help you comprehend them here, but I may perhaps help you believe."

He moved to the closet door, gave a low whistle, and stood listening. A moment later, I heard—or seemed to hear—a soft whir of wings. Looking up, I saw a white dove perch for an instant atop the shelves above the portrait, then drop to Mr. Raven's shoulder, resting its head against his cheek. Only the motion of their heads revealed they were conversing; I heard nothing. I did not move my eyes from them when suddenly the bird was gone, and Mr. Raven returned to his seat.

"Why did you whistle?" I asked. "Sound here is not sound there!"

"You are right," he replied. "I whistled so you would know I called her. Not the whistle itself, but what the whistle meant reached her. There is not a minute to lose. You must go!"

"I will at once!" I replied, moving for the door.

"You will sleep tonight at my hostelry!" he said, not as a question, but with mild authority.

"My heart is with the children," I replied. "But if you insist—"

"I do insist. You can achieve nothing else. I will accompany you as far as the mirror and see you off."

He rose. Suddenly, a shock rippled through the closet. Apparently, the leopardess had thrown herself against the heavy door. I looked at my companion.

"Come; come!" he urged.

Before we reached the library door, a howling yell followed us, mingled with the screech of claws scoring the hard oak. I hesitated, turning halfway.

"To think of her lying there alone," I murmured, "with that terrible wound!"

"Nothing will ever close that wound," he answered, sighing deeply. "It must eat into her heart! Annihilation itself is no death to evil. Only good where evil was can kill evil. An evil thing must live with its evil until it chooses to be good. That alone is the slaying of evil."

I held my peace until a sound I did not understand overtook us.

"If she breaks loose!" I cried.

"Make haste!" he rejoined. "I shall hurry down the moment you are gone. I have already disarranged the mirrors."

We ran, reaching the wooden chamber breathless. Mr. Raven seized the chains and adjusted the hood. Then he set the mirrors in their proper relation and stepped beside me in front of the standing one. Already, I saw the mountain range emerging from the mist.

Between us, wedging us apart, darted the huge bulk of the spotted leopardess, yelping like a demon. She leaped through the mirror as through an open window and settled instantly into a low, swift gallop.

I cast a look of dismay at my companion and sprang through to follow her. He trailed behind me leisurely.

"You need not run," he called out. "You cannot overtake her. This is our way."

As he spoke, he turned in the opposite direction.

"She has more magic at her fingertips than I care to know!" he added quietly.

"We must do what we can!" I said, running on, but stopping as I saw her dwindle in the distance. I turned back to him.

"Doubtless we must," he answered. "But my wife has warned Mara, and she will do her part. You must sleep first: you have given me your word!"

"Nor do I mean to break it. But surely sleep is not the first thing! Surely, action takes precedence over repose!"

"A man can do nothing he is not fit to do. See! Did I not tell you Mara would do her part?"

I looked where he pointed and saw a white spot moving at an acute angle to the line taken by the leopardess.

"There she is!" he cried. "The spotted leopardess is strong, but the white is stronger!"

"I have seen them fight; the combat did not appear decisive regarding strength."

"How should such eyes tell which have never slept? The princess did not confess herself beaten—that she never does—but she fled! When she confesses her last hope gone, when it is indeed hard to kick against the goad, then will her day begin to dawn! Come; come! He who cannot act must make haste to sleep!"

I stood and watched the last shimmer of the white leopardess dissolve into the void, then turned to follow my guide—though with a heavy, reluctant heart. Why should I succumb to sleep? Reason, surely, was a constant across all realities, and what logic dictated resting while the living called? Besides, who would wake me? How could I trust in awakening at all, when those within that house allowed morning to bleed into noon, and noon to drown in night, never stirring? I murmured my protest, yet followed, for I knew no other path.

The librarian walked on in silence, and I matched his step with a matching quietude. Time and space flowed past us like water. The sun dipped below the horizon; darkness thickened, carrying with it the chilling breath of the chamber of death. My spirit sank deeper with every passing second. The lean, long-coated figure of my guide began to fade from my sight, his swishing stride through the heather eventually lost to the gloom.

Then, the sound shifted. The slow, rhythmic flapping of a raven's wings replaced the footsteps. At intervals, a firefly or a glowing butterfly rose into the rayless air, tiny lanterns in the deepening night.

By and by, the moon crept slowly across the distant horizon.

"You are weary, are you not, Mr. Vane?" the raven asked, landing heavily on a stone. "You must make acquaintance with the horse that will carry you in the morning!"

He emitted a strange, piercing whistle through his long black beak. Instantly, a dark spot bloomed on the face of the half-risen moon. To my ears came the drumming of swift, soft-galloping hooves, and within moments, thundering from the very disc of the moon itself, emerged the terrible steed. His mane streamed behind him like the crest of a wave fighting the wind, torn seaward in a spray of hoary mist, while the lash of his tail blinded the lunar light. He stood nineteen hands high, massive in bone, taut in skin, hard in muscle—a creature the holy Death himself might choose to ride abroad and slay! Even the moon seemed to regard him with awe; in her eerie light, he appeared a magnificent skeleton, loosely roped together by sheer force of will. Yet, terrifyingly large, he moved with the lightness of a winged insect. As he drew near, his speed slackened, his mane and tail drifting about him, settling into stillness.

Now, I was not merely a lover of horses; I adored every beast I beheld. I had spent my wealth only upon them and never sold one. The sight of this mighty, terrible creature ignited a fierce longing within me to possess him. It was pure greed, rank covetousness—an evil thing in all worlds. I did not mean I would steal him, but that regardless of his sacred purpose, I would have bought him if I could. I laid my hands upon him, stroking the protuberant bones that humped a hide smooth, thin, and shiny as satin—so polished that the very shape of the moon was reflected in it. I fondled his sharp-pointed ears, whispered words into them, and breathed into his red nostrils the warm breath of a man's life. In return, he breathed into mine the raw, primal breath of a horse's life, and we loved one another. What eyes he possessed! Blue-filmy, like the gaze of the dead, yet behind each eye burned a glowing coal! The raven, wings half-extended, watched my courtship of his magnificent steed with evident pleasure.

"That is well! Be friends with him," he said. "He will carry you all the better tomorrow! Now, we must hurry home!"

My desire to ride the horse had grown into a passion.

"May I not mount him at once, Mr. Raven?" I cried.

"By all means!" he answered. "Mount, and ride him home."

The horse bent his head over my shoulder, affectionate. I twisted my hands into his mane and scrambled onto his back, aided by certain sturdy protrusions of his spine.

"He would outspeed any leopard in creation!" I exclaimed.

"Not that way at night," the raven replied. "The road is difficult. But come; loss now will be gain then! To wait is harder than to run, and its reward is fuller. Go on, my son—straight to the cottage. I shall be there as soon as you. It will rejoice my wife's heart to see her son on that horse!"

I sat silent. The horse stood like a block of marble.

"Why do you linger?" the raven asked.

"I long so much to ride after the leopardess," I answered, "that I can scarce restrain myself!"

"You have promised!"

"My debt to the Little Ones appears, I confess, a greater thing than my bond to you."

"Yield to the temptation, and you will bring mischief upon them—and upon yourself also."

"What matters it for me? I love them; and love works no evil. I will go."

But the truth was, I had forgotten the children, infatuated by the horse.

Eyes flashed through the darkness, and I knew Adam stood in his true form beside me. I knew also by his voice that he repressed an indignation almost too strong for him.

"Mr. Vane," he said, "do you not know why you have not yet done anything worth doing?"

"Because I have been a fool," I answered.

"Wherein?"

"In everything."

"Which do you count your most indiscreet action?"

"Bringing the princess to life: I ought to have left her to her just fate."

"Nay, now you talk foolishly! You could not have done otherwise than you did, not knowing she was evil! But you never brought anyone to life! How could you, yourself dead?"

"I dead?" I cried.

"Yes," he answered. "And you will be dead, so long as you refuse to die."

"Back to the old riddling!" I returned scornfully.

"Be persuaded, and go home with me," he continued gently. "The most—nearly the only foolish thing you ever did, was to run from our dead."

I pressed the horse's ribs, and he was off like a sudden wind. I gave him a pat on the side of his neck, and he went about in a sharp-driven curve, "close to the ground, like a cat when scratchingly she wheels about after a mouse," leaning sideways until his mane swept the tops of the heather.

Through the dark, I heard the wings of the raven. Five quick flaps, and he perched on the horse's head. The horse checked himself instantly, ploughing up the earth with his hooves.

"Mr. Vane," croaked the raven, "think what you are doing! Twice already has evil befallen you—once from fear, and once from heedlessness: breach of word is far worse; it is a crime."

"The Little Ones are in frightful peril, and I brought it upon them!" I cried. "But indeed I will not break my word to you. I will return, and spend in your house what nights—what days—what years you please."

"I tell you once more you will do them other than good if you go tonight," he insisted.

But a false sense of power, a sensation with no root and merely vibrated into me from the strength of the horse, had rendered me too stupid to listen!

"Would you take from me my last chance of reparation?" I cried. "This time there shall be no shirking! It is my duty, and I will go—even if I perish for it!"

"Go, then, foolish boy!" he returned, with anger in his croak. "Take the horse, and ride to failure! May it be to humility!"

He spread his wings and flew away. Again I pressed the lean ribs beneath me.

"After the spotted leopardess!" I whispered in his ear.

He turned his head this way and that, snuffing the air; then started, and went a few paces in a slow, undecided walk. Suddenly he quickened his pace; broke into a trot; began to gallop, and in a few moments his speed became tremendous. He seemed to see in the dark; he never stumbled, not once faltered, not once hesitated. I sat as on the ridge of a wave. I felt under me the play of each individual muscle: his joints were so elastic, and his every movement glided so seamlessly into the next, that not once did he jar me. His growing swiftness bore him along until he flew rather than ran. The wind met and passed us like a tornado.

Across the evil hollow we sped like a bolt from an arbalest. No monster lifted its neck; all knew the hooves that thundered over their heads! We rushed up the hills, shot down their farther slopes; from the rocky chasms of the river-bed he did not swerve; he held on over them his fierce, terrible gallop. The moon, halfway up the heavens, gazed with a solemn trouble in her pale countenance. Rejoicing in the power of my steed and in the pride of my life, I sat like a king and rode.

We were near the middle of the many channels, my horse clearing one, sometimes two in his stride, gathering himself for a great bounding leap, when the moon reached the keystone of her arch. Then came a wonder and a terror: she began to descend, rolling like the nave of Fortune's wheel bowled by the gods, speeding faster and faster. Like our own moon, this one had a human face, and now the broad forehead, now the chin was uppermost as she rolled. I gazed aghast.

Across the ravines came the howling of wolves. An ugly fear began to invade the hollow places of my heart; my confidence was on the wane! The horse maintained his headlong swiftness, with ears pricked forward and thirsty nostrils exulting in the wind his career created. But there was the moon jolting like an old chariot-wheel down the hill of heaven, with awful boding! She rolled at last over the horizon-edge and disappeared, carrying all her light with her.

The mighty steed was in the act of clearing a wide, shallow channel when we were caught in the net of the darkness. His head dropped; his momentum carried his helpless bulk across, but he fell in a heap on the margin, and where he fell, he lay. I got up, knelt beside him, and felt him all over. Not a bone could I find broken, but he was a horse no more. I sat down on the body and buried my face in my hands.

The night descended with a biting, unnatural cold that seeped through my bones, freezing the very marrow beneath me. Somewhere nearby, the wind carried the soft, rhythmic padding of wolf paws on rock, punctuated by quick, greedy pants. Through the encroaching dark, a circle of glowing eyes tightened its grip around me. My time had arrived. I scrambled to my feet, panic seizing my throat—only to realize with a jolt of horror that I possessed not even a stick to defend myself.

They surged forward in a blur of fury, black throats gaping to consume me. I stood paralyzed, waiting for the end. Just as they lunged, a cloud of emerald eyes erupted from the shadows, diving onto the wolves' flanks. Heads bearing those eyes shot forward, crying out with a sound fiercer yet weaker than the wolves' own snarls. I recognized the cry instantly: it was the Little Ones, led by a massive gray figure whose presence I felt more than saw. A terrible battle erupted in the darkness, a chaotic clash of teeth and claws whose details were lost to me save for the sheer violence of it.

Desire to flee warred with the knowledge that any movement would only mark me for death; both predators could see and smell me in the gloom. Suddenly, the howling ceased, replaced by a wilder, more frantic caterwauling. The soft padding returned, signaling flight—the cats had won. But victory brought no relief. In an instant, sharp teeth sank into my legs, and I was overwhelmed by a living cataract of attackers. They bit and scratched with furious intensity, a multitude clinging to my skin like limpets. I thrashed wildly, tearing at them, throttling them in vain; when I tried to fling them off, they clung to my hands with impossible tenacity. I trampled them, thrust fingers into their eyes, and bit harder than they could, yet I could not dislodge a single one. They constantly discovered new spaces on my body to feast, their curved claws hauling at my skin while hissing spittle coated my face. Desperate, I collapsed to the ground, and they abandoned my body to dart at my face. Rising again, they immediately returned to my legs. In agony, I broke free and ran blindly into the solid dark. They pursued me in a surrounding torrent, rubbing against me and leaping up, tormenting me without ceasing. Every time I stumbled, they allowed me a moment to rise; every time I slowed from fear, they attacked my legs anew.

That miserable night they drove me along a surprisingly smooth path, sparing me from gullies as I passed the Evil Wood unseen. By morning, I found myself beyond the channels, standing on the verge of the orchard valley. Overcome with joy, I wished to make peace with my persecutors, but not a cat was to be seen. Exhausted, I threw myself onto the moss and fell into a deep sleep.

I was awakened by a sharp kick, finding myself bound hand and foot once more, a thrall of the giants. "What fitter fate?" I muttered to myself, laughing in the triumph of self-disgust. "To whom else should I belong?" A second kick silenced my false merriment. Thus aided by my captors, I eventually rose to my feet. Six of them surrounded me. They untied the rope binding my legs, attached separate ropes to each limb, and dragged me away. I walked as best I could, though frequent double-pulls sent me crashing to the ground, requiring them to kick me upright again. They marched me straight to my old labor site, tied my leg-ropes to a tree, freed my arms, and placed the hateful flint in my left hand. Then they lay down and pelted me with fallen fruit and stones, rarely hitting me. If only I could free my legs and grab the stick I'd spotted a couple of yards away, I might have taken down all six of them! "But the Little Ones will come at night!" I told myself, finding comfort in the thought.

All day I labored hard. When darkness fell, they tied my hands and left me fastened to the tree. I slept fitfully, waking often from dreams of lying amidst heaps of children. With dawn, my enemies returned with their kicks and their bestial company.

Around noon, nearly succumbing to fatigue and hunger, I heard a sudden commotion in the brushwood, followed by a burst of bell-like laughter so dear to my heart. I cried out in delight. Immediately, trumpeting like baby elephants, neighing like foals, and bellowing like calves filled the air. Through the bushes came a crowd of Little Ones riding diminutive horses, small elephants, and little bears—the noises emanating from the riders, not the beasts. Walking among them were older boys and girls, including a woman cradling a baby crowing in her arms. The giants sprang to their lumbering feet but were instantly greeted by a storm of sharp stones; horses charged their legs, bears hugged them at the waist, and elephants wrapped their trunks around their necks, pulling them down for a trampling they had never received before. In moments, my ropes were undone, and I was embraced by what seemed like an innumerable sea of Little Ones. For a while, I saw no more of the giants.

They seated me, and Lona appeared, silently beginning to feed me the loveliest red and yellow fruits. The entire colony mounted guard until I finished. Then, two of their largest elephants were brought forward, placed side by side, and hooked by trunk and tail. The docile creatures could have untied themselves with a shake or forgotten the hooks entirely, but they held fast, clearly understanding they must remain parallel. I rose and lay in the hollow between their backs. As the wise animals leaned against one another to counteract my weight, they formed a comfortable litter. My feet projected beyond their tails, but my head rested pillowed on an ear of each. Smaller children mounted as a bodyguard, lining the backs of my bearers, forming a procession that began to move.

I did not try to guess my destination, yielding myself to their pleasure, almost as happy as they. At first, they chattered, laughed, and played endless tricks, but the moment they sensed my drifting toward sleep, they fell silent as judges.

I woke to a sudden musical uproar. We were traveling through the forest where they found the babies, a stretch of woods reaching from the valley to the hot stream. A tiny girl sat with her feet close to my face, looking down coaxingly before speaking, the rest of the group hanging on her words.

"We make a petisson to king," she said.

"What is it, my darling?" I asked.

"Shut eyes one minute," she answered.

"Certainly I will! Here goes!" I replied, closing my eyes tight.

"No, no! Not before I tell you!" she cried.

I opened them again, and we talked and laughed for another hour.

"Close eyes!" she commanded suddenly.

I closed them and kept them shut. The elephants stood still. I heard a soft scurry, a little rustle, and then a profound silence—for in that world, some silences are heard.

"Open eyes!" twenty voices shouted from a distance. But when I obeyed, not a creature was visible except the elephants bearing me. I knew the children were marvelously quick at clearing the way—the giants had taught them that—but when I raised myself and scanned the open, shrubless forest, seeing neither hand nor heel, I stared in blank astonishment.

The sun had set, and darkness was fast approaching, yet soon a multitude of birds began to sing. I lay listening, certain that if I left them alone, the hiders would soon emerge. The singing grew into a storm of bird-voices. "Surely the children must have something to do with this! And yet how could they set the birds singing?" I wondered aloud. Soon, however, looking up into the tree beneath my elephants, I spied a slight motion among the leaves. I looked keenly. Sudden white spots appeared in the dark foliage, the music died down, and a gale of childish laughter rippled the air. White spots emerged in every direction; the trees were full of children! In wild merriment, they began to descend, some dropping from bough to bough so rapidly I could scarcely believe they hadn't fallen. I left my litter and was instantly surrounded—a target for all their jubilant artillery. With stately composure, the elephants walked away to bed.

"But," I said after their uproarious gladness had run its course, "how is it that I never before heard you sing like the birds? Even when I thought it must be you, I could hardly believe it!"

"Ah," said one of the wildest, "but we were not birds then! We were run-creatures, not fly-creatures! We had our hide-places in the bushes then; but when we came to no-bushes, only trees, we had to build nests! When we built nests, we grew birds, and when we were birds, we had to do birds! We asked them to teach us their noises, and they taught us, and now we are real birds! Come and see my nest. It's not big enough for king, but it's big enough for king to see me in it!"

I told him I could not climb a tree without the sun to guide me, promising to try when he returned.

"Kings seldom have wings!" I added.

"King! King!" cried one, "oo knows none of us hasn't no wings--foolis feddery tings! Arms and legs is better."

"That is true. I can get up without wings—and carry straws in my mouth too, to build my nest with!"

"Oo knows!" he answered, and wandered off sucking his thumb.

A moment later, I heard him calling from his nest, high up in an enormous walnut tree:

"Up adain, king! Dood night! I seepy!"

And I heard no more of him until he woke me in the morning.

I reclined beneath the boughs of a great tree, watching as the children drifted away one by one, or in small, murmuring clusters, to climb into their woven nests. They possessed a peculiar rhythm to their existence; so utterly exhausted by day and so miraculously refreshed by dawn, they seemed equally content to surrender to sleep and to wake anew. Though weariness weighed upon me as well, I remained awake, my heart holding vigil. Lona had not bid me goodnight, and I was certain she would return.

The moment I beheld her again, a profound shock had struck me: her resemblance to the Princess was undeniable. There could be no doubt she was the daughter Adam had spoken of. Yet, in Lona, the dazzling, terrifying radiance of Lilith was softened by an innocent childlikeness, deepened by a tender sense of motherhood. "She is likely occupied," I murmured to myself, "with the child of the woman I met fleeing!"—a woman who, Lona had confessed, was not yet half a mother.

She arrived at last, sitting beside me. After a few moments of silent, radiant joy, expressed through the gentle stroking of my face and hands, she began to recount everything that had transpired since my departure. As we spoke, the moon rose, and occasionally, filtering through the leaves, its silver light illuminated her beautiful face—a countenance filled with thought and a care that love alone could redeem and glorify. How such a child could be born of such a mother, of such a woman and such a princess, was a mystery hard to fathom; yet, happily, she had two parents—or perhaps three. She drew my heart toward her with a quality within me that mirrored her own, and I loved her as one who, regardless of the perfection she might achieve, would forever remain a child. I knew then that I loved her even as I left her, and that the hope of reunion had been my sole solace. Every word she spoke seemed to pierce straight to my heart, purifying it like the truth itself.

She told me that after I departed the orchard valley, the Giants began to believe more firmly in the actual existence of their neighbors. Consequently, their hostility grew. Sometimes the Little Ones would witness them trampling furiously, reacting to mere shadows or imagined signs of our presence, while we stood nearby, laughing at their foolish rage. Soon, however, their animosity took a practical form: they began destroying the trees whose fruit sustained us. This drove the Mother of them all to meditate on counteraction. She sent her sharpest scouts to listen at night and learned that the Giants believed I was hidden somewhere nearby, intending to strike them down in their sleep once I had recovered my strength. She concluded that the only way to halt the destruction was to give them grounds to believe we had abandoned the place entirely. The Little Ones must migrate into the forest—beyond the Giants' reach, yet close enough to their own trees, which they would visit only by night. The plan's main flaw was that the forest lacked the dense undergrowth needed to conceal them.

But she reflected that where there were birds, there could be habitation for the Little Ones. They possessed eager sympathies for all modes of life and could learn from the wildest creatures. Why not seek refuge from the cold and their enemies in the tree-tops? Why not, having slept in the low brushwood, now seek the lofty foliage? Why not build nests where scooping hollows was impossible? All that birds could do, the Little Ones could learn—except, indeed, to fly!

She spoke to them on the subject, and they listened with approval. They could already climb the trees and had often watched birds constructing their homes! The forest trees, though massive, did not look unwelcoming. They stretched their branches high toward the sky, some even reaching down, as if inviting us to dwell with them. Perhaps, in the crown of the tallest, they might find the bird that laid baby-eggs, sat upon them until ripe, and then tumbled them down to release the little ones! Yes; they would build sleep-houses in the trees, unseen by the Giants, for never did one of the dull-bearded brutes raise his head to look upward. Thus convinced, the Giants would surely believe we had fled the country, and the Little Ones would gather their apples, pears, figs, mesples, and peaches while the giants slept.

Thus reasoned the Lovers, eagerly adopting Lona's suggestion. Soon, they were as at home in the tree-tops as the birds themselves. The Giants, finding no trace of us, concluded we had been frightened out of the land, forgot their trees, and almost ceased to believe in the existence of their small neighbors.

Lona asked if I had noticed that many of the children had grown. I replied that I had not, though I could readily believe it. She assured me it was true, but added that the certain evidence that their minds had grown alongside their bodies had gone far toward mitigating the alarm the discovery had caused her.

In the final moments of twilight, and later when the moon shone brightly, they descended to the valley to gather enough fruit to sustain them for the next day. For the Giants never ventured out in the twilight; to them, it was absolute darkness, and they hated the moon—they would have extinguished it had they been able. But soon the Little Ones discovered that fruit gathered in the night was not wholly good the following day. The question arose: would it not be better, instead of merely pretending to have left, to make the bad Giants leave themselves?

She said they had already, while exploring the forest, made acquaintance with its animals, meeting most of them personally. Knowing how strong, wise, and docile some were, and how swift and manageable others, they set themselves to secure their aid against the Giants. With loving, playful approaches, they soon made more than friends of most of them. They began by addressing horse and elephant as "Brother Horse" or "Sister Elephant," until before long they had individual names for each. It took a little longer before they called the Bears "Brother Bear" or "Sister Bear," but that followed quickly. The other day she had heard one little fellow cry, "Ah, Sister Serpent!" to a snake that bit him as he played with it too roughly. Most would have nothing to do with a caterpillar except watch it through its changes; but when it finally emerged from its retreat with wings, all would immediately address it as "Sister Butterfly," congratulating it on its metamorphosis—for which they used a word meaning something like *repentance*—evidently regarding it as something sacred.

One moonlit evening, as they went to gather fruit, they encountered a woman seated on the ground with a baby in her lap—the very woman I had met on my way to Bulika. Mistaking her for a Giantess who had stolen one of their babies (for they regarded all infants as their property), they fell upon her in a multitude, beating her in a childish yet sufficiently bewildering fashion. She would have fled, but a boy threw himself down and held her by the feet. Recovering her wits, she recognized in her assailants the children whose hospitality she sought and immediately yielded the baby. Lona appeared and carried it away in her bosom.

While the woman noted that in striking her they were careful not to hurt the child, the Little Ones observed that, as she surrendered her, she hugged and kissed the infant just as they desired to do. They concluded she must be a Giantess of the same kind as the good Giant. The moment Lona secured the baby, they brought the woman fruit and began to shower her with every sort of childish attention.

The woman, previously in perplexity as to where to go, dared not return to the city because the Princess was certain to discover who had lamed her leopardess. Delighted by the friendliness of the little people, she resolved to remain with them for the present. She would have no trouble with her infant and might find a way to return to her husband, who was rich in money and gems and seldom unkind to her.

Here I must supplement, partly from conjecture, what Lona told me about the woman. Like the rest of the inhabitants of Bulika, she was aware of the tradition that the Princess lived in terror of the birth of an infant destined to her destruction. They were all unacquainted, however, with the frightful means by which she preserved her youth and beauty. Her deteriorating physical condition requiring a larger use of those means, they took the apparent increase of her hostility toward children as a sign that she saw her doom approaching. This, although no one dreamed of any attempt against her, nourished in them hopes of change.

Now arose in the woman's mind the idea of furthering the fulfillment of the shadowy prediction, or using the myth at least for her own restoration to her husband. What seemed more probable than that the fate foretold lay with these very children? They were marvelously brave, whereas the Bulikans were cowards in abject terror of animals! If she could rouse in the Little Ones the ambition of taking the city, then, in the confusion of the attack, she would escape from the little army, reach her house unrecognized, and lie hidden there, awaiting the result!

Should the children now succeed in expelling the Giants, she would begin at once, while they were yet flushed with victory, to suggest the loftier aim! By disposition, indeed, they were unfit for warfare; they hardly ever quarreled and never fought; they loved every living thing and hated either to hurt or to suffer. Still, they were easily influenced and could certainly be taught any exercise within their strength. At once she set some of the smaller ones to throwing stones at a mark. Soon they were all engrossed with the new game, growing skilful in it.

The first practical result was their use of stones in my rescue. While gathering fruit, they found me asleep, went home, held a council, and returned the next day with their elephants and horses. They overwhelmed the few Giants watching me and carried me off. Jubilant over their victory, the smaller boys were childishly boastful, the bigger boys less ostentatious, while the girls, though their eyes flashed more, were not so talkative as usual. The woman of Bulika no doubt felt encouraged.

We talked for the greater part of the night, chiefly about the growth of the children and what it might indicate. With Lona's power of recognizing truth, I had long been familiar; now I began to be astonished at her practical wisdom. Probably, had I been more of a child myself, I should have wondered less.

It was far from morning when I became aware of a slight fluttering and scrambling. I rose on my elbow and, looking about, saw many Little Ones descend from their nests. They disappeared, and in a few moments all was still again.

"What are they doing?" I asked.

"They think," answered Lona, "that, stupid as they are, the Giants will search the wood, and they are gone to gather stones with which to receive them. Stones are not plentiful in the forest, and they have to scatter far to find enough. They will carry them to their nests and, from the trees, attack the Giants as they come within reach. Knowing their habits, they do not expect them before morning. If they do come, it will be the opening of a war of expulsion: one or the other people must go. The result, however, is hardly doubtful. We do not mean to kill them; indeed, their skulls are so thick that I do not think we could—not that killing would do them much harm; they are so little alive! If one were killed, his Giantess would not remember him beyond three days!"

"Do the children then throw so well that the thing *might* happen?" I asked.

"Wait till you see them!" she answered, with a touch of pride. "But I have not yet told you of a strange thing that happened the night before last! We had come home from gathering our fruit and were asleep in our nests when we were roused by the horrid noises of beasts fighting. The moon was bright, and in a moment our trees glittered with staring little eyes, watching two huge leopardesses—one perfectly white, the other covered with black spots—which worried and tore each other with I do not know how many teeth and claws. To judge by her back, the spotted creature must have been climbing a tree when the other sprang upon her. When first I saw them, they were just under my own tree, rolling over and over each other. I got down on the lowest branch and saw them perfectly. The children enjoyed the spectacle, siding some with this one, some with that, for we had never seen such beasts before and thought they were only at play. But by degrees their roaring and growling almost ceased, and I saw that they were in deadly earnest, and heartily wished neither might be left able to climb a tree. But when the children saw the blood pouring from their flanks and throats, what do you think they did? They scurried down to comfort them, gathering in a great crowd about the terrible creatures and beginning to pat and stroke them. Then I got down as well, for they were much too absorbed to heed my calling to them; but before I could reach them, the white one stopped fighting and sprang among them with such a hideous yell that they flew up into the trees like birds. Before I got back into mine, the wicked beasts were at it again, tooth and claw. Then Whitey had the best of it; Spotty ran away as fast as she could run, and Whitey came and lay down at the foot of my tree. But in a minute or two she was up again, walking about as if she thought Spotty might be lurking somewhere. I waked often, and every time I looked out, I saw her. In the morning she went away."

"I know both the beasts," I said. "Spotty is a bad beast. She hates the children and would kill every one of them. But Whitey loves them. She ran at them only to frighten them away, lest Spotty should get hold of any of them. No one needs be afraid of Whitey!"

By this time the Little Ones were coming back, making much noise, for they had no care to keep quiet now that they were at open war with the Giants, and laden with good stones. They mounted to their nests again, though with difficulty because of their burdens,

With victory secured, the woman of Bulika turned her gaze to the prize itself. She spoke of the city's fragility, of the wickedness festering within its walls, and of the cowardice that paralyzed its people. Within days, the chatter of the children shifted entirely to Bulika, though they possessed no true concept of what a city entailed. It was then that I discerned the design of the woman, though the motive remained veiled.

The notion of seizing the place appealed profoundly to Lona—and to me. The children were evolving with startling rapidity; I saw no insurmountable barrier to our enterprise. I knew the terrible Lilith, whether woman or leopardess, possessed only one vulnerable point: her doom lay through her daughter, and the ancient prophecy held sway over the citizens' minds. Surely, whatever risk the venture entailed was justified! If successful—who could doubt our triumph?—the Little Ones, rising from a crowd of children, would swiftly become a youthful people. Their government and influence would be rooted in righteousness. Could they not rule the wicked with a rod of iron, becoming the redemption of the nation?

Yet, I must confess, I harbored views of personal advantage, not devoid of ambition. It seemed just that Lona should reclaim the throne her mother had occupied, and natural that she should make me her consort and minister. I envisioned spending my life in her service; between us, with the Little Ones as our core, what might we not achieve in building a noble state?

I also confess to a foolish dream of establishing a gem trade between the two worlds—a project happily impossible, for it would have served only to harm both realms.

Recalling Adam's plea, I suggested to Lona that finding water might accelerate the growth of the Little Ones. She deemed it prudent to postpone this, fearing unknown consequences, noting that eventually, they would likely face a new necessity regardless.

"They are what they are without it!" she declared. "When we possess the city, we shall search for water!"

Thus, we commenced our preparations, constantly reviewing the merry troops. Lona attended chiefly to the commissariat, while I drilled the little soldiers, teaching them to throw stones and wield various weapons, striving to forge them into warriors. Our greatest challenge was rallying them instantly to the flag at the sound of the call. Most were armed with slings; the older boys carried bows and arrows. The older girls bore aloe-spikes, strong as steel and sharp as needles, mounted on long shafts—formidable indeed. Their sole duty was to guard those too small to fight.

Lona had grown significantly, yet she seemed unaware, remaining as tall as ever. Her hair lengthened, and she had become almost a woman, yet she retained none of the fleeting beauties of childhood. When we reunited after our long separation, she laid down her infant, embraced me silently, her face glowing with joy, while the child whimpered. She sprang to him, holding him instantly to her bosom. To see her with any thoughtless, obstinate, or irritable child was to witness a tender grandmother. I seemed to have known her for ages—from before time began! I hardly remembered my mother, but in my mind's eye, she now resembled Lona; if I imagined a sister or child, invariably she wore Lona's face. My every imagination flew to her; she was my heart's wife! She rarely sought me, yet was almost always within hearing distance. Whatever I did or thought, I referred to her, rejoicing that while working in absolute independence, she felt most at home by my side. She never neglected a single child, and my love only quickened my sense of duty. To love her and to fulfill my duty seemed inseparable. She might suggest tasks for me or ask counsel, but she never seemed to suppose that either of us cared for anything other than what must be done. Her love overflowed upon me—not in caresses, but in a closeness of recognition akin to the devotion of a divine animal.

I never told her anything of her mother.

The forest teemed with birds, their plumage so splendid it seemed to compensate for the lack of flowers, which apparently could not grow without water. Their glorious feathers scattered everywhere, prompting me to fashion a garment for Lona from them. As I gathered and bound them in overlapping rows, she watched with evident appreciation, never asking what I crafted but waiting expectantly for the result. Within a week or two, it was finished: a long, loose mantle to fasten at the throat and waist, with openings for the arms.

I rose and placed it upon her. She rose, removed it, and laid it at my feet—I suspect from a sense of propriety. I placed it again on her shoulders, showing her how to insert her arms. She smiled, glanced at the feathers, stroked them, then removed it and set it aside. When she departed, she carried it, and I saw it no more for several days. Finally, one morning, she approached me wearing it, carrying another garment she had fashioned similarly from the dried leaves of a tough evergreen. These leaves possessed the strength of leather and the appearance of scale armor. I donned mine immediately, and thereafter we wore these garments whenever we rode.

For, on the forest's outskirts, a troop of full-grown horses had appeared one day. Unalarmed by creatures of such different shape, I quickly befriended them, training two of the finest for Lona and myself. Already accustomed to riding a small steed, her delight was immense when she first looked down from the back of a giant beast, which seemed proud of its burden. We exercised them daily until they obeyed instantly and feared nothing; thereafter, we rode them at parade and on the march.

The undertaking sometimes appeared foolhardy, yet the woman of Bulika's confidence—real or simulated—always overcame my hesitation. The princess's magic, she insisted, would prove powerless against the children; regarding any force she might muster, our animal allies alone assured our superiority. She claimed she was ready, with a simple stick, to face any two men of Bulika. She confessed a fear of the leopardess, but I was prepared for her. Still, I shrank from carrying *all* the children with us.

"Would it not be better," I asked, "that you remain in the forest with your baby and the smallest of the Little Ones?"

She replied that she relied heavily on the impression their sight would make on the women, especially the mothers.

"When they see the darlings," she said, "their hearts will be taken by storm! I must be there encouraging them to make a stand! If there remains any hardihood in the place, it will be found among the women!"

"You must not encumber yourself," I urged Lona, "with any of the children; you will be needed everywhere!"

There were two babies besides the woman's, and even on horseback, she almost always held one in her arms.

"I do not remember ever being without a child to care for," she answered, "but when we reach the city, it shall be as you wish!"

Her confidence in one who had failed so unworthily shamed me. Yet neither had I initiated the movement, nor did I have grounds to oppose it. I had no choice but to lend my best aid. For myself, I was ready to live or die with Lona. Her humility and trust humbled me, and I gave myself heartily to her purposes.

Our path lay across a grassy plain, requiring no food for the horses or the two cows accompanying us for the infants; however, the elephants required provisions. Though the grass suited them, it was short, and with their one-fingered trunks, they could not gather enough for a single meal. We therefore set the entire colony to gathering grass and making hay, which the elephants could carry in quantities lasting several days, supplemented by fresh gathering at each halt. For the bears, we stored nuts, and for ourselves, we dried abundant fruit. Having caught and tamed several more large horses, we loaded them and the elephants with these provisions, ready to depart.

Then Lona and I held a general review, and I addressed them. I began by stating that I had learned much about them and knew their origins. "We did not come from anywhere," they cried, interrupting me. "We are here!"

I told them that every one had a mother, like the mother of the last baby; that I believed they had all been brought from Bulika when so small they could not remember; that the wicked princess there feared babies so greatly and sought to destroy them that their mothers had carried them away to safety; and that now we went to Bulika to find their mothers and deliver them from the bad giantess.

"But I must warn you," I continued, "there is danger ahead, for we may have to fight hard to take the city."

"We can fight! We are ready!" cried the boys.

"Yes, you can," I returned, "and I know you will: mothers are worth fighting for! Only mind, you must all stay together."

"Yes, yes; we'll take care of each other," they answered. "Nobody shall touch one of us but his own mother!"

"You must heed every officer's command immediately!"

"We will, we will!--Now we're quite ready! Let us go!"

"One more thing," I added. "When you strike, make it a downright swinging blow; when you shoot an arrow, draw it to the head; when you sling a stone, sling it strong and straight."

"That we will!" they shouted with jubilant, fearless cries.

"Perhaps you will be hurt!"

"We don't mind that!--Do we, boys?"

"Not a bit!"

"Some of you may very possibly be killed!" I said.

"I don't mind being killed!" cried one of the finest smaller boys, riding a beautiful little bull that galloped and leaped like a horse.

"I don't either! I don't either!" echoed from all sides.

Then Lona, queen and mother and sister to them all, spoke from her great horse beside me:

"I would give my life," she said, "to have my mother! She might kill me if she liked! I should just kiss her and die!"

"Come along, boys!" cried a girl. "We're going to our mothers!"

A pang pierced my heart.—But I could not draw back; it would be moral ruin for the Little Ones!

The dawn broke cool and clear over the vast, emerald plain, painting the sky in shades of pale azure. We moved forward then, a procession of innocence and instinct that cut a striking figure against the natural world. Our plan was precise: march through the morning, rest in the heat of the afternoon, and press on under the cover of night. We aimed to slip into the city gates just as the first light of dawn touched the horizon, arriving unseen until we were already within the walls.

It seemed the entire forest had risen to accompany us. A vanguard of birds darted ahead, convinced of their own leadership, while swarms of butterflies, wasps, and dragonflies danced around our heads. Behind us trailed a host of four-legged creatures. As night fell, most of these terrestrial companions deserted us, but the avian and insect life remained loyal, fluttering alongside us all the way to the city's threshold.

We halted for a deep, undisturbed sleep during the afternoon; though this was our first true march, fatigue had not yet claimed us. By night, the cold spurred us to greater speed. Many of the children fell asleep on the backs of their beasts, waking refreshed in the morning, not a single soul tumbling from their perch. Some rode shaggy, shambling bears that nonetheless matched the pace of elephants; others were mounted on various species of deer, eager to race the entire distance had I not held them back. Those perched atop the hay-stacks on the elephants, blind to the massive forms beneath them, chatted endlessly with their mounts. One little voice, drawing hay from a trough, cooed to his "darling beast":

"Nosy dear, I am digging you out of the mountain, and shall soon get down to you: be patient; I'm a coming! Very soon now you'll send up your nose to look for me, and then we'll kiss like good elephants, we will!"

That same night, a cacophony erupted—a tumult of elephant trumpeting, horse neighing, and children imitating the sounds—that rang across the silent plains. Unsure of the city's proximity, I quickly silenced the uproar, fearing it might betray our approach.

Then, suddenly, the sun and the city rose together. To the children, the walls appeared merely as a colossal mass of rock. But when I explained that the interior was filled with nests of stone, a wave of apprehension washed over them. For the first time in their brief lives, many of them knew fear. The place looked ominous; how could they find mothers in such a fortress? Yet they pressed on bravely, trusting in Lona and in me, however unworthy I felt of that trust.

We rode through the sounding archway. Never had such a drumming of hooves, such a padding of paws, echoed so violently upon the ancient pavement. The horses startled at the resonance of their own steps; some halted, others plunged wildly and wheeled about, but they were soon calmed and continued. The Little Ones shivered, falling into a stillness as profound as death. The three girls clutched the infants they carried tighter. Only the bears and butterflies showed no fear; everyone else trembled.

A dark anxiety settled upon the woman's face, and I was not immune to the collective dread. The entire army rested on my shoulders and my conscience; I had led them to the very shadow of danger. Yet, I was bolstered by the vision of the coming kingdom of the Little Ones, where bad giants would be slaves and animals loving, obedient friends. Alas, the dreamer had not learned obedience himself. Untrusting, obstinate, I had placed myself at the head of this army of innocents, a slave to my own whims just like any king I had left behind. But Lona rode beside me as a child indeed—therefore a free woman—calm, silent, watchful, and utterly unafraid.

We were nearly at the city's heart before its inhabitants noticed us. Then, windows began to open, and sleepy heads peered out. Every face wore a dull stare of wonderless astonishment, which instantly curdled into consternation upon seeing the animals. Despite their terror, when they realized their invaders were almost entirely children, the women rushed into the streets, followed by the men. Yet, for a time, they kept to the edges of the road, daring not approach the beasts.

Finally, a boy of about five, obsessed with the idea of his mother, spotted a woman in the crowd whose face caught his eye. He threw himself from his antelope onto her, clinging to her neck. She returned his embrace eagerly, but a man's hand came over her shoulder, seizing the child by the throat. Instantly, a girl drove her sharp spear into the man's arm. He let out a savage howl and, stabbed by two or three others, fled yelling.

"They are just bad giants!" Lona cried, her eyes flashing as she drove her horse against a particularly tall man who, having stirred up a false sense of manhood, stood barring her path with a club. He dared not withstand the shock and slunk aside, only to fall moments later, struck by several stones. Another huge brute, avoiding my charger, stepped suddenly between me and the boy riding behind. The boy ordered him to address the king; the giant responded by striking the little horse on the head with a hammer, sending it crashing down. Before the brute could strike again, an elephant from behind laid him prostrate, trampling him so thoroughly that he did not attempt to rise until hundreds of feet had walked over him, and the army had passed.

But at the sight of the women, dismay clouded Lona's face. Hardly one of them possessed a pleasant countenance! Could her darlings ever find mothers among such figures?
