Scarcely had we halted in the central square when two girls rode up in anxious haste, reporting that two boys had been hurried away by some women. We turned immediately, discovering that the woman we had befriended had vanished with her baby. At that exact moment, however, we saw a white leopardess bounding down a narrow lane leading from the square to the palace. The Little Ones remembered the fight of the two leopardesses in the forest; some looked terrified, their ranks wavering, but they recalled my command and stood fast.

We stopped to observe. Suddenly, a small boy named Odu, renowned for his speed and courage, leaped from his bear—which followed him shambling—and ran to meet the leopardess. To avoid knocking him down, she pulled herself up so abruptly that she rolled over and over. When she regained her footing, she found the child on her back. Who could doubt the subjugation of a people that saw an urchin of the enemy striding atop an animal they lived in daily terror of? Confident this would affect the whole army, we rode on.

As we stopped at the house where our guides led us, a scream pierced the air. I sprang down and thundered at the door. My horse pushed me away with his nose, turned, and began battering the door with his heels. Just then, little Odu arrived on the leopardess; at sight of her, he froze, trembling. But she too had heard the cry. Forgetting the child on her back, she threw herself at the door. The boy was dashed against it and fell senseless. Before I could reach him, Lona had him in her arms, and once he regained consciousness, set him back on his bear, which still followed.

When the leopardess threw herself against the door a third time, it gave way, and she darted inside. We followed, but she had already vanished. We climbed the stairs and searched the house, finding no one. Darting back down, we spotted a door beneath the stairs and entered a labyrinth of excavations. We had not gone far when we met the leopardess carrying the child we sought across her back.

He told us that the woman he believed to be his mother had thrown him into a hole, claiming she would give him to the leopardess. But the leopardess was good; she had taken him out.

Following in search of the other boy, we entered the next house more easily, only to find, alas, that we were too late: one of the savages had just killed the little captive! It consoled Lona to learn who he was, for she had feared he would grow into a bad giant, a fate from which death had mercifully saved him. The leopardess sprang upon his murderer, seized him by the throat, dragged him into the street, and followed Lona with him, like a cat clutching a great rat in her jaws.

"Let us leave this horrible place," said Lona. "There are no mothers here! This people is not worth delivering."

The leopardess dropped her burden and charged into the crowd, weaving this way and that wherever it was thickest. The slaves cried out and ran, tumbling over one another in heaps.

When we returned to the army, we found it exactly as we had left it, standing in order and ready.

Yet I was far from at ease. The princess gave no sign, and her intentions remained unknown. Watch and ward must be kept throughout the night!

The Little Ones were such hardy creatures that they could repose anywhere. We told them to lie down with their animals where they were and sleep until called. In one moment they were down, and in the next, lost in the music of their sleep—a sound like water over grass or a soft wind among leaves. Their animals slept more lightly, ever on the edge of waking. The older boys and girls walked softly among the dreaming multitude. All was still; the whole wicked place appeared at rest.

A profound disgust burned within Lona, a fire directed not just at the people of this city, but specifically at the women among them. She yearned to flee, to abandon this place immediately. Yet, I stood firm against her impulse. To retreat now would be a willful failure where victory was still within reach; worse, it would shatter the spirits of the Little Ones, casting them into a peril far greater than the present. If we withdrew, the Princess would surely descend upon us without mercy. If we met her, the prophecy's thread remained intact. Mother and daughter were destined to collide. Perhaps Lona's radiant loveliness would strike the Princess's heart with such force that the conflict would end before it truly began. If violence threatened, I would stand between them, a shield of flesh and bone. And if that shield proved insufficient, if I found myself powerless to sway her, I was prepared, for the sake of my Lona, to strike the Princess's closed hand with pitiless force. I knew the doom awaited us; it was likely written that this very moment would seal her fate through our hands.

Without revealing the nature of my bond to the Princess, I laid out this grim scenario to Lona. Instantly, she agreed to accompany me to the palace.

High atop one of the great towers, the Princess had watched the dawn break over the sleeping city, spotting the approaching army of the Little Ones. The sight had seized her with a terror so overwhelming it stole her breath. She had failed in her quest to destroy them; they were upon her! The prophecy was about to fulfill its terrible promise.

When reason returned, she descended to the black hall and took her seat in the northern focus of the ellipse, beneath the opening in the roof. She needed to think. But her version of thinking required a specific delusion: she had to perceive herself not as she was, but as she chose to believe herself. To aid this realization, she had suspended a mirror slightly above and beyond her, invisible in the hall's darkness, designed to catch the full sunlight reflected from her own form. She waited for the meridian sun, hoping the vision of her own splendor would sustain her.

Shadows moved about her in the gloom. Whenever her inner eye caught sight of one, she refused to acknowledge it. Close beneath the mirror stood the Shadow that attended her walks, yet, lost in its own obsessions, she did not see him.

The city fell; its inhabitants cowered in terror. The Little Ones and their strange cavalry encamped in the square. The sun shone upon the Princess, and for a fleeting moment, she saw herself glorious. But the vision faded, and she remained seated. Night fell, cloaking the glass in darkness, yet she did not stir. A gloom, teeming with shadows, swelled within the palace. The servants shivered, paralyzed by the beasts of the Little Ones, daring not to leave the chamber. All night long, the Princess sat motionless, desperate to see her beauty again, desperate to try to think. But courage and will had grown weary of her and departed.

At dawn, we selected twelve of the tallest and bravest boys to join us. We mounted our great horses, while the Little Ones rode their small mounts and elephants.

The Princess sat waiting for the sun to grant her the joy of her own presence. The tide of light crept up the sky's shore, but until the sun stood directly overhead, not a single ray could penetrate the black hall.

Then he rose to our eyes and swiftly ascended. As we climbed the steep path to the palace, he climbed the dome of its great hall. He peered into its eye—and with sudden radiance, the Princess flashed upon her own sight. But she sprang to her feet with a cry of despair. Alas for her whiteness! A spot covered half her side, black as the marble surrounding it. She clutched her robe and collapsed back into her chair. The Shadow glided out, and she watched him depart.

We found the gate open as usual, passed through the paved grove to the palace door, and entered the vestibule. There, in her cage, lay the spotted leopardess, appearing asleep or lifeless. The Little Ones paused to gaze at her. Suddenly, she leaped up rampant against the bars. The horses reared and plunged; the elephants stepped back. In the next instant, she fell supine, writhed in quivering spasms, and lay motionless. We rode into the great hall.

The Princess still leaned back in her chair, bathed in the shaft of sunlight, when the noise of hooves struck her ears from the stones of the court. She started, listened, and trembled. Never had such a sound been heard in her palace. She pressed her hand to her side and gasped. The trampling drew nearer, entering the hall itself; moving figures that were not shadows approached her through the darkness!

To us, however, there was a splendour, a glorious woman centering the dark. Lona sprang from her horse and bounded forward. I dismounted and followed.

"Mother! Mother!" she cried, her clear, lovely voice echoing in the dome.

The Princess shivered. Her face grew almost black with hate, her eyebrows knitting together on her forehead. She rose to her feet and stood rigid.

"Mother! Mother!" Lona cried again, leaping onto the dais and flinging her arms around the Princess.

In an instant, I would have reached them—but in that split second, I saw Lona lifted high and dashed onto the marble floor. Oh, the horrible sound of her fall! At my feet she lay still. The Princess sat down, wearing the smile of a demoness.

I dropped to my knees beside Lona, raised her from the stones, and pressed her to my bosom. With indignant hate, I glanced at the Princess; she answered with her sweetest smile. I would have sprung upon her, seized her by the throat, and strangled her, but love for the child was stronger than hate for the mother. I clasped my precious burden tighter. Her arms hung helpless; her blood trickled over my hands, falling to the floor with soft, slow little plashes.

The horses sensed it—mine first, then the small ones. Mine reared, shivering and wild-eyed, thundering blindly down the dark hall, with the little horses following. Lona's horse stood gazing down at his mistress, trembling all over. The boys threw themselves from their mounts; failing to see the black wall ahead, they dashed themselves, along with mine, to pieces against it. The elephants advanced to the foot of the dais and stopped, trumpeting wildly. The Little Ones sprang upon them, standing horrified. The Princess lay back in her seat, her face that of a corpse, her eyes alone alive, wickedly flaming. She was again withered and wasted, as I had found her in the wood, her side marked as if by a great branding iron. But Lona saw nothing, and I saw only Lona.

"Mother! Mother!" she sighed, and her breathing ceased.

I carried her into the court. The sun shone upon a white face, bearing the pitiful shadow of a ghostly smile. Her head hung back. She was "dead as earth."

I forgot the Little Ones, forgot the murdering Princess, forgot the body in my arms, and wandered away, searching for my Lona. The doors and windows were crowded with brute-faces jeering at me, yet they dared not speak, for they saw the white leopardess behind me, hanging her head close at my heel. I spurned her with my foot. She held back a moment, then followed me again.

I reached the square: the little army was gone! Its emptiness roused me. Where were the Little Ones, *her* Little Ones? I had lost her children! I stared helplessly about me, staggered to a pillar, and sank upon its base.

But as I sat gazing at the still countenance, it seemed to offer a live, momentary smile. I never doubted it was an illusion, yet I believed what it said: I should yet see her alive! It was not she who was lost, but I; she would find me!

I rose to go after the Little Ones, instinctively seeking the gate by which we had entered. I looked around, but saw nothing of the leopardess.

The street was rapidly filling with a fierce crowd. They saw me encumbered with my dead, but for a time dared not assail me. Before I reached the gate, however, they had gathered courage. The women began to hustle me; I held on heedless. A man pushed against my sacred burden; with a kick I sent him away howling. But the crowd pressed upon me, and fearing for the dead that was beyond hurt, I clasped my treasure closer and freed my right arm. That instant, a commotion arose behind me; the crowd broke. Through the gap came the Little Ones I had left in the palace. Ten of them rode four elephants; on the two others lay the Princess, bound hand and foot, quite still save for her eyes rolling in their ghastly sockets. The two remaining Little Ones rode behind her on Lona's horse. Every now and then, the wise creatures that bore her threw their trunks back to feel her cords.

I walked on in front, leading the way out of the city. What an end to the hopes with which I entered this evil place! We had captured the bad Princess, and lost our all-beloved Queen! My life was bare; my heart was empty!

The low hum of delight that rippled through my companions snapped me back to awareness; they had spotted their kin in the distance. The two riders atop Lona's horse spurred forward to join the gathering, only to be met by a wavering shout that instantly dissolved into silence. As we closed the gap, the sound of their weeping reached us, rising like the breaking of tiny, desperate billows.

When I stepped into the circle, the truth of their plight became undeniable. Something terrible had struck them, etching a haggard, hollow terror onto their childish faces that no ordinary grief could produce. Several of them moved slowly toward me, arms outstretched to receive my burden. I yielded it to them. The tender, hopeless smile with which they accepted her made my own heart swell with pity, drowning my personal desolation in theirs. Their sobs over their mother-queen were futile; their attempts to coax even a flicker of recognition from her were in vain. They kissed her, fondled her, and carried her away, but she remained stone-cold. Two bore her arms, stroking them gently; as many as could reach placed their limbs beneath her body; the rest crowded close, a wall of small, grieving forms. They laid her down upon a patch of thick, soft grass, and there, surrounded by the Little Ones, the silence of her death settled over them.

Beyond the weeping crowd stood the elephants, and I stood near them, peering over the sea of small heads to gaze at Lona. Those nearest me caught sight of the princess and recoiled, trembling. Odu was the first to break the silence.

"I have seen that woman before!" he whispered to the boy beside him. "It was she who fought the white leopardess the night they woke us with their screaming!"

"Silly!" his companion retorted. "That was just a wild beast with spots!"

"Look at her eyes!" Odu insisted, his voice tight. "I know she is a bad giantess, but she is a wild beast all the same. I know she is the spotted one!"

The other boy took a step closer; Odu yanked him back with a sharp tug.

"Don't look at her!" he cried, shrinking away yet drawn by a hate-filled longing in her unseeing eyes. "She would eat you up in a moment! It was HER shadow! She is the wicked princess!"

"That cannot be! They said she was beautiful!"

"Indeed it is the princess!" I interposed, my voice steady. "Wickedness has made her ugly!"

She heard me, and the look that passed across her face was a storm of recognition and rage.

"It was very wrong of me to run away!" Odu murmured thoughtfully.

"What made you run away?" I asked. "I expected to find you where I left you!"

He hesitated.

"I don't know what made me run," another child admitted. "I was frightened!"

"It was a man that came down the hill from the palace," a third offered.

"How did he frighten you?"

"I don't know."

"He wasn't a man," Odu corrected, his eyes wide. "He was a shadow; he had no thickness to him!"

"Tell me more about him."

"He came down the hill very black, walking like a bad giant, but spread flat. He was nothing but blackness. We were frightened the moment we saw him, but we didn't run; we stood and watched. He came on as if he would walk right over us. But before he reached us, he began to spread and spread, growing bigger and bigger, until he was so vast he vanished from our sight entirely. Then... then he was upon us!"

"What do you mean by that?"

"He was all black through between us, and we couldn't see one another. And then he was inside us."

"How did you know he was inside you?"

"He did me quite differently. I felt like bad. I was not Odu anymore—not the Odu I knew. I wanted to tear Sozo to pieces—not really, but like!"

He turned and hugged Sozo tightly.

"It wasn't me, Sozo," he sobbed. "Really, deep down, it was Odu, loving you always! And Odu came up, and knocked Naughty away. I grew sick, thinking I must kill myself to get out of the black. Then came a horrible laugh that had heard my thoughts, and it set the air trembling around me. And then I suppose I ran away, but I didn't know I had run until I found myself moving, fast as I could, and all the rest running too. I would have stopped, but I never thought of it until I was out of the gate and among the grass. Then I knew I had fled from a shadow that wanted to be me and wasn't, and that I was the Odu who loved Sozo. It was the shadow that got into me, hating him from within; it was not my own self! And now I know I ought not to have run! But indeed I didn't quite know what I was doing until it was done! My legs did it, I think: they grew frightened, forgot me, and ran away! Naughty legs! There! And there!"

With a kick to each of his "naughty" legs, Odu finished his tale.

"What became of the shadow?" I asked.

"I do not know," he answered. "I suppose he went home into the night where there is no moon."

My mind wandered to where Lona had gone. Dropping to the grass, I took the dead princess into my lap and whispered into her ear, "Where are you, Lona? I love you!" Her lips gave no answer. I kissed them, feeling them not quite cold, laid her body down once more, and appointed a guard to watch over her. Rising, I prepared to ensure the safety of Lona's people throughout the coming night.

Before the sun dipped below the horizon, I had established a watch over the princess outside the camp, placing sentinels around her. Intending to patrol the perimeter myself all night, I ordered the rest of the army to sleep. They threw themselves onto the grass and were asleep in an instant.

When the moon rose, I caught a glimpse of something white; it was the leopardess. She swept silently around the sleeping camp, passing three times between the princess and the Little Ones. Seeing this, I made the watch lie down with the others and stretched myself beside the still form of Lona.

The sun rose pale and thin over the wastes, and we pressed forward toward the forest with desperate speed. I rode Lona's horse, the beast I had stolen from her, while I carried her corpse wrapped in her own cloak. My mind was a storm of conflicting duties. I intended to deliver Lona's body to her father, hoping he would grant her a proper resting place in a chamber of the dead. If he refused, or if he deemed her unworthy since she had not come willingly, I would leave her to rot in the desert sands, a final testament to her rebellion. But I dared not hope for refusal; she had died long ago, and the weight of her death demanded a humble penance from me.

And then there was Lilith. To Adam, I owed the return of his wife. I possessed no power to force her repentance, no moral authority to slay her, and certainly no right to remain her eternal jailer. Yet, here she was, bound and broken, trailing behind us.

On the road, I offered her food again and again, but she met my kindness only with a glare of hungry hatred. Her eyes, fiery and unblinking, rolled restlessly until we crossed the scorching stream. Afterward, they remained sealed shut until we finally approached the House of Bitterness.

That evening, as we camped, a small child wandered toward Lilith. Instinctively, I lunged to intervene, but it was too late. The child had pressed something to the princess's lips and recoiled with a scream of agony.

"Please, king," the child whimpered, holding up a tiny, bleeding finger. "Suck finger. Bad giantess make hole in it!"

I leaned down and sucked the wound clean. The child's name was Luva. A moment later, she offered another fruit to a mouth that craved sustenance, but Lilith snatched her hand away, dropping the offering to the dust.

The following day, we crossed the hot stream once more. On their own territory, the Little Ones—the children of the forest—were jubilant. Their nests lay far ahead, so we halted at the Ivy-Hall, drawn by the scent of ripe grapes. Seeing the great clusters, the children rushed forward, feasting eagerly until exhaustion claimed them. They slept soundly on the green floor and scattered through the surrounding woods. Hoping to witness the nightly dance of the spirits, I cleared a wide space in the center of the hall. I lay down among the sleeping children, Lona's body beside me, though sleep eluded me.

Night fell, and suddenly the company arrived. I wondered if these spectral dancers would perform eternally, perhaps forcing me to join them in my stubbornness, when the children's eyes snapped open. They leaped to their feet, grabbing the dancers' hands and pulling them into a chaotic, joyful chase. The spectres seemed to welcome the interruption; perhaps they remembered their own lost childhoods. The children's innocent gambols brought a strange refreshment to weary souls who possessed neither present nor future, living only on the shadow of a vanished past. Their pranks were never rude, and though they occasionally disrupted the rhythm, the ghosts showed no annoyance, for they had nothing left to smile with.

Just as dawn began to bleed into the sky, I saw the skeleton-princess standing in the doorway. Her eyes were open and glowing, the black spot of decay marking her side. She hesitated, then glided inward as if to join the dance. I sprang up, and a cry of repugnant fear erupted from the children, causing the lights to vanish. The moon peered in, revealing the children clinging to one another in terror. The ghosts were gone, or at least invisible. The princess had disappeared too. I darted to where she had stood; she lay motionless, eyes closed, as if she had never moved. Returning to the hall, I found the Little Ones already settling down for sleep.

The next morning, we spotted two skeletons moving through a nearby thicket. The children broke formation and ran to them. Even though I walked freely now, without splints or ligatures, I recognized the pair. The children made friends instantly, stroking the long bones of their fingers. The skeletons seemed to welcome the touch, drawn together by their shared deprivation. Seeing them gather herbs to rub onto their bones, the Little Ones collected similar plants and filled the outstretched skeletal hands. They promised to visit again before resuming their journey, marveling that such kind people lived in the forest.

At the nest-village, I stayed the night to ensure the children were settled safely. Lona still appeared lifeless, and there was no rush. But the princess had eaten nothing, her eyes remaining shut. Fearing she might die before we reached our destination, I woke in the night and placed my bare arm against her lips. She bit down with ferocious strength, and I cried out in pain. I barely escaped her grasp, waking to find myself lying beyond her reach. Morning broke, and I immediately prepared to depart.

I selected twelve of the sweetest, merriest Little Ones, mounting them on six elephants, and assigned two of the wise Clumsies to carry the princess. I rode Lona's horse, dragging her body before me. I chose the direct route across the left branch of the riverbed to the House of Bitterness, hoping to learn how to navigate the broader, rougher branch and avoid the basin of monsters. I dreaded the terrain for the elephants and the danger for the children.

The third night in the desert proved terrible. We stopped the elephants in a sheltered spot, letting the princess slip down between them to lie on the sand. She seemed dead, yet I suspected otherwise. I lay a short distance away, keeping watch over both the corpse and the dangerous spirit. The moon hung half-way down the west, a pale, thoughtful orb mottling the desert with shadows. Suddenly, it was eclipsed, sending forth no light despite remaining visible. A thick, diaphanous film covered its beauty, looking troubled. As the film swept aside, I saw the jagged outline of a bat-like wing, torn and hooked.

A cold wind with a burning sting swept over us, and Lilith was upon me. Her hands were still bound, but with her teeth, she tore the cloak from my shoulder and drove her nails into my flesh. I lay paralyzed as life seemed to drain from me into her. Remembering my resolve, I struck her hand. She raised her head with a gurgling shriek, shivering violently. I flung her off and sprang to my feet.

She knelt, rocking back and forth. A second blast of hot-stinging cold enveloped us, and the moon shone clear again. Her face was gaunt and ghastly, smeared with blood.

"Down, devil!" I cried.

"Where are you taking me?" she asked, her voice a dull echo from a sepulchre.

"To your first husband," I answered.

"He will kill me!" she moaned.

"At least he will take you off my hands!"

"Give me my daughter!" she screamed, grinding her teeth.

"Never! Your doom is upon you at last!"

"Loose my hands for pity's sake! I am in torture. The cords are sunk in my flesh."

"I dare not. Lie down!" I commanded.

She threw herself on the ground like a log. The rest of the night passed in silence, and by morning, she appeared dead once more.

By evening, we saw the House of Bitterness. Immediately, one of the elephants approached my horse.

"Please, king, you are not going to that place?" whispered the Little One riding on its neck.

"Indeed I am! We are going to stay the night there," I replied.

"Oh, please, don't! That must be where the cat-woman lives!"

"If you had ever seen her, you would not call her by that name!"

"Nobody ever sees her: she has lost her face! Her head is back and side all round."

"She hides her face from dull, discontented people! Who taught you to call her the cat-woman?"

"I heard the bad giants call her so."

"What did they say about her?"

"That she had claws to her toes."

"It is not true. I know the lady. I spent a night at her house."

"But she MAY have claws to her toes! You might see her feet, and her claws be folded up inside their cushions!"

"Then perhaps you think that I have claws to my toes?"

"Oh, no; that can't be! You are good!"

"The giants might have told you so!" I pursued.

"We shouldn't believe them about you!"

"Are the giants good?"

"No; they love lying."

"Then why do you believe them about her? I know the lady is good; she cannot have claws."

"Please how do you know she is good?"

"How do you know I am good?"

I rode on, telling the others what I had said. When they caught up, I explained further.

"I would not take you to her house if I did not believe her good," I said.

"We know you would not," they answered.

"If I were to do something that frightened you--what would you say?"

"The beasts frightened us sometimes at first, but they never hurt us!" answered one.

"That was before we knew them!" added another.

"Just so!" I answered. "When you see the woman in that cottage, you will know that she is good. You may wonder at what she does, but she will always be good. I know her better than you know me. She will not hurt you,--or if she does,----"

"Ah, you are not sure about it, king dear! You think she MAY hurt us!"

"I am sure she will never be unkind to you, even if she do hurt you!"

They fell silent.

"I'm not afraid of being hurt--a little!--a good deal!" cried Odu. "But I should not like scratches in the dark! The giants say the cat-woman has claw-feet all over her house!"

"I am taking the princess to her," I said.

"Why?"

"Because she is her friend."

"How can she be good then?"

"Little Tumbledown is a friend of the princess," I answered, "so is Luva: I saw them both, more than once, trying to feed her with grapes!"

"Little Tumbledown is good! Luva is very good!"

"That is why they are her friends."

"Will the cat-woman--I mean the woman that isn't the cat-woman, and has no claws to her toes--give her grapes?"

"She is more likely to give her scratches!"

"Why?--You say she is her friend!"

"That is just why.--A friend is one who gives us what we need, and the princess is sorely in need of a terrible scratching."

They were silent again.

"If any of you are afraid," I said, "you may go home; I shall not prevent you. But I cannot take one with me who believes the giants rather than me, or one who will call a good lady the cat-woman!"

"Please, king," said one, "I'm so afraid of being afraid!"

"My boy," I answered, "there is no harm in being afraid. The only harm is in doing what Fear tells you. Fear is not your master! Laugh in his face and he will run away."

"There she is--in the door waiting for us!" cried one, covering his eyes.

"How ugly she is!" cried another, doing the same.

"You do not see her," I said; "her face is covered!"

"She has no face!" they answered.

"She has a very beautiful face. I saw it once.--It is indeed as beautiful as Lona's!" I added with a sigh.

"Then what makes her hide it?"

"I think I know:--anyhow, she has some good reason for it!"

"I don't like the cat-woman! she is frightful!"

"You cannot like, and you ought not to dislike what you have never seen.--Once more, you must not call her the cat-woman!"

"What are we to call her then, please?"

"Lady Mara."

"That is a pretty name!" said a girl; "I will call her 'lady Mara'; then perhaps she will show me her beautiful face!"

Mara, dressed and muffled in white, stood in the doorway to receive us.

"At last!" she said. "Lilith's hour has been long on the way, but it is come! Everything comes. Thousands of years have I waited--and not in vain!"

She came to me, took the princess from my arms, carried her into the house, and returned to take the bound woman. Lilith shuddered but made no resistance. The beasts lay down by the door. We followed our hostess, the Little Ones looking very grave. She laid the princess on a rough settle at one side of the room, unbound her, and turned to us.

"Mr. Vane," she said, "and you, Little Ones, I thank you! This woman would not yield to gentler measures; harder must have their turn. I must do what I can to make her repent!"

The pitiful-hearted Little Ones began to sob sorely.

"Will you hurt her very much, lady Mara?" said the girl who had spoken first, putting her warm little hand in mine.

"Yes; I am afraid I must; I fear she will make me!" answered Mara. "It would be cruel to hurt her too little. It would have all to be done again, only worse."

"May I stop with her?"

"No, my child. She loves no one, therefore she cannot be WITH any one. There is One who will be with her, but she will not be with Him."

"Will the shadow that came down the hill be with her?"

"The great Shadow will be in her, I fear, but he cannot be WITH her, or with any one. She will know I am beside her, but that will not comfort her."

"Will you scratch her very deep?" asked Odu, going near and putting his hand in hers. "Please, don't make the red juice come!"

She caught him up

The night was a tempest of silence, a troubled darkness that bled into the waking day. Fear did not merely accompany their sleep; it inhabited it, a silent, terrible presence that made the house itself tremble. To the children, it was a phantom wind that shook the walls without a sound, a rising tide that filled the garret to the rafters yet made no splash, and a swarm of cats that clawed at their nightgowns before vanishing into the howl of a distant ancestor. They slept and woke in a cycle of dry, warm terror, unaware that while they dreamed of monsters, a far more ancient and profound judgment was unfolding in the shadows of the room.

I, however, was witness to the truth. When Mara departed with the children, the veil of the night lifted slightly. In the corner, cowering in mortal dread, lay the white leopardess, a creature of instinct terrified by the unnatural stillness. The Princess, Lilith, sat upon the settle, a statue of sullen defiance, while the room flickered with cloudy presences that seemed to breathe with the approaching doom.

When Mara returned, she drew the settle to the center of the hearth, placing herself opposite me. Between us, a small fire burned, but the air was thick with a dread sense of impending judgment. I felt the heaving of the earth, the shifting of the soul, a vibration that was neither physical nor spiritual, but the very fabric of reality straining under the weight of what was to come.

Midnight arrived, and the muffled woman rose. She unwound the swathes of cloth that hid her face, letting them drop to the floor, and revealed the visage of the Princess. It was a face of heartbreaking beauty, white and sorrowful, yet devoid of unhappiness—a serene vessel for a storm. Great tears streamed down her cheeks, wiped away by her robe, leaving her expression severe with pity.

"Will you turn away from the wicked things you have been doing so long?" Mara asked, her voice soft as a whisper.

Lilith remained silent. Mara repeated the question, then a third time. Finally, the seeming corpse stirred. Her mouth opened, and though no sound emerged, words formed in the air, direct and terrible.

"I will not," Lilith declared. "I will be myself and not another!"

"You are another now, not yourself!" Mara countered gently. "Will you not be your real self?"

"I will be what I mean myself now."

"If you were restored, would you not make amends for the misery you have caused?"

"I would do after my nature."

"You do not know it: your nature is good, and you do evil!"

"I will do as my Self pleases."

"You will do as the Shadow inclines you?"

"I will do what I will to do."

"You have killed your daughter, Lilith!"

"I have killed thousands. She is my own!"

"She was never yours as you are another's."

"I am not another's; I am my own, and my daughter is mine."

"Then, alas, your hour is come!"

"I care not. I am what I am; no one can take from me myself!"

"You are not the Self you imagine."

"So long as I feel myself what it pleases me to think myself, I care not. My own thought makes me me. Another shall not make me!"

"But another has made you, and can compel you to see what you have made yourself. You will not much longer have satisfaction in the thought of yourself. At this moment you are aware of the coming change!"

"No one ever made me! I defy that Power to unmake me from a free woman! You are his slave, and I defy you! You may torture me, but you shall not compel me!"

"There is a light that goes deeper than the will," Mara said, her voice steady. "That light can change your will, can make it truly yours and not another's. Into the created can pour itself the creating will, and so redeem it!"

"That light shall not enter me: I hate it! Begone, slave!"

"I am no slave, for I love that light. There is no slave but the creature that wills against its creator. Who is a slave but she who cries, 'I am free,' yet cannot cease to exist!"

"You speak foolishness from a cowering heart! I hold myself against you! What I choose to be, you cannot change!"

"I am sorry: you must suffer!"

"But be free!"

"She alone is free who would make free; she loves not freedom who would enslave. Every life you sought to subdue, you have enslaved. You are the slave of every slave you have made—such a slave that you do not know it! See your own self!"

Mara stepped back. Suddenly, the house was possessed by a soundless presence, a roaring flame that the children had mistaken for a wind. From the shadows, a silvery creature, like a slowworm of living fire, crawled across the floor. It moved with deliberate slowness toward the settle, its heat glowing vivid as incandescent silver. It stopped at the Princess's feet, then climbed the robe, inch by inch, until it vanished into the folds of her bosom.

The Princess's face remained stonily calm, her eyelids closed as over dead eyes. Minutes passed in agonizing stillness. Then, on her parched skin, drops of dew appeared, swelling into seed-pearls that poured down in streams. I lunged to crush the creature, but Mara interposed, drawing aside the robe. There was no serpent, no scorch mark. The fire had passed through the center of the black spot, piercing through the joints and marrow straight to the heart.

"She is seeing herself!" Mara whispered, pulling me back.

The Princess arched her body, springing to her feet. Horror twisted her features, and her hair stood out, emitting sparks before falling again to pour sweat onto the floor. She was far away, Mara explained, trapped in the hell of self-consciousness. The central fire of the universe was radiating into her the knowledge of good and evil. She saw the good she was not, and the evil she was. She knew she was the fire burning her, but she did not know that the Light of Life was the heart of that fire.

At last, she collapsed onto the floor. Mara stood over her, tears falling from her own eyes onto the woman who had never wept.

"Will you change your way?" Mara asked.

"Why did he make me such?" Lilith gasped, her voice breaking. "I would have made myself so different! I am glad it was he that made me and not I myself! He alone is to blame! Never would I have made such a worthless thing! He meant me such that I might know it and be miserable! I will not be made any longer!"

"Unmake yourself, then," Mara said.

"Alas, I cannot! The tyrant keeps me being! I curse him! Now let him kill me!"

The words jetted out like a dying fountain.

"Had he not made you," Mara said gently, "you could not even hate him. But he did not make you such. You have made yourself what you are. Be of better cheer: he can remake you."

"I will not be remade!"

"He will not change you; he will only restore you to what you were."

"I will not be aught of his making."

"Are you not willing to have that set right which you have set wrong?"

Silence stretched, the suffering abating slightly.

"If you are willing, put yourself again on the settle."

"I will not," she answered, forcing the words through clenched teeth.

A wind woke inside the house, blowing without sound or impact. Cold water rose, smooth and silent, lifting her gently until she floated onto the settle, unable to resist. Then it sank away.

The internal strife resumed, fiercer than before. Lilith's soul lay naked to the torture of pure light. She moaned, sighed, and murmured in a strange, shadowy language that touched upon the disparted manuscripts of Adam and forces I did not recognize. Her queendom was divided; she exulted over her enemies and wept for friends she hated.

"Those are not the tears of repentance!" Mara observed. "Self-loathing is not sorrow. Yet it is good, for it marks a step in the way home."

"Will you restore that which you have wrongfully taken?" Mara asked again.

"I have taken nothing," Lilith spat, pain twisting her face. "My power to take manifested my right."

Mara left her. Gradually, an invisible darkness enveloped us, a positive Nothingness that bordered on absolute Death. It was not the absence of everything, but the presence of void. Lilith dashed herself from the settle to the floor with a bitter cry—the recoil of Being from Annihilation.

"For pity's sake," she shrieked, "tear my heart out, but let me live!"

Then, perfect calm fell upon us, as of a summer night. The cup of her suffering had overflowed, and a hand had emptied it. She rose, standing erect with the air of a conqueror. She had met her spiritual foes and driven them back. She raised her withered arm in a pæan of unholy triumph—until her eyes fixed in a ghastly stare.

Before her, cast from an unseen mirror, stood the reflection of herself, and beside it, a form of splendent beauty. She trembled and sank to the floor. She knew the one: what God had intended her to be. She knew the other: what she had made herself.

For the rest of the night, she lay motionless. With the gray dawn, she rose, turning to Mara with a prideful humility.

"You have conquered," she said. "Let me go into the wilderness and bewail myself."

Mara saw the submission was neither feigned nor real. She returned.

"Begin, then, and set right in the place of wrong."

"I know not how," Lilith replied, fear in her eyes.

"Open thy hand, and let that which is in it go."

A fierce refusal struggled within her, but she kept it imprisoned.

"I cannot," she said. "I have no longer the power. Open it for me."

She held out the offending hand. It was more a paw than a hand, clenched tight.

"You must open it yourself," Mara said quietly.

"I have told you I cannot!"

"You can if you will. At worst you could beat it open! Gather your strength."

"I will not try what I know impossible. It would be the part of a fool!"

"Which you have been playing all your life! Oh, you are hard to teach!"

Defiance flared on her face. She turned her back on Mara. "I know what you have been tormenting me for! You have not succeeded! I will yet be mistress of myself! I am still queen of Hell!"

Then came the most fearful thing of all. It was Life in Death—life dead, yet existent. Lilith had glimpsed it before, but never fully until now. As she stood rigid, a sense of cold crept out of her being, pervading it. The lamp of life and the eternal fire seemed to die together.

Something was taking place in her that we could not feel, yet we felt its misery. The source of life had withdrawn itself. All that remained was the dregs of her dead and corrupted existence. She was a live death, a conscious corpse whose coffin would never break. She had killed her life, and she knew it. She must DEATH IT forever. She had tried to unmake herself and failed; she was a dead life that could not cease. She must BE.

In her face, I saw dismay beyond manifestation. The light in her was darkness, shining after its own kind. She was what God could not have created. She had usurped her share in self-creation, and her part had undone His. She saw what she had made, and behold, it was not good. Her right hand was clenched upon existent Nothing—her inheritance.

But with God, all things are possible. He can save even the rich.

Without changing her look, Lilith walked toward Mara. She felt her coming and rose to meet her.

"I yield," the princess said. "I cannot hold out. I am defeated. Not the less, I cannot open my hand."

"Have you tried?"

"I am trying now with all my might."

"I will take you to my father. You have wronged him worst of the created, therefore he best of the created can help you."

"How can HE help me?"

"He will forgive you."

"Ah, if he would but help me to cease! Not even that am I capable of! I have no power over myself; I am a slave! Let me die."

"A slave thou art that shall one day be a child!" Mara answered. "Verily, thou shalt die, but not as thou thinkest. Thou shalt die out of death into life. Now is the Life for, that never was against thee!"

Like her mother, in whom lay the motherhood of all the world, Mara put her arms around Lilith and kissed her on the forehead. The fiery-cold misery went out of her eyes, and their fountains filled. She was lifted and borne to her own bed in a corner of the room, laid softly upon it, and covered with caressing hands.

Lilith lay and wept. The Lady of Sorrow went to the door and opened it. Morn, with the Spring in her arms, waited outside. Softly they stole in, bringing a gentle wind that rippled the unknown, upwaking sea of her eternal life. It flowed about her, healing the desert places of her heart. When Mara returned to sit by her bed, her tears were flowing softer than the rain, and soon she was fast asleep.

The Mother of Sorrows rose, muffling her face against the chill of the coming journey, and called to the Little Ones. They slept as though the night had never broken them, motionless and deep, but the moment her voice pierced the silence, they sprang to their feet, fresh as if newly made. Merrily down the stairs they followed her, a tide of small, eager limbs, until she brought them to the princess. The princess lay there, her tears still flowing as she slept, carving hollow channels down her cheeks. The glad faces of the children grew grave instantly. They looked from the weeping princess out onto the rain, then back at her, their innocence suddenly weighed down by wonder.

"The sky is falling!" cried one.

"The white juice is running out of the princess!" another wailed, awestruck.

"Is it rivers?" asked Odu, gazing at the little streams that flowed adown her hollow cheeks.

"Yes," answered Mara, her voice soft yet commanding, "--the most wonderful of all rivers."

"I thought rivers were bigger, and rushed, like a lot of Little Ones making loud noises!" he returned, casting a glance at me, the only one among them who had ever heard of such vast things.

"Look at the rivers of the sky!" Mara said, gesturing upward. "See how they come down to wake the waters under the earth! Soon will the rivers flow everywhere, merry and loud, like thousands of happy children. Oh, how glad they will make you, Little Ones! You have never seen any, and do not know how lovely water is!"

"That will be the gladness of the ground because the princess is grown good," said Odu. "See the gladness of the sky!"

"Are the rivers the gladness of the princess?" asked Luva. "They are not her juice, for they are not red!"

"They are the juice inside the juice," Mara answered simply.

Odu put one finger to his eye, examined it, and shook his head in confusion.

"Princess will not bite now!" declared Luva.

"No; she will never do that again," replied Mara. "--But now we must take her nearer home."

"Is that a nest?" asked Sozo, pointing to the space where they stood.

"Yes; a very big nest. But we must take her to another place first."

"What is that?"

"It is the biggest room in all this world. But I think it is going to be pulled down soon; it will be too full of little nests. Go and get your clumsies."

"Please, are there any cats in it?"

"Not one. The nests are too full of lovely dreams for one cat to get in."

"We shall be ready in a minute," said Odu, and ran out, followed by all except Luva.

Lilith was now awake, listening with a sad smile that did not reach her eyes.

"But her rivers are running so fast!" said Luva, standing by her side, unable to take his eyes from her face. "Her robe is all... I don't know what. The clumsies won't like it!"

"They won't mind it," answered Mara. "Those rivers are so clean that they make the whole world clean."

I had fallen asleep by the fire, but for some time had been awake and listening, and now I rose.

"It is time to mount, Mr. Vane," said our hostess.

"Tell me, please," I said, "is there not a way by which to avoid the channels and the den of monsters?"

"There is an easy way across the river-bed, which I will show you," she answered; "but you must pass once more through the monsters."

"I fear for the children," I said.

"Fear will not once come nigh them," she rejoined.

We left the cottage. The beasts stood waiting about the door. Odu was already on the neck of one of the two elephants destined to carry the princess. I mounted Lona's horse; Mara brought her body, and gave it to me in my arms. When she came out again with the princess, a cry of delight arose from the children: she was no longer muffled! Gazing at her, entranced by her loveliness, the boys forgot to receive the princess from her; but the elephants took Lilith tenderly with their trunks, one round her body and one round her knees, and, with Mara's help, laid her along between them.

"Why does the princess want to go?" asked a small boy. "She would keep good if she stayed here!"

"She wants to go, and she does not want to go: we are helping her," answered Mara. "She will not keep good here."

"What are you helping her to do?" he went on.

"To go where she will get more help—help to open her hand, which has been closed for a thousand years."

"So long? Then she has learned to do without it: why should she open it now?"

"Because it is shut upon something that is not hers."

"Please, Lady Mara, may we have some of your very dry bread before we go?" said Luva.

Mara smiled, and brought them four loaves and a great jug of water.

"We will eat as we go," they said. But they drank the water with delight.

"I think," remarked one of them, "it must be elephant-juice! It makes me so strong!"

We set out, the Lady of Sorrow walking with us, more beautiful than the sun, and the white leopardess following her. I thought she meant but to put us in the path across the channels, but I soon found she was going with us all the way. Then I would have dismounted that she might ride, but she would not let me.

"I have no burden to carry," she said. "The children and I will walk together."

It was the loveliest of mornings; the sun shone his brightest, and the wind blew his sweetest, but they did not comfort the desert, for it had no water.

We crossed the channels without difficulty, the children gamboling about Mara all the way, but did not reach the top of the ridge over the bad burrow until the sun was already in the act of disappearing. Then I made the Little Ones mount their elephants, for the moon might be late, and I could not help some anxiety about them.

The Lady of Sorrow now led the way by my side; the elephants followed—the two that bore the princess in the centre; the leopardess brought up the rear; and just as we reached the frightful margin, the moon looked up and showed the shallow basin lying before us untroubled. Mara stepped into it; not a movement answered her tread or the feet of my horse. But the moment that the elephants carrying the princess touched it, the seemingly solid earth began to heave and boil, and the whole dread brood of the hellish nest was commoved. Monsters uprose on all sides, every neck at full length, every beak and claw outstretched, every mouth agape. Long-billed heads, horribly jawed faces, knotty tentacles innumerable, went out after Lilith. She lay in an agony of fear, nor dared stir a finger. Whether the hideous things even saw the children, I doubt; certainly not one of them touched a child; not one loathly member passed the live rampart of her body-guard to lay hold of her.

"Little Ones," I cried, "keep your elephants close about the princess. Be brave; they will not touch you."

"What will not touch us? We don't know what to be brave at!" they answered; and I perceived they were unaware of the deformities around them.

"Never mind then," I returned; "only keep close."

They were panoplied in their blindness! Incapacity to see was their safety. What they could nowise be aware of, could not hurt them.

But the hideous forms I saw that night! Mara was a few paces in front of me when a solitary, bodiless head bounced on the path between us. The leopardess came rushing under the elephants from behind and would have seized it, but, with frightful contortions of visage and a loathsome howl, it gave itself a rapid rotatory twist, sprang from her, and buried itself in the ground. The death in my arms assuaging me from fear, I regarded them all unmoved, although never, surely, was elsewhere beheld such a crew accursed!

Mara still went in front of me, and the leopardess now walked close behind her, shivering often, for it was very cold, when suddenly the ground before me to my left began to heave, and a low wave of earth came slinking toward us. It rose higher as it drew near; out of it slouched a dreadful head with fleshy tubes for hair, and opening a great oval mouth, snapped at me. The leopardess sprang, but fell baffled beyond it.

Almost under our feet, shot up the head of an enormous snake, with a lamping, wallowing glare in its eyes. Again the leopardess rushed to the attack, but found nothing. At a third monster she darted with like fury, and like failure—then sullenly ceased to heed the phantom-horde. But I understood the peril and hastened the crossing—the rather that the moon was carrying herself strangely. Even as she rose she seemed ready to drop and give up the attempt as hopeless; and since, I saw her sink back once fully her own breadth. The arc she made was very low, and now she had begun to descend rapidly.

We were almost over, when, between us and the border of the basin, arose a long neck, on the top of which, like the blossom of some Stygian lily, sat what seemed the head of a corpse, its mouth half open and full of canine teeth. I went on; it retreated, then drew aside. The lady stepped on the firm land, but the leopardess between us, roused once more, turned, and flew at the throat of the terror. I remained where I was to see the elephants, with the princess and the children, safe on the bank. Then I turned to look after the leopardess. That moment the moon went down. For an instant I saw the leopardess and the snake-monster convolved in a cloud of dust; then darkness hid them. Trembling with fright, my horse wheeled, and in three bounds overtook the elephants.

As we came up with them, a shapeless jelly dropped on the princess. A white dove dropped immediately on the jelly, stabbing it with its beak. It made a squelching, sucking sound, and fell off. Then I heard the voice of a woman talking with Mara, and I knew the voice.

"I fear she is dead!" said Mara.

"I will send and find her," answered the mother. "But why, Mara, shouldst thou at all fear for her or for any one? Death cannot hurt her who dies doing the work given her to do."

"I shall miss her sorely; she is good and wise. Yet I would not have her live beyond her hour!"

"She has gone down with the wicked; she will rise with the righteous. We shall see her again ere very long."

"Mother," I said, although I did not see her, "we come to you many, but most of us are Little Ones. Will you be able to receive us all?"

"You are welcome every one," she answered. "Sooner or later all will be little ones, for all must sleep in my house! It is well with those that go to sleep young and willing!--My husband is even now preparing her couch for Lilith. She is neither young nor quite willing, but it is well indeed that she is come."

I heard no more. Mother and daughter had gone away together through the dark. But we saw a light in the distance, and toward it we went stumbling over the moor.

Adam stood in the door, holding the candle to guide us, and talking with his wife, who, behind him, laid bread and wine on the table within.

"Happy children," I heard her say, "to have looked already on the face of my daughter! Surely it is the loveliest in the great world!"

When we reached the door, Adam welcomed us almost merrily. He set the candle on the threshold, and going to the elephants, would have taken the princess to carry her in; but she repulsed him, and pushing her elephants asunder, stood erect between them. They walked from beside her, and left her with him who had been her husband—ashamed indeed of her gaunt uncomeliness, but unsubmissive. He stood with a welcome in his eyes that shone through their severity.

"We have long waited for thee, Lilith!" he said.

She returned him no answer.

Eve and her daughter came to the door.

"The mortal foe of my children!" murmured Eve, standing radiant in her beauty.

"Your children are no longer in her danger," said Mara; "she has turned from evil."

"Trust her not hastily, Mara," answered her mother; "she has deceived a multitude!"

"But you will open to her the mirror of the Law of Liberty, mother, that she may go into it, and abide in it! She consents to open her hand and restore: will not the great Father restore her to inheritance with His other children?"

"I do not know Him!" murmured Lilith, in a voice of fear and doubt.

"Therefore it is that thou art miserable," said Adam.

"I will go back whence I came!" she cried, and turned, wringing her hands, to depart.

"That is indeed what I would have thee do, where I would have thee go—to Him from whom thou camest! In thy agony didst thou not cry out for Him?"

"I cried out for Death—to escape Him and thee!"

"Death is even now on his way to lead thee to Him. Thou knowest neither Death nor the Life that dwells in Death! Both befriend thee. I am dead, and would see thee dead, for I live and love thee. Thou art weary and heavy-laden: art thou not ashamed? Is not the being thou hast corrupted become to thee at length an evil thing? Wouldst

I turned to Eve and spoke, my voice trembling with a mixture of shame and desperate need. "Mother, there is an empty couch beside Lona. I know I am unworthy, yet may I not spend this night among the dead? Forgive my cowardice and my arrogance; take me in. I am sick of myself and crave only the silence of sleep."

"The couch beside Lona is prepared for you," she replied, her tone gentle but firm. "Yet, something must be done before you rest."

"I am ready," I said.

"How do you know you possess the strength to do it?" she asked, a faint smile playing on her lips.

"Because you require it," I answered. "What is the task?"

She turned to Adam. "Is he forgiven, husband?"

"From my heart," Adam replied.

"Then tell him what he must do."

Adam stepped forward and took Mara's hand, holding it tenderly in his lap. "Come, my child, let us go to the cottage. There, I shall instruct you."

As we moved away, a sudden, violent gust of wind battered the roof, accompanied by a great flapping noise, only to die away into a deep, mournful sigh. Once the door of the death-chamber was shut behind us, Adam sat, and I stood before him, waiting.

"You will remember," he began, his voice steady, "how, after leaving your mother's house, you arrived at a dry rock marked by the scars of an ancient waterfall? You climbed it and found a sandy desert. Go to that rock now. From its summit, walk deep into the desert. Do not take many steps before lying down and placing your head upon the sand. If you hear the murmur of water beneath, proceed a little farther and listen again. If the sound persists, you are on the right path. Every few yards, stop, lie down, and hearken. Should you ever hear no sound, you have strayed; you must listen in every direction until the water calls you again. Follow the sound, never retracing your steps, and it will grow louder, leading you to its source. That is the spot you seek. Dig there with the spade I will give you until you reach moisture. Place the hand within, cover it flush with the desert surface, and return home."

He handed me the hand, then produced a spade. "This is my gardening spade," he said softly. "With it, I have brought many lovely things into the sun."

I took the tools and stepped out into the night. The air was biting cold, and darkness was absolute. Falling would have been a terror, and the path ahead seemed treacherous even in daylight. Yet, I had not undertaken this task lightly. The moment I started, I realized I was guided by no chance. A pale light erupted from the ground with every step, illuminating exactly where to place my foot. Through the heather and low rocks, I walked without a single stumble. The bad burrow was utterly still; no waves rose, no heads appeared as I crossed it.

A moon rose, casting a silver glow that revealed the easy way forward. By morning, I was nearly past the dry channels of the river's first branch, judging I was close to Mara's cottage.

The moon hung low, the sun not yet risen, when I saw ahead, where the path narrowed between rocks, a figure veiled from head to foot in moonlit mist. I pressed on as if blind to it. The figure threw aside its veil.

"Have you forgotten me already?" the voice asked—princess, or something pretending to be one.

I neither hesitated nor answered; I walked straight on.

"You meant then to leave me in that horrible sepulchre! Do you not understand that where I please to be, there I am? Take my hand; I am alive as you!"

I was on the verge of saying, "Give me your left hand," but I caught myself, held my peace, and advanced steadily.

"Give me my hand!" she shrieked suddenly, "or I will tear you in pieces: you are mine!"

She flung herself upon me. I shuddered but did not falter. Nothing touched me, and she vanished from sight.

With measured tread, a body of armed men filled the path ahead. I walked through them—I knew not if they parted for me or were mere phantoms. They turned and followed, their march audible and felt at my heels, but I cast no look behind. Their steps and the clash of armor faded into the night.

A little farther, where the moon dipped near the horizon and shadows deepened, I saw a woman seated across the narrow path, her face muffled.

"Ah," she said, "you are come at last! I have waited an hour or more. You have done well! Your trial is over. My father sent me to meet you so you might rest. Give me your charge, and lay your head in my lap; I will guard both until the sun rises. I am not always bitterness, nor to all men!"

Her words were a terrible temptation, for I was exhausted. What was more likely to be true? If I were to trample the very person of the Lady of Sorrow underfoot through slavish obedience and a lack of pure insight, my heart would break. My heart grew faint, then hammered against my ribs as if to burst.

Nevertheless, my will hardened against my heart, and my step did not falter. I bit my tongue to keep from answering and pressed on. If Adam had sent her, he could not complain of my refusal. Nor would the Lady of Sorrow love me less for the fact that even she could not turn me aside.

Just as I reached the phantom, she pulled the covering from her face. Great indeed was her loveliness, but those were not Mara's eyes! No lie could truly or for long imitate them. I advanced as if she were not there, and my foot found empty space.

I had almost reached the other side when a Shadow barred my way. I believe it was The Shadow. He wore what looked like a helmet, but as I drew closer, I saw it was his own head—distorted beyond recognition, bearing only a doubtful resemblance to humanity. A cold, dank wind smote me, sickening and repulsive as the air of a charnel house. Firmness left my joints, and my limbs trembled as if they would collapse into a helpless heap. I seemed to pass through him, though perhaps he passed through me; for a moment, I was one of the damned. Then, a soft wind, like the first breath of a new spring, greeted me, and before me arose the dawn.

My path now led past the door of Mara's cottage. It stood wide open, and upon the table lay a loaf of bread and a pitcher of water. There was no howl, no wail within or around the cottage.

I reached the precipice that testified to the vanished river. I climbed its worn face and entered the desert. Finally, after much listening back and forth, I determined the spot where the hidden water roared loudest. I hung Lilith's hand about my neck and began to dig. It was long labor, for the sand was loose and required a large hole, but eventually, I threw up a damp spadeful. I tossed the sexton-tool onto the edge and laid the hand down. Water began to ooze from beneath its fingers. I sprang out and hastened to fill the grave. Utterly fatigued, I dropped beside it and fell asleep.

When consciousness returned, the earth beneath me felt damp and yielding, my path to the grave hardening into quicksand. The ancient river swelled in its bed, pushing against its banks with a rising roar. Soon, it would cascade over the precipice, splitting into twin torrents—one to reclaim the orchard valley, the other to potentially drown the monster horde, leaving the Evil Wood isolated between them. With this knowledge settling in my mind, I turned immediately toward the home of those who had sent me.

Reaching the precipice, I threaded my way through the branches, driven by a lingering hope to visit Mara's cottage one last time before the final sleep. I knew the crossing points even if the river had already claimed them. Yet, when I arrived, the door stood ajar. Inside, bread and water remained untouched on the table, swallowed by a profound, heavy silence. I called out, my voice echoing into the void, but received no answer. I turned away, the silence pressing against my ears.

A short distance further, I found a gray-haired man sitting on the sand, weeping openly.

"What troubles you, sir?" I asked gently. "Have you been abandoned?"

"I weep," he replied, his voice thick with grief, "because they will not let me die. I have gone to the House of Death, and its mistress, despite my age, refuses me. If you know her, intercede for me."

"Nay, sir," I answered. "I cannot. She refuses none whom it is lawful for her to receive."

"How do you know this?" he demanded. "You have never sought death yourself! You are far too young to desire it!"

"I fear your words suggest that, if you were young again, neither would you desire it."

"Indeed, young sir, I would not! And I am certain you cannot."

"I may not be old enough to wish to die, but I am young enough to wish to live! Therefore, I go now to learn if she will eventually take me in. You wish to die because you do not care to live; she will not open her door to you, for no one can die who does not long to live."

"It ill becomes your youth to mock a friendless old man. Please, cease your riddles!"

"Did not the Mother tell you something similar?"

"In truth, I believe she did; but I paid little heed to her excuses."

"Ah, then, sir," I rejoined, "it is plain you have not yet learned to die, and I am heartbroken for you. Such was I, too, but for the Lady of Sorrow. I am indeed young, yet I have wept many tears. Pardon me if I presume to offer counsel: Go to the Lady of Sorrow, and 'take with both hands' what she offers. Her cottage lies yonder. She is not inside now, but her door stands open, and bread and water rest on her table. Enter, sit, eat, drink, and wait for her appearance. Then seek her counsel, for she is true, and her wisdom is vast."

He broke into fresh weeping, and I left him sobbing. Whether he understood my words, I feared not. But Mara would surely find him.

Sunset had fallen, and the moon had not yet risen, when I reached the abode of the monsters. The place lay as still as stone until I crossed the threshold, after which a noise of rushing waters erupted, accompanied by a great cry behind me that I ignored.

Before reaching the House of Death, the cold became biting and the darkness absolute; they merged into one, seeping into my bones. Yet, the candle of Eve, glowing from the window, guided me, keeping frost and murk at bay within my heart.

The door stood open, revealing an empty cottage. I sat down, disconsolate.

A profound loneliness seized me, unlike any I had felt in my wanderings. Thousands were near, yet none were with me. True, they were alive while I was dead, but whether by their life or my death, we were severed. They lived, yet I was not dead enough to perceive them as living; doubt gnawed at me. They were distant, and I had no helper to lay me beside them.

Never before had I known such desolation. I chided myself, telling myself the solitude was merely an illusion: I was awake, and they slept—that was all. They lay so still, silent, yet they were with me now, and soon, very soon, I would be with them.

I dropped Adam's old spade, and the dull thud of its fall on the clay floor seemed to reverberate from the chamber beyond. A childish terror gripped me. I sat staring at the coffin door. But Father Adam, Mother Eve, Sister Mara would come soon, and then—welcome the cold world and the white neighbors! I pushed aside my fears, lived a moment more, and loved my dead.

Something moved in the chamber of the dead. It emitted a sound like a dim, far-off vibration, yet it was not what I knew as sound. My soul leaped into my ears. Was it a mere thrill of the dead air, too faint to be heard but quivering in every spiritual sense? I KNEW without hearing, without feeling it!

The presence was coming! It drew nearer! Within my desertion, an infant hope awoke. The noiseless thrill reached the coffin door, became sound, and struck my ear.

The door began to move, with a low, soft creak of its hinges. It was opening! I ceased listening, staring expectantly.

It opened a fraction, and a face appeared in the gap. It was Lona's. Her eyes were closed, yet the face itself seemed to see me. It was white as Eve's, white as Mara's, but it did not shine like theirs. She spoke, and her voice was like a sleepy night-wind in the grass.

"Are you coming, king?" she said. "I cannot rest until you are with me, gliding down the river to the great sea and the beautiful dream-land. The sleepiness is full of lovely things: come and see them."

"Oh, my darling!" I cried. "Had I but known! I thought you were dead!"

She lay upon my bosom, cold as ice frozen to marble. She threw her arms, so white and feeble, around me and sighed.

"Carry me back to my bed, king. I want to sleep."

I bore her to the death-chamber, holding her tight lest she dissolve from my grasp. Unaware that I was being watched, I carried her straight to her couch.

"Lay me down," she whispered, "and cover me from the warm air; it hurts—a little. Your bed is there, next to mine. I shall see you when I wake."

She was already asleep. I threw myself onto my couch, blessed as no man has been on the eve of his wedding.

"Come, sweet cold," I murmured, "and still my heart speedily."

Instead, a glimmer of light filled the chamber, and I saw Father Adam approaching. He held no candle, yet I saw him clearly. Beside Lona's couch, he looked down upon her with a questioning smile, then greeted me across the space between us.

"We have climbed to the top of the hill to hear the waters on their way," he said. "They will reach the den of the monsters tonight. But why did you not await our return?"

"My child could not sleep," I answered.

"She is fast asleep!" he rejoined.

"Yes, now!" I said. "But she was awake when I laid her down."

"She was asleep all the time!" he insisted. "She was perhaps dreaming about you—and came to you?"

"She did."

"And did you not see that her eyes were closed?"

"Now I think of it, I did."

"If you had looked before you laid her down, you would have seen her asleep on the couch."

"That would have been terrible!"

"You would only have found that she was no longer in your arms."

"That would have been worse!"

"It is, perhaps, to think of; but to see it would not have troubled you."

"Dear father," I said, "how is it that I am not sleepy? I thought I should go to sleep like the Little Ones the moment I laid my head down!"

"Your hour is not quite come. You must have food ere you sleep."

"Oh, I ought not to have lain down without your leave, for I cannot sleep without your help! I will get up at once!"

But I found my own weight more than I could move.

"There is no need: we will serve you here," he answered. "Do you not feel cold?"

"Not too cold to lie still, but perhaps too cold to eat!"

He came to the side of my couch, bent over me, and breathed upon my heart. Instantly, I was warm.

As he left me, I heard a voice and knew it was the Mother's. She was singing, and her song was sweet, soft, and low. I thought she sat by my bed in the dark; but before it ceased, her song soared aloft, seeming to come from the throat of a woman-angel, high above all the region of larks, higher than man had ever lifted his heart. I heard every word she sang, yet kept only this:

I thought I had heard this song before.

Then the three came to my couch together, bringing me bread and wine. I sat up to partake. Adam stood on one side of me, Eve and Mara on the other.

"You are good indeed, father Adam, mother Eve, sister Mara," I said, "to receive me! In my soul, I am ashamed and sorry!"

"We knew you would come again!" answered Eve.

"How could you know it?" I returned.

"Because here was I, born to look after my brothers and sisters!" Mara answered with a smile.

"Every creature must one night yield himself and lie down," Adam replied. "He was made for liberty, and must not be left a slave!"

"It will be late, I fear, ere all have lain down!" I said.

"There is no early or late here," he rejoined. "For him the true time then first begins who lays himself down. Men are not coming home fast; women are coming faster. A desert, wide and dreary, parts him who lies down to die from him who lies down to live. The former may well make haste, but here is no haste."

"To our eyes," said Eve, "you were coming all the time: we knew Mara would find you, and you must come!"

"How long is it since my father lay down?" I asked.

"I have told you that years are of no consequence in this house," Adam answered. "We do not heed them. Your father will wake when his morning comes. Your mother, next to whom you are lying—"

"Oh, then, it IS my mother!" I exclaimed.

"Yes—she with the wounded hand," he assented. "She will be up and away long ere your morning is ripe."

"I am sorry."

"Rather be glad."

"It must be a sight for God Himself to see such a woman come awake!"

"It is indeed a sight for God, a sight that makes her Maker glad! He sees of the travail of His soul, and is satisfied! Look at her once more, and sleep."

He let the rays of his candle fall upon her beautiful face.

"She looks much younger!" I said.

"She IS much younger," he replied. "Even Lilith already begins to look younger!"

I lay down, blissfully drowsy.

"But when you see your mother again," he continued, "you will not at first know her. She will go on steadily growing younger until she reaches the perfection of her womanhood—a splendour beyond foresight. Then she will open her eyes, behold on one side her husband, on the other her son—and rise and leave them to go to a father and a brother more to her than they."

I heard as one in a dream. I was very cold, but the cold caused me no suffering. I felt them place upon me the white garment of the dead. Then I forgot everything. The night about me was pale with sleeping faces, but I was asleep also, nor did I know that I slept.

I became aware of existence, and with it, the profound, infinite cold. I felt intensely blessed—more blessed than my heart, now imagining, can recall. I could not think of warmth with the slightest suggestion of pleasure. I knew I had enjoyed it, but I could not remember how. The cold had soothed every care, dissolved every pain, comforted every sorrow. Comforted? Nay; sorrow was swallowed up in the life drawing nigh to restore every good and lovely thing a hundredfold. I lay at peace, full of the quietest expectation, breathing the damp odours of Earth's bountiful bosom, aware of the souls of primroses, daisies, and snowdrops, patiently waiting in it for the Spring.

How to convey the delight of that frozen, yet conscious sleep! I had no more to stand up; I had only to lie stretched out and still. How cold I was, words cannot tell; yet I grew colder and colder—and welcomed the cold yet more and more. I grew continuously less conscious of myself, continuously more conscious of bliss, unimaginable yet felt. I had neither made it nor prayed for it; it was mine in virtue of existence, and existence was mine in virtue of a Will that dwelt within me.

Then the dreams began to arrive, and they came crowding. I lay naked on a snowy peak. The white mist heaved below me like a billowy sea. The cold moon was in the air with me, and above the moon and me the colder sky, in which the moon and I dwelt. I was Adam, waiting for God to breathe into my nostrils the breath of life. I was not Adam, but a child in the bosom of a mother white with a radiant whiteness. I was a youth on a white horse, leaping from cloud to cloud of a blue heaven, hasting calmly to some blessed goal. For centuries I dreamed—or was it chiliads? Or only one long night? But why ask? For time had nothing to do with me; I was in the land of thought—farther in, higher up than the seven dimensions, the ten senses. I think I was where I am—in the heart of God. I dreamed away dim cycles in the centre of a melting glacier, the spectral moon drawing nearer and nearer, the wind and the welter of a torrent growing in my ears. I lay and heard them: the wind and the water and the moon sang a peaceful waiting for a redemption drawing nigh. I dreamed cycles, I say, but, for aught I knew or can tell, they were the solemn, æonian march of a second, pregnant with eternity.

Then, of a sudden, but not once troubling my conscious bliss, all the wrongs I had ever done, from far beyond my earthly memory down to the present moment, were with me. Fully in every wrong lived the conscious I, confessing, abjuring, lamenting the dead, making atonement with each person I had injured, hurt, or offended. Every human soul to which I had caused a troubled thought was now grown unspeakably dear to me, and I humbled myself before it, agonizing to cast from between us the clinging offence. I wept at the feet of the mother whose commands I had slighted; with bitter shame I confessed to my father that I had told him two lies, and long forgotten them; now for long had remembered them, and kept them in memory to crush at last at his feet. I was the eager slave of all whom I had thus or anyhow wronged. Countless services I devised to render them! For this one I would build such a house as had never grown from the ground! For that one I would train such horses as had never yet been seen in any world! For a third I would make such a garden as had never bloomed, haunted with still pools, and alive with running waters! I would write songs to make their hearts swell, and tales to make them glow! I would turn the forces of the world into such channels of invention as to make them laugh with the joy of wonder! Love possessed me! Love was my life! Love was to me, as to Him that made me, all in all!

Suddenly I found myself in a solid blackness, upon which the ghost of light that dwells in the caverns of the eyes could not cast one fancied glimmer. But my heart, which feared nothing and hoped infinitely, was full of peace. I lay imagining what the light would be when it came, and what new creation it would bring with it—when, suddenly, without conscious volition, I sat up and stared about me.

The moon was looking in at the lowest, horizontal, crypt-like windows of the death-chamber, her long light slanting, I thought, across the fallen, but still ripening sheaves of the harvest of the great husbandman. But no; that harvest was gone! Gathered in, or swept away by chaotic storm, not a sacred sheaf was there! My dead were gone! I was alone! In desolation dread lay depths yet deeper than I had hitherto known! Had there never been any ripening dead? Had I but dreamed them and their loveliness? Why then these walls? Why the empty couches? No; they were all up! They were all abroad in the new eternal day, and had forgotten me! They had left me behind, and alone! Tenfold more terrible was the tomb its inhabitants away! The quiet ones had made me quiet with their presence—they had pervaded my mind with their blissful peace; now I had no friend, and my lovers were far from me! A moment I sat and stared horror-stricken. I had been alone with the moon on a mountain top in the sky; now I was alone with her in a huge cenotaph: she too was staring about, seeking her dead with ghastly gaze! I sprang to my feet, and staggered from the fearful place.

The cottage was empty. I ran out into the night.

No moon was there! Even as I left the chamber, a cloudy rampart had risen and covered her. But a broad shimmer came from far over the heath, mingled with a ghostly murmuring music, as if the moon were raining a light that plashed as it fell.
