I ran stumbling across the moor, and found a lovely lake, margined with reeds and rushes: the moon behind the cloud was gazing upon the monsters' den, full of clearest, brightest water, and very still. But the musical murmur went on, filling the quiet air, and drawing me after it.

I walked round the border of the little mere, and climbed the range of hills. What a sight rose to my eyes! The whole expanse where, with hot, aching feet, I had crossed and recrossed the deep-scored channels and ravines of the dry river-bed, was alive with streams, with torrents, with still pools—a river deep and wide! How the moon flashed on the water! How the water answered the moon with flashes of its own—white flashes breaking everywhere from its rock-encountered flow! And a great jubilant song arose from its bosom, the song of new-born liberty. I stood a moment gazing, and my heart also began to exult: my life was not all a failure! I had helped to set this river free! My dead were not lost! I had but to go after and find them! I would follow and follow until I came whither they had gone! Our meeting might be thousands of years away, but at last—AT LAST I should hold them! Wherefore else did the floods clap their hands?

I hurried down the hill: my pilgrimage was begun! In what direction to turn my steps I knew not, but I must go and go till I found my living dead! A torrent ran swift and wide at the foot of the range: I rushed in, it laid no hold upon me; I waded through it. The next I sprang across; the third I swam; the next I waded again.

I stopped to gaze on the wondrous loveliness of the ceaseless flash and flow, and to hearken to the multitudinous broken music. Every now and then some incipient air would seem about to draw itself clear of the dulcet confusion, only to merge again in the consorted roar. At moments the world of waters would invade as if to overwhelm me—not with the force of its seaward rush, or the shouting of its liberated throng, but with the greatness of the silence wandering into sound.

As I stood lost in delight, a hand was laid on my shoulder. I turned, and saw a man in the prime of strength, beautiful as if fresh from the heart of the glad creator, young like him who cannot grow old. I looked: it was Adam. He stood large and grand, clothed in a white robe, with the moon in his hair.

"Father," I cried, "where is she? Where are the dead? Is the great resurrection come and gone? The terror of my loneliness was upon me; I could not sleep without my dead; I ran from the desolate chamber. Whither shall I go to find them?"

"You mistake, my son," he answered, in a voice whose very breath was consolation. "You are still in the chamber of death, still upon your couch, asleep and dreaming, with the dead around you."

"Alas! when I but dream, how am I to know it? The dream best dreamed is the likest to the waking truth!"

"When you are quite dead, you will dream no false dream. The soul that is true can generate nothing that is not true, neither can the false enter it."

"But, sir," I faltered, "how am I to distinguish betwixt the true and the false where both alike seem real?"

"Do you not understand?" he returned, with a smile that might have slain all the sorrows of all his children. "You CANNOT perfectly distinguish between the true and the false while you are not yet quite dead; neither indeed will you when you are quite dead—that is, quite alive, for then the false will never present itself. At this moment, believe me, you are on your bed in the house of death."

"I am trying hard to believe you, father. I do indeed believe you, although I can neither see nor feel the truth of what you say."

"You are not to blame that you cannot. And because even in a dream you believe me, I will help you. Put forth your left hand open, and close it gently: it will clasp the hand of your Lona, who lies asleep where you lie dreaming you are awake."

I put forth my hand: it closed on the hand of Lona, firm and soft and deathless.

"But, father," I cried, "she is warm!"

"Your hand is as warm to hers. Cold is a thing unknown in our country. Neither she nor you are yet in the fields of home, but each to each is alive and warm and healthful."

Then my heart was glad. But immediately supervened a sharp-stinging doubt.

"Father," I said, "forgive me, but how am I to know surely that this also is not a part of the lovely dream in which I am now walking with thyself?"

"Thou doubtest because thou lovest the truth. Some would willingly believe life but a phantasm, if only it might for ever afford them a world of pleasant dreams: thou art not of such! Be content for a while not to know surely. The hour will come, and that ere long, when, being true, thou shalt behold the very truth, and doubt will be for ever dead. Scarce, then, wilt thou be able to recall the features of the phantom. Thou wilt then know that which thou canst not now dream. Thou hast not yet looked the Truth in the face, hast as yet at best but seen him through a cloud. That which thou seest not, and never didst see save in a glass darkly—that which, indeed, never can be known save by its innate splendour shining straight into pure eyes—that thou canst not but doubt, and art blameless in doubting until thou seest it face to face, when thou wilt no longer be able to doubt it. But to him who has once seen even a shadow only of the truth, and, even but hoping he has seen it when it is present no longer, tries to obey it—to him the real vision, the Truth himself, will come, and depart no more, but abide with him for ever."

"I think I see, father," I said; "I think I understand."

"Then remember, and recall. Trials yet await thee, heavy, of a nature thou knowest not now. Remember the things thou hast seen. Truly thou knowest not those things, but thou knowest what they have seemed, what they have meant to thee! Remember also the things thou shalt yet see. Truth is all in all; and the truth of things lies, at once hid and revealed, in their seeming."

"How can that be, father?" I said, and raised my eyes with the question; for I had been listening with downbent head, aware of nothing but the voice of Adam.

He was gone; in my ears was nought but the sounding silence of the swift-flowing waters. I stretched forth my hands to find him, but no answering touch met their seeking. I was alone—alone in the land of dreams! To myself I seemed wide awake, but I believed I was in a dream, because he had told me so.

Even in a dream, however, the dreamer must do something! He cannot sit down and refuse to stir until the dream grow weary of him and depart: I took up my wandering, and went on.

Many channels I crossed, and came to a wider space of rock; there, dreaming I was weary, I laid myself down, and longed to be awake.

I was about to rise and resume my journey, when I discovered that I lay beside a pit in the rock, whose mouth was like that of a grave. It was deep and dark; I could see no bottom.

Now in the dreams of my childhood I had found that a fall invariably woke me, and would, therefore, when desiring to discontinue a dream, seek some eminence whence to cast myself down that I might wake: with one glance at the peaceful heavens, and one at the rushing waters, I rolled myself over the edge of the pit.

For a moment

The fourth night dissolved into a profound slumber, only to shatter into a terrifying clarity when I opened my eyes. Darkness enveloped the room, yet I knew with absolute certainty that I lay within the House of Death. Every moment since my arrival had been a dream, and in this instant, I was truly awake. "At last!" I whispered to my own heart, feeling it leap with a joyous, electric pulse. I turned my gaze, and there she stood by my couch, waiting. Lona. I had never truly lost her—merely the sight of her for a fleeting span. My grief had been unnecessary.

Though the room was dark, she was not. She burned with the radiance of the Mother, a light issuing from her face and pouring through her death-dress, which now glowed white as fresh snow, tenfold brighter in the power of resurrection. She had fallen asleep as a girl; she awoke as a woman, ripe with the essential loveliness of life itself. I folded her into my arms, and the sensation confirmed my existence: I lived.

"I woke first," she said, her voice trembling with a wondering smile.

"You did, my love, and you woke me," I replied.

"I only looked at you and waited," she answered softly.

A candle drifted through the gloom, carried by unseen hands, until Adam, Eve, and Mara appeared. They greeted us with a quiet, familiar good morning, their smiles etched with the familiarity of those who witness such awakenings daily.

"I hope you found the darkness pleasant," the Mother said gently.

"Not entirely," I admitted. "But the waking from it is heavenly."

"It has barely begun," she rejoined. "You are hardly awake yet."

"He is at least clothed in Death," Adam observed, his tone grave yet warm. "Which is the radiant garment of Life."

He embraced Lona, his child, and placed an arm around me. He looked inquiringly at the princess, then patted the head of the leopardess resting nearby. "I think we shall meet you two again before long," he said, his gaze shifting between Lona and me.

"Must we die again?" I asked, fear tightening my chest.

"No," Adam answered, smiling with the same serene assurance as the Mother. "You have died into life, and you will die no more. You have only to remain dead. Once we die as we do here, all dying ends. Now you must live, with all your blessed might. The more you live, the stronger you become to live."

"But shall I not grow weary?" I asked. "What if I cease to live with all my might?"

"It requires only the will," the Mother said firmly. "The strength is already there! Pure life knows no weakness to cause weariness. THE Life keeps generating ours. Those who refuse to die die many times, constantly, sinking deeper into death forever. Here, everything moves upward, toward love and gladness."

She paused, her smile deepening into a look of profound understanding. "We are mother and son; we understand each other. Between us, no farewell is possible."

Mara kissed my forehead, her voice bright and buoyant. "I told you, brother, all would be well! When you need comfort next, say, 'What will be well, is even now well.'"

She sighed, a small, melancholic sound. I sensed her unspoken thought: *But they will not believe you.*

"--You know me now!" she finished, her smile mirroring her mother's.

"I know you!" I declared. "You are the voice crying in the wilderness before the Baptist arrived! You are the shepherd who hunts the wandering sheep home before the shadows rise and the night grows dark!"

"My work will one day end," she said. "And then I shall be glad with the gladness of the Great Shepherd who sent me."

"All night long, the morning is at hand," Adam intoned.

"What is that flapping of wings I hear?" I asked, startled.

"The Shadow hovers," Adam replied. "There is one here whom he claims as his own. But once ours, she can never be his again."

I turned to kiss my father and mother before we departed. Their couches were empty, save for the Little Ones who had boldly claimed their hospitality. For a split second, the awful dream of desolation returned, overshadowing me, and I turned away.

"What is it, my heart?" Lona asked, sensing my distress.

"Their empty places frightened me," I confessed.

"They left long ago," Adam said calmly. "They kissed you before they went and whispered, 'Come soon.'"

"And I could neither feel nor hear them!" I murmured, the loss feeling acute.

"How could you? You were far away in your dreary old house, thinking the dreadful place had reclaimed you once more! Now go and find them. Your parents, my child, must come and find you!"

The hour of departure approached. Lona moved to the couch of the mother who had slain her, kissing her tenderly before laying herself in her father's arms.

"That kiss will draw her homeward, my Lona!" Adam said.

"Who were her parents?" Lona asked, confused.

"My father," Adam answered simply, "is her father also."

She turned, placing her hand in mine.

I knelt and humbly thanked the three for aiding me in my death. Lona knelt beside me, and they all breathed upon us, sealing the transition.

"Hark! I hear the sun," Adam said suddenly.

I listened. It came with the rush of ten thousand wings multiplied by ten, accompanied by the roar of a molten, flaming world millions of miles away. Its approach was a crescendo of a hundred harmonies.

The three looked at one another and smiled. That smile floated heavenward like a three-petaled flower, a family offering of morning thanksgiving. From their mouths and faces, it spread over their bodies, shining through their garments. Before I could speak, Adam and Eve stood before me as the angels of resurrection, and Mara stood as the Magdalene at the sepulcher. Adam's countenance was like lightning; Eve held a napkin that scattered flakes of pure splendor across the room.

A wind began to moan in pulsing gusts.

"You hear his wings now!" Adam said, clarifying that he did not mean the wings of the morning.

"It is the great Shadow stirring to depart," he continued. "Wretched creature, he has himself within him and cannot rest!"

"But is there not something deeper within him?" I asked.

"Without a substance, a shadow cannot exist—yes, or without a light behind that substance!" he answered.

He listened for a moment, then called out with a glad smile. "Hark to the golden cock! Silent and motionless for millions of years, he has stood on the clock of the universe. Now, at last, he flaps his wings! Soon he will begin to crow, and at intervals, men will hear him until the dawn of the eternal day."

I listened. Far away, in the heart of an aeonian silence, I heard the clear, jubilant outcry of the golden throat. It hurled defiance at death and the dark, singing of infinite hope and coming calm. It was the "expectation of the creature" finally finding a voice; the cry of a chaos destined to become a kingdom!

Then came a great flapping.

"The black bat is flown!" Mara cried.

"Amen, golden cock, bird of God!" Adam shouted, and the words rang through the house of silence, ascending into the airy regions.

At his Amen, like doves rising on wings of silver from among potsherds, the Little Ones sprang to their knees on their beds, calling aloud, "Crow! Crow again, golden cock!" as if they had seen and heard him in their dreams. Then each turned to look at their sleeping bedfellows, gazing with loving eyes, kissing the silent companions of the night, and springing from the couches. The Little Ones who had lain beside my father and mother stared blankly and sadly at their empty spaces for a moment, then slid slowly to the floor. There, they fell into each other's arms, as if only then, through each other's eyes, were they assured they were alive and awake. Suddenly spotting Lona, they ran toward her, radiant with bliss, to embrace her. Odu, catching sight of the leopardess at the princess's feet, bounded to her side, threw an arm over the great sleeping head, and fondled it.

"Wake up, wake up, darling!" he cried. "It is time to wake!"

The leopardess did not move.

"She has slept herself cold!" he said to Mara, his face upturned in appealing consternation.

"She is waiting for the princess to wake, my child," Mara said gently.

Odu looked at the princess and saw that beside her, still asleep, lay two of his companions. He flew to them.

"Wake up! Wake up!" he cried, pushing and pulling this one and that.

Soon, however, he looked troubled and turned to me with misty eyes. "They will not wake!" he said. "And why are they so cold?"

"They too are waiting for the princess," I answered.

He stretched across and laid his hand on her face. "She is cold too! What is it?" he cried, looking around in wondering dismay.

Adam went to him. "Her wake is not ripe yet," he explained. "She is busy forgetting. When she has forgotten enough to remember enough, she will soon be ripe and wake."

"And remember?"

"Yes—but not too much at once, though."

"But the golden cock has crowed!" the child argued, falling back onto his companions.

"Peter! Peter! Crispy!" he cried. "Wake up, Peter! Wake up, Crispy! We are all awake but you two! The golden cock has crowed so loud! The sun is awake and coming! Oh, why won't you wake?"

But Peter would not wake, nor would Crispy. Odu wept outright at last.

"Let them sleep, darling!" Adam said soothingly. "Would you not like the princess to wake and find nobody? They are quite happy. So is the leopardess."

Comforted, Odu wiped his eyes as if he had spent his whole life weeping and wiping, though now, for the first time, he had tears to shed—tears that would soon be wiped away altogether.

We followed Eve to the cottage. There, she offered us neither bread nor wine but stood radiantly, desiring our departure. So, with not a word of farewell, we went out. The horse and the elephants waited at the door. We were too happy to mount them, and they followed us.

The darkness had lifted, replaced by a twilight so profound it felt like a second dawn. We walked through this dimness, inhaling the very breath of the spring, as a wondrous transformation settled over the world—or perhaps, more marvelously, within us. There was no sunlight to illuminate the path, yet every heather bush, every shrub, every blade of grass stood in perfect, radiant clarity. They glowed either from within, like the fire Moses witnessed in the desert, or reflected a light emanating from our own eyes. Shadows were nonexistent; instead, all things exchanged a gentle luminescence. Each living thing revealed its inner essence, its guiding thought, through its shape and color. My bare feet seemed to embrace the plants beneath them. The boundary between the microcosm and the macrocosm had dissolved; my life and the world's life were one. To perceive anything was to instantly know its existence and my own, to realize that we are all what we are because Another exists as He is. Senses long dormant awoke in me—sensations too vast for words, too pure for imagination. My consciousness expanded endlessly, a peaceful ocean upon which the swell of living joy raised new waves, yet remaining eternally the same joy in countless forms. Life was a cosmic holiday.

I knew now that life and truth were identical; that pure life is bliss itself, and where being lacks bliss, it is merely life-in-death. Every gust of the dark wind became a sigh of gratitude. At last, I existed fully, untouchable by harm. My darling walked beside me, and together we journeyed home to the Father.

What we possessed before the first sun rose upon our freedom was immense; what must the eternal day bring?

We arrived at the hollow where the earth's monsters once wallowed. As I had dreamed, it was now a lovely lake. Gazing into its pellucid depths, I saw a whirlpool had scourged away the soil where the abortions had burrowed, leaving the entire horrid brood visible at the bottom. A dim greenish light permeated the crystal water, illuminating every hideous form. Coiled in spires, folded in layers, or extended long and large, they weltered in motionless heaps—shapes more fantastic in their ghastly dismay than any fevered poet could conjure. Even the diver in the Maelstrom would find them incomparable in horror: tentacular convolutions, tumid bulges, glaring orbs of sepia deformity. Every head resembled a wicked flower bursting from an abominable stalk, perfecting its evil significance.

None moved as we passed, yet they were not dead. As long as men and women of unwholesome minds exist, this lake will remain populated with loathsomeness.

Then came the herald of the sun, the auroral wind, softly trumpeting his arrival. The master-minister of the human tabernacle was at hand. Heaving a huge, ripple-fretted wave of crimson and gold before his prow, he rushed aloft, as if newly launched from the Maker's hand into the upper sea. He paused, looking down upon the world—a white-raving storm of molten metals, yet nothing more than a coal from the altar of the Father's endless sacrifice to His children. See how every little flower straightened its stalk, lifted its neck, and stood expectant with outstretched head. Something greater than the sun, greater than light, was coming. None the less surely coming that the road was long. What mattered today, tomorrow, or ten thousand years to Life itself, to Love itself? He was coming, and the necks of all humanity were stretched to witness it. Every morning they would outstretch themselves; every evening they would droop in waiting—until He came. Was this but an air-drawn vision? Would He indeed find them watching thus when He arrived?

It was a glorious resurrection morning, the night spent in preparation for it.

The children gambolled ahead, followed by the beasts. Fluttering butterflies and darting dragonflies hovered and shot hither and thither around our heads, a cloud of colors and flashes. Sometimes they descended like a snowstorm of rainbow flakes; at other times, they rose into the humid air like a rolling vapor of embodied odors. It was a summer day more ideal than any man who had not died had ever known. I walked on the new earth under the new heaven, finding them the same as before, except now they opened their minds to me, and I saw into theirs. The soul of everything I met came forth to greet me and make friends, telling me we came from the same source and meant the same. "We are going to Him," they seemed to say, "with whom we always were, and whom we always meant." They were lightnings taking shape as they flashed from Him to His. The dark rocks drank the showering rays like sponges; the great world soaked up the light and sent out the living. Two joy-fires burned in Lona and me. Earth breathed heavenward her sweet-savoured smoke; we breathed homeward our longing desires. Our very consciousness was an act of thanksgiving.

We reached the channels, once so dry and weary, now running, flashing, and foaming with living water that shouted in its gladness. Far as the eye could see, it was a rushing, roaring, dashing river made vocal by its rocks.

We did not cross it but walked in glory and joy up its right bank until we reached the great cataract at the foot of the sandy desert, where the river roared and swirled, dropping sheer to divide into two branches. There we climbed the height—and found no desert. Through grassy plains, between grassy banks, flowed the deep, wide, silent river, full to the brim. For the first time, the glory of God was revealed to the Little Ones in the limpid flow of the water. Instinctively, they plunged and swam, and the beasts followed them.

The desert rejoiced and blossomed like a rose. Wide forests had sprung up, their undergrowth filled with flowering shrubs and songbirds. Every thicket gave birth to a rivulet, and every rivulet sang its water-song.

The place of the buried hand gave no sign. Beyond and still beyond, the river came in full volume from afar. Up and up we went, now along the grassy margin, now through forests of gracious trees. The grass grew sweeter, its flowers more lovely and varied; the trees grew larger, and the wind fuller of messages.

At length, we arrived at a forest whose trees were greater, grander, and more beautiful than any we had seen. Their living pillars upheaved a thick, embowed roof, between whose leaves and blossoms hardly a sunbeam filtered. Into the rafters of this aerial vault, the children climbed, scrambling and leaping in a land of bloom, shouting to the unseen elephants below and hearing them trumpet their replies. Lona understood these conversations while I guessed at them blunderingly. The Little Ones chased the squirrels, who frolicked and drew them on, always eventually allowing themselves to be caught and petted. Often, a bird, lovely in plumage and form, would light upon one of them, sing a song of what was coming, and fly away. Not one monkey of any sort could they see.

The shout tore through the canopy, a sudden explosion of sound that made the leaves tremble. Below, Lona and I paused, looking up as the Little Ones began their descent, tumbling from the high branches like playful sparks. They were breathless, their faces alight with a news that seemed to defy the laws of our world.

"We climbed the tallest one," a small voice gasped, "and we saw it! Far across the plain, rising from a solitary peak, there is something strange. It goes up and up until the sky itself seems to gather, thick and heavy, trying to push it back. But it keeps going!"

"It might be a city," another chimed in, "but it isn't like Bulika at all."

I ascended the nearest trunk, pushing through the dense foliage until I broke the surface. My breath caught in my throat. There, piercing the azure haze, was a city that defied gravity. It did not sit upon the mountain; it climbed it. Stone and spire merged with cloud and precipice in a dizzying tapestry of light and shadow. Palaces seemed to grow out of the rocks, and the boundary between earth and sky dissolved into a chaotic, beautiful blur.

Descending, I was joined by the Little Ones, who raced ahead of me, their laughter echoing through the trees. They moved with a joyous urgency, eyes fixed only on the path forward. As we hurried, the river transformed before our eyes. The muddy, sluggish stream of our world gave way to something ethereal—water so clear and vibrant it felt less like liquid and more like a living jewel. Nothing in this realm was merely *like* it; this was perfection itself.

From the plain, the city loomed larger, a beacon amidst the blue mists. But above it, a towering spire—or perhaps a natural rock formation crowned by magic—pierced the heavens. Around it, clouds swirled in violent, confused dances, twisting from gray to dark purple, tossing up vaporous foam that churned like whirlpools. Suddenly, a blinding flash erupted. For a heartbeat, the light danced around the children, then collapsed into a darkness so profound it felt like a void. Yet, through the gloom, their voices rose, hushed with awe.

"Did you see?"
"I saw!"
"What did you see?"
"The most beautiful man!"
"I heard him speak!"
"I didn't hear anything. What did he say?"

The smallest voice, belonging to Luva, cut through the silence. "He said, 'Ours are all mine, little ones: come along!'"

I had witnessed the lightning, but the words had eluded my ears. Lona, however, saw and heard with the children. Then came a second flash, and suddenly my vision expanded. The great quivering light coalesced into angelic faces, glowing and vanishing in rapid succession. A third flash followed, this time radiating pure humanity.

"My mother!" I cried out, tears stinging my eyes.
"Lots of mothers!" Luva shouted back.

Another flash, and the spectrum of creation exploded before us. Horses and elephants, lions and dogs—beasts of legend and reality alike. Birds of impossible splendor: great wings shimmering with every hue of the sunset, tiny feathers sparkling like scattered diamonds, silvery cranes, red flamingos, opal pigeons, peacocks blazing in gold and emerald, jeweled hummingbirds, and great-winged butterflies. Even the creeping things of the earth seemed to dance in the light.

"I see that serpents grow birds here, just as caterpillars used to grow butterflies!" Lona marveled.
"I saw my white pony," I whispered, a strange comfort settling in my chest. "The one that died when I was a child. I shouldn't have been so sad; I just needed to wait."

There was no thunder, no rolling crash of storms. Instead, a gentle, sweet rain began to fall, washing the air with a caressing coolness. We breathed deeply, our steps strengthening with every drop. The raindrops caught the light, flashing with the colors of awakened gems, while a magnificent rainbow arched over the city, bridging the gap between our world and the divine.

The clouds thickened, and the rain poured down in torrents, yet the children only exulted, running wild and free. The river, too, changed its nature. With a silent, radiant surge, it swept onward, filling its channel not with rough stone or sand, but with lush grass carpeted in a starry multitude of flowers—primroses, daisies, crocuses, narcissi, pimpernels, and anemones. The water remained crystal clear, untouched by the deluge, shining with a pale, berylline glow from its deep, dainty bed.

As we drew nearer to the mountain, we realized the river's source: it gushed from the very peak, rushing in full volume through the main street of the city. It cascaded down a grand staircase of deep, wide steps carved from porphyry and serpentine, leading to the foot of the mountain. Here, shallower steps branched off both banks, winding up toward the gate and the ascending street. Without hesitation, the Little Ones sprinted straight up the stairs to the gate, which stood wide open.

On the landing outside sat the portress, a woman-angel of dark visage, leaning her shadowed brow on her idle hand. The children rushed her, burying her in affectionate embraces. Before she could comprehend the intrusion, they had seized heaven by surprise and were already climbing the stairs beside the descending torrent. A great angel, attended by a host of shining beings, came down to greet them, but the children merrily evaded his grasp, running higher. Soon, a group of woman-angels descended in a joyful dance, and in a moment, the children were caught in heavenly arms, carried away by the radiants until they vanished from sight.

"Ah!" boomed the mighty angel as he continued his descent toward us, who were now close enough to hear his words. "This is well! These are soldiers ready to take heaven itself by storm! I hear of a horde of black bats on the frontiers; these will make short work of them!"

Seeing the horses and elephants struggling up behind us, he added, "Take those animals to the royal stables. Tend them well, then turn them into the king's forest."

Turning to us, he bowed low with a smile sweeter than any earthly grace. "Welcome home!"

Immediately, he turned and led the way upward. The scales of his armor flashed like fragments of lightning. No thought could fully articulate what I felt in that moment, standing before the officers of heaven. All I desired, all I did not yet know, was surely on its way to me.

We paused at the gate, where the radiant river roared forth. I could not name the stones that formed the archway, but among them I recognized the prototypes of every gem I had ever loved on earth. They were far more beautiful than their earthly counterparts; these were living stones. In them, I saw not just the intent, but the creator; not just the idea, but the embodied presence, the active sender. Nothing in this kingdom was dead, nothing was merely an object. Everything was alive with purpose.

We ascended through the city and emerged onto the upper side. There was no wall here, only a massive pile of broken rocks sloping upward like the moraine of an eternal glacier. Through the gaps between the stones, the river billowed out, white and powerful. Upon the peaks of these rocks, I could dimly discern three or four great steps of a stair, disappearing into a cloud white as snow. Above the steps, using only my mind's eye, I perceived a grand, ancient chair—the throne of the Ancient of Days. From over, under, and between those steps, the river of the water of life flowed endlessly, birthing new life without cease.

The great angel could guide us no further; those final steps we must climb alone.

My heart hammered with hope and longing as I tightened my grip on Lona's hand. We began the ascent, but soon we released each other, using our hands as well as our feet to navigate the treacherous, toilsome climb of the huge stones. Finally, we drew near the cloud that hung down the steps like the hem of a garment. We passed through the fringe and entered the deep folds. A warm, strong hand seized mine and pulled me to a small door with a golden lock. The door swung open; the hand released me and gently pushed me through. I turned quickly, and saw the board of a large book closing behind me. I stood alone in my library.

The search for Lona remains fruitless, yet Mara's presence anchors me. She has imparted wisdom, and her lessons deepen with every passing hour. A shadow of doubt lingers: was that final awakening merely another layer of the dream? Am I still trapped within the chamber of death, suspended in slumber because I am not yet ready to rise? Perhaps I did not surrender fully to sleep, waking prematurely before the true dawn. If that awakening was itself a fabrication, then it must be a prelude to a far superior reality, a false vision guarding a lovelier truth beneath its surface.

In these moments of uncertainty, I cry out, "Could God Himself create such exquisite things as I dreamed?"
"Whence then came thy dream?" asks Hope.
"From the depths of my darkness, rising into the light of my consciousness."
"But whence first into thy dark self?" Hope presses.
"My brain was its mother, and the fever coursing through my blood its father."
"Speak rather," Hope suggests gently, "that your brain was the violin from which the music issued, and the fever the bow that drew the strings. But who crafted the violin? Who guided the bow across those strings? Consider again: who placed the songbirds upon the branches of the Tree of Life, startling them into song in their proper order? Where did the fantasia originate? And where did the life that danced to it come from? Did you, in the abyss of your own unconscious, speak the command, 'Let beauty be; let truth seem!' and instantly beauty existed, while truth remained only a semblance?"

Man dreams and desires; God broods, wills, and brings life into being.
When a man dreams his own dream, he is merely the plaything of his imagination; but when Another grants him the dream, that Other holds the power to fulfill it.

I have never again sought the reflection in the mirror. The hand that sent me back has barred the door; I will not step through that threshold again. "All the days of my appointed time," I resolve, "I shall wait until my change comes."

Occasionally, glancing at my books, they seem to tremble as if a wind ripples through their solid forms, hinting that another world is poised to burst forth. Sometimes, when I walk among the living, the heavens and the earth, the trees and the grass, appear to shake as though about to dissolve; yet lo, they settle once more into their familiar faces. At times, I hear whispers surrounding me, as if beloved ones speak of me, but when I strive to catch the words, they vanish, leaving profound silence. I cannot discern if these sensations arise within my mind or enter from without. I do not chase them; they arrive, and I let them pass.

Strange, dim memories, refusing to be pinned down, gaze upon me through misty windows of the past even in broad daylight. Yet, I no longer dream. It may be that when I am most awake, I am merely dreaming more deeply! But when I finally awaken into that life which, like a mother carrying her child, holds this existence within its bosom, I shall know with absolute certainty that I have woken, and doubt shall cease forever.

I wait. Whether asleep or awake, I wait.
As Novalis wrote, "Our life is no dream, but it should and will perhaps become one."
